# Roadmap: the Mordell conjecture after Lawrence and Venkatesh

**Summary.** This roadmap proves Faltings's theorem — a smooth projective geometrically connected curve
of genus at least two over a number field has only finitely many rational points — by the method of
Lawrence and Venkatesh, and by the same method the finiteness of the S-unit equation. A rational point
`y` of the base of a polarized family of abelian varieties gives a global `p`-adic Galois
representation `ρ_y`. On one `p`-adic residue disk the crystalline comparison identifies the restriction
of `ρ_y` to a decomposition group with a filtered `φ`-module whose underlying `φ`-module is fixed by
Gauss–Manin transport and whose Hodge filtration is the value at `y` of a `p`-adic period map. Faltings's
finiteness lemma allows only finitely many semisimple global representations, so the points with
semisimple representation are sent into finitely many orbits of a Frobenius centralizer; once a local
field factor has degree at least eight these orbits are small compared with the period variety. Big
monodromy of the family over `ℂ`, transported to the `p`-adic side through one common tuple of power
series, makes the image of the period map Zariski dense, and Strassmann's theorem converts density into
finiteness on a curve. Two further arguments make this apply to every rational point of a curve of genus
`g ≥ 2`: the Kodaira–Parshin family of reduced Pryms of `Aff(q)`-covers branched at one point has big
monodromy, proved with mapping class groups by lifting point-pushing maps and Dehn twists to the covers;
and at a friendly place a count of short Frobenius orbits on `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q−1))` guarantees a large local
factor above every rational point, while purity of Hodge weights controls the reducible fibres. The end
theorems are Faltings's theorem (11.6), the S-unit theorem (6.10), the finiteness of rational points on
the base of an abelian-by-finite family (7.9), the finiteness of good-reduction fibres with semisimple
representation in a residue disk (4.5), Zariski density of the point-pushing monodromy (10.9) and full
monodromy of the Kodaira–Parshin family (10.10).

## Scope and ownership

This roadmap owns:

1. **Linear algebra of the method** (Layer LV.0): centralizers of semilinear automorphisms and their
   dimension, Frobenius centralizers over unramified extensions, the affine group `Aff(q)` of a prime
   field as a permutation group (cycle types, centralizer and normalizer in `Sym(𝔽_q)`, the
   commutator-product map), counting generating tuples of `ℤ/N`, isotropic subgroups of a perfect
   pairing, symplectic transvections, Zariski closures of groups generated by transvections, and the
   Goursat theorems for products of simple Lie algebras and of symplectic groups.
2. **Galois-representation inputs** (Layer LV.1): Faltings's finiteness lemma for pure representations
   with integral Frobenius polynomials, the largest CM-or-totally-real subfield, friendly places,
   purity of Hodge–Tate exponents of de Rham characters, the weight of a filtration, and the Hodge-weight
   identities for pure and induced representations at a friendly place.
3. **Abelian-by-finite families** (Layer LV.2): the families, their good models over rings of
   `S`-integers, the de Rham bundle over the finite étale algebra, residue disks, formal horizontal
   sections with their `p`-adic and complex convergence, `p`-adic and complex Gauss–Manin transport,
   and crystalline Frobenius on the fibres.
4. **Period maps** (Layer LV.3): Lagrangian Grassmannians and the Lagrangian period variety of a
   symplectic module over a finite étale algebra, the algebraic monodromy group and full monodromy, the
   `p`-adic and complex period maps, their common power series, Zariski density of the period image,
   and finiteness of preimages on a curve.
5. **The finiteness criterion** (Layer LV.4) and the two applications: the S-unit theorem through the
   cyclic Legendre family (Layer LV.6) and finiteness on the base of an abelian-by-finite family
   (Layer LV.7).
6. **Surface topology for the monodromy theorem** (Layer LV.5): surfaces with boundary and punctures,
   their classification, homology and intersection forms, simple closed curves, mapping class groups,
   Dehn twists and their action on homology, point-pushing and the Birman exact sequence, capping,
   surjectivity of the symplectic representation, configuration fibrations, and lifts of twists and
   point-pushes to finite covers.
7. **The Kodaira–Parshin family** (Layers LV.8–LV.10): surjections onto a centre-free group that are
   nontrivial on a peripheral class, Hurwitz spaces of covers branched at one point and their descent,
   the idempotents of `ℚ[Aff(q)]`, the reduced relative Prym, `Aff(q)`-covers of surfaces with their
   primitive homology, lifted monodromy, liftable curves and normal form, the monodromy theorem, and
   full monodromy of the Kodaira–Parshin family.
8. **Faltings's theorem** (Layer LV.11): the auxiliary prime `q` and friendly place `v`, the Weil pairing
   on `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q−1))`, the short-orbit count, the size bound and the conclusion.

Two end statements are shared with other roadmaps, which own their declarations. The S-unit theorem
(6.10) is the number-field specialization of DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence §2.4,
`DiophantineApproximation.finite_two_term_unit_equation` (finitely many solutions of `ax + by = 1` in a finitely generated subgroup of `K^×`), and Faltings's
theorem (11.6) is the declaration `mordell_finite` of HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions, §§5.12, 6.4.
This roadmap gives the Lawrence–Venkatesh proofs of these statements; they use neither the subspace
theorem nor Baker's method, nor the Parshin–Shafarevich or Vojta–Bombieri routes, and conversely.

It uses, and does not redevelop:

- period rings, de Rham and crystalline representations, filtered `φ`-modules, weak admissibility and the
  crystalline comparison for abelian schemes with good reduction (PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.3,
  CrystallineCohomology); abelian schemes, polarizations, dual abelian schemes, the de Rham bundle with
  its Gauss–Manin connection and the de Rham–Betti comparison (AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli);
- Hermite–Minkowski (ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1); this supplies bounded-degree
  extension finiteness to the Frobenius test-set argument. FaltingsFinitenessAndIsogenyTheorems
  §R28.5.1 supplies Weil-polynomial finiteness and §R28.5.2 the semisimple Frobenius test set; the semisimple trace criterion
  is ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §1.8;
- Strassmann's theorem over a complete nonarchimedean field (RestrictedPowerSeries §2.4);
- the universal Legendre curve over `ℤ[1/2][λ, 1/(λ(λ−1))]` (TauCetiRoadmap ModularCurves, Layer 4C);
- the one-dimensional affine group `TauCeti.AffineGroup F = F ⋊ F^×` with its translation and linear
  subgroups, its order and its Frobenius-group structure (Tau Ceti at `a91d3aa`,
  `TauCeti/GroupTheory/SpecificGroups/Affine/Basic.lean`); this roadmap adds the permutation-group statements
  about `Aff(q)` that the covers need;
- Chebotarev density, global and local Artin reciprocity and the unramified local theory
  (TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev, ClassFieldTheory, LocalFieldsRamification); Galois representations (ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §§6.1–6.3),
  Frobenius polynomials at good reduction (WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.2) and
  Néron–Ogg–Shafarevich (NeronModelsAndSemistableAbelianVarieties §5.1); Weil numbers and purity
  (DeligneWeightsAndPurity, WeightsInEtaleCohomology);
- algebraic groups, their Lie algebras, closed subgroups and identity components (TauCetiRoadmap
  ReductiveGroups); Grassmannians (Mathlib `Module.Grassmannian` and AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry);
  scheme foundations, spreading out and étale cohomology (SchemeAndStackFoundations); analytification
  and GAGA (ComplexComparisonPartII); étale fundamental groups and Riemann existence
  (InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups); relative Picard schemes and Jacobians (TauCetiRoadmap
  JacobianChallenge and AlgebraicCurves); étale duality on curves (EtaleDualityAndPerverseSheaves);
- singular homology, van Kampen, cellular homology, transfer, Poincaré–Lefschetz duality and relative
  homotopy (TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology); covering spaces, the universal cover and the lifting
  criterion (TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers); manifolds with boundary, collars, gluing and
  isotopy extension (TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology, Layer 1); the pinned library’s
  `TauCeti.GLSymplecticFin.eq_top_of_root_subgroups` in
  `LinearAlgebra/Matrix/GeneralLinearGroup/Symplectic/TorusGeneration` supplies symplectic-group
  generation over every field. A symplectic basis transports it to `Sp(V,ω)`, and the formulas of 0.14
  identify the long roots with rank-one transvections and the short roots with products of them.
  The geometric connectedness of Sp is
  `TauCeti.Symplectic.geometricallyConnectedCommHopfAlgProperty_coordinateHopfAlgebra`.

It leaves to other roadmaps:

- the sections of Lawrence–Venkatesh after §8 (hypersurfaces, the Ax–Schanuel input, unlikely
  intersections); this roadmap covers the one-dimensional S-unit and Mordell arguments of §§2–8;
- any effective bound on the number or the height of the rational points: the method is ineffective and
  nothing here computes `Y(K)`.

## Conventions

- **Number fields and places.** A number field `K` has a fixed embedding `ι : K → ℂ` whenever complex
  comparison is used. `S` is a finite set of places containing the archimedean ones, and `𝒪 = 𝒪_S` is
  the ring of `S`-integers. Finite places are identified with nonzero primes of `𝒪_K`; `q_v` is the
  residue cardinality and `K_v` the completion.
- **Frobenius and weights.** Arithmetic Frobenius acts on a finite residue field by `x ↦ x^{q_v}`.
  Weights of global representations are measured with geometric Frobenius: `ℚ_p(n)` has weight `−2n`,
  and on the finite-coefficient `H¹` pairing the arithmetic Frobenius multiplier is `q_v^{−1}`.
- **Galois representations.** Continuous and finite-dimensional over `ℚ_p` when `p`-adic arithmetic is in
  view; abstract representation lemmas state their characteristic-zero field. For good reduction outside
  `S`, the ramification set of `p`-adic cohomology is `T = S ∪ {places above p}`; crystalline at `p` does
  not mean unramified at `p`.
- **Filtrations.** Decreasing, integer-indexed, exhaustive and separated with finitely many jumps.
  `t_H(D) = Σ_j j·dim gr^j D`, and the weight `t_H(D)/dim D` is taken only for `D ≠ 0`. The jump of
  `D_dR(ℚ_p(n))` is `−n`. `D_cris(V) = (V ⊗ B_cris)^{G_K}` is covariant, and local Artin is normalized by
  `χ_cyc(Art_F(u)) = N_{F/ℚ_p}(u)^{−1}` on units.
- **Finite étale algebras.** For a finite étale `E/k`, a rank-`d` submodule means rank `d` on every field
  factor of `E`; total `k`-dimension does not replace this. The total fibre of an abelian-by-finite family
  is the disjoint union of its abelian varieties, not their product or Weil restriction.
- **Symplectic forms.** Alternating and nondegenerate; Lagrangian linear algebra holds in every
  characteristic unless characteristic zero is stated. `T_v^r(x) = x + r·ω(v, x)·v` and `T_v = T_v^1`.
  On oriented surface homology the intersection pairing `î(x, b)` is used in the order written: a
  positive Dehn twist acts by `x ↦ x + î(x, b)·b`, so the action `x ↦ x + q·î(x, ẽ)·ẽ` of a lifted twist
  is `T_{ẽ}^{−q}` when `ω = î`.
- **The affine group.** `Aff(q)` acts on the prime field `𝔽_q` by `x ↦ ax + b` with `a ≠ 0`; multiplication
  is composition; `λ : Aff(q) → 𝔽_q^×` is the linear part. Centre-freeness and self-normalization are
  stated for primes `q ≥ 3`.
- **Paths and monodromy.** Mathlib's fundamental-group multiplication is `p * q = q.trans p`: traverse `q`,
  then `p`. Forward transport satisfies `T_{p*q} = T_p T_q`; backward transport is the homomorphism
  `μ : π₁^op → GL`, `μ(op δ) = T_δ^{−1}`, and the left deck action by prepending `δ` uses the same opposite
  group. Forward and backward transport have the same image, so they define the same algebraic monodromy
  group.
- **Surjections.** For a surjection `φ : Γ → G`, `γ·φ = φ ∘ Ad(γ)^{−1}` and `φ·h = Ad(h^{−1}) ∘ φ`; the
  stabilizer of `φ` in `Γ × G^op` is `{(γ, h) : h^{−1} = φ(γ)}`.

Three settings recur.

**Setting P (period maps).** `K` is a number field with an embedding `ι : K → ℂ`; `𝒳 → 𝒴' → 𝒴` is a
good model over `𝒪 = 𝒪_S` (2.2) of an abelian-by-finite family `X → Y' → Y` of relative dimension `d`, with
`π : Y' → Y` finite étale of degree `n` and `Y` smooth and geometrically connected of dimension `m` over
`K`; `v ∉ S` is a finite place with `K_v/ℚ_p` unramified and `p > 2`; `y₀ ∈ 𝒴(𝒪)`; `E₀ := E_{y₀}` is the
fibre algebra; `V := ℋ_{y₀} = H¹_dR(X_{y₀}/K)` is a free `E₀`-module of rank `2d` with the polarization
pairing `ω₀`; `H := LGr_{E₀}(V, ω₀)` is the period variety (3.8) and `h₀ := F¹V ∈ H(K)`; `V_v, H_v` and
`V_ℂ, H_ℂ` are the base changes along `K → K_v` and `ι`; `Ω_v := Ω_v(y₀)` is the residue disk (2.5) with
coordinates `z : Ω_v ≅ (p𝒪_v)^m`.

**Setting Q (rational points on a curve).** In setting P, `Y` is a smooth projective geometrically connected
curve (`m = 1`), `d ≥ 1`, the family has full monodromy relative to `ι` (3.11), the good model has `𝒴` proper,
so that `Y(K) = 𝒴(𝒪)`, and `v` is a friendly place (1.5) whose residue characteristic `p` is odd and lies
below no place of `S`; setting P then applies at every `y₀ ∈ Y(K)`. For a closed point `y'` above `y` and a
place `w` above `v`, `ρ_{y'} = H¹_et(X_{y', K̄}, ℚ_p)` and `ρ_{y', w}` is its restriction to the decomposition
group at `w`; the local factors `K(y')_w` are unramified over `K_v`.

**Setting C (covers of a surface).** `Y` is a closed oriented surface of genus `g ≥ 2`, `y ∈ Y`, and `q ≥ 3` is
a prime. A singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover of `(Y, y)` is a connected degree-`q` covering `Z° → Y ∖ {y}` with
monodromy group `Aff(q)` acting on a fibre as on `𝔽_q` and nontrivial monodromy around `y`, together with
its compactification `π : Z → Y` (9.1). `Z_1, …, Z_N` represent the isomorphism classes;
`Mod(Y ∖ {y})'` is the common stabilizer of these classes, `π₁(Y, y)'` its inverse image under
point-pushing, and `Mon` the product monodromy map of 9.4.

**Names.** Lean names are relative to the namespace `TauCetiRoadmap.MordellLawrenceVenkatesh` of
[Suggested.lean](Suggested.lean), which states the declarations that can be typed against the pinned
libraries. Section numbers `k.n` refer to this document; `LV20` is the paper of Lawrence and Venkatesh
(see References), and its section, lemma and page numbers are those of arXiv version 3.

### From TauCetiRoadmap roadmaps

- **ReductiveGroups** Layers 2, 3, 6 and 7: Lie algebras of smooth affine groups and the adjoint representation; closed
  subgroup schemes, quotients and identity components; centres and semisimple groups; split structure theory. What
  this roadmap needs beyond them about Zariski closures, unit groups, orbit maps and the symplectic group is stated
  here as 0.15–0.17.
- **LocalFieldsRamification** Layer 2: for `ℚ_p ⊆ K ⊆ L` finite unramified, `L/ℚ_p` is cyclic generated by arithmetic
  Frobenius, `σ^{[K:ℚ_p]}` generates `Gal(L/K)` with fixed field `K`; composita of unramified extensions are unramified;
  the residue correspondence.
- **NumberFieldArithmetic** Layers 1.5, 2.3 and 5 (5.3 `semilocalEquiv`, 5.6 decomposition groups): unramified
  primes in composita, Frobenius orders and residue degrees, `K_v ⊗_K L ≅ ∏_{w|v} L_w`.
- **Chebotarev** Layer 10: every conjugacy class of a finite Galois group is the Frobenius class of infinitely many
  unramified primes, so of one outside any finite set.
- **ClassFieldTheory** Layer 7 (absolute local Artin map, `cyclotomicCharacter_artinMap`:
  `χ_cyc(Art_F(u)) = N_{F/ℚ_p}(u)^{−1}` on units) and Layer 11 (global Artin reciprocity for finite abelian extensions,
  compatible with the local maps and trivial on principal ideles). The passage to continuous characters of `G_K` is
  1.8.
- **ModularCurves** Layer 4C: the universal Legendre curve `y² = x(x − 1)(x − λ)` over `ℤ[1/2][λ, 1/(λ(λ − 1))]`.
- **JacobianChallenge** Layers B (genus `dim H¹(X, 𝒪)`, Serre duality) and D (relative Picard functor, `Pic⁰`,
  properness); **AlgebraicCurves** Layer 12 (12E: the cohomological genus equals the function-field genus).
- **AlgebraicTopology** Stages 1 (van Kampen), 2 (singular homology and local coefficients), 3 (Mayer–Vietoris with
  the pinned sign), 4 (cellular homology), 5 (covers, transfer `p_*p^* = deg` and `p^*p_* = Σ g_*` for unbranched regular
  covers, Serre fibrations and the bundle-to-fibration bridge), 6 (Poincaré–Lefschetz duality for smooth compact
  manifolds) and 8 (relative homotopy, Hurewicz, Whitehead). The topological surface statements these do not contain
  — Schönflies, triangulation, duality for topological surfaces, the fibration homotopy sequence, asphericity — are
  5.2–5.24.
- **GeometricTopology** Layer 1: manifolds with boundary, collars, gluing along boundary pieces.
- **Completed/UniversalCovers** Stages 0–2: the universal cover as path classes, deck transformations
  (`Deck ≃ π₁^op`), the lifting criterion and the Galois correspondence of covers.

  `VanishingCycles.smoothBaseChange`) give torsion proper base change and invertible-torsion smooth
  base change. Section F.4 gives adic realization. They identify proper geometric `H¹_et` after
  extension of algebraically closed fields and make `H¹_et(Y_K̄, ℤ/N)` unramified at a good-reduction
  place where `N` is invertible, as used in §§4.1 and 11.3.

  root absolute value to `q^(w/2)`. Monicity bounds each coefficient by the corresponding elementary
  symmetric-polynomial bound; Mathlib `Polynomial.finite_mahlerMeasure_le` gives the general

**Checks of the Weil-polynomial supplier.**

- For `d = 1, q = 4` and `w = 1` the possibilities are `X−2` and `X+2`.
- For `d = 0` the unique polynomial is 1, with no roots to check.
- For `d = 2, q = 3` and `w = 1, X²−3` is allowed: purity alone does not prescribe the sign of the constant coefficient.
- Integrality is essential: the polynomials `X²−2aX+1, −1 < a < 1` rational, already give infinitely many monic rational polynomials with roots of absolute value 1.

## How to read the build

Layers LV.0 and LV.1 are independent toolkits: linear algebra and finite group theory, and the Galois-theoretic
constraints on Hodge weights together with the finiteness of global representations. Layers LV.2 and LV.3 build the analytic heart of the method — families,
Gauss–Manin transport, period varieties and the two period maps, and the density of the `p`-adic period image — and
Layer LV.4 combines them with crystalline comparison into the finiteness criterion on a residue disk. The S-unit theorem
(Layer LV.6) is the first application and only needs Layers LV.0–LV.4 and the elementary part of LV.5. The general
finiteness theorem for abelian-by-finite families (Layer LV.7) adds the short-orbit measure, purity at friendly places
and general position. The topological part — surfaces and mapping class groups (LV.5), `Aff(q)`-covers (LV.9) and the
monodromy theorem (LV.10) — is independent of the arithmetic until Layer LV.8 builds the Kodaira–Parshin family and
LV.10 identifies its monodromy. Layer LV.11 chooses the auxiliary prime and place and concludes. A reader interested
only in the S-unit theorem can stop after Layer LV.6.

## Layer LV.0: Semilinear centralizers, the affine group and symplectic generation

This layer collects the linear algebra and finite group theory that the arithmetic and topological
arguments call. The first part concerns a bijective semilinear map `φ` over a field automorphism `σ`:
after splitting the coefficient field, `φ` permutes equal-dimensional eigenspaces cyclically, so its
centralizer is a form of the centralizer of the linear map `φ^e` and has dimension at most `(dim V)²`
over the fixed field (0.4). This is what makes Frobenius-centralizer orbits small: enlarging the
local field increases the dimension of the period variety without increasing this bound. The second
part treats `Aff(q)` as a permutation group of `𝔽_q`: cycle types, the trivial centralizer and
self-normalization in `Sym(𝔽_q)`, the commutator-product map that counts surjections from a surface
group, the count of generating tuples of `ℤ/N`, and the bound for isotropic subgroups of a perfect
pairing that limits short Frobenius orbits. The third part is the symplectic generation machinery:
powers of a transvection are Zariski dense in its root group, pairs with nonzero pairing generate
`Sp` of their plane, a connected intersection graph spanning `V` generates `Sp(V)`, and the Goursat theorems reduce
density in a product of symplectic groups to excluding graphs between two factors.

### 0.1 Splitting of a finite separable extension after base change to a splitting field

**Lemma.** Let `E/F` be a finite separable field extension and `Ω` a field containing `F` over which the minimal polynomial of a primitive element of `E` splits (for instance an algebraically closed field containing `F`). Let `Σ = Hom_F(E, Ω)`. The `Ω`-algebra map `E ⊗_F Ω → Ω^Σ, e ⊗ x ↦ (τ(e)x)_{τ∈Σ}`, is an isomorphism.

*Proof sketch.* Choose a primitive element `α` with `E = F(α)` (primitive element theorem) and let `f` be its minimal polynomial, which is separable. `E ⊗_F Ω ≅ Ω[X]/(f)`; since `f` splits over `Ω` into the distinct linear factors `X − τ(α), τ ∈ Σ`, the ideals `(X − τ(α))` are pairwise coprime and the Chinese remainder theorem gives `Ω[X]/(f) ≅ ∏_τ Ω[X]/(X − τ(α)) ≅ Ω^Σ`. Trace the composite on `e ⊗ x` to identify it with `e ⊗ x ↦ (τ(e)x)_τ`. (LV20, §2.1, proof of Lemma 2.1, p. 9.) *Needs:* Mathlib `Field.exists_primitive_element`; Mathlib `Ideal.quotientInfRingEquivPiQuotient`.

**Checks.**

- For `E = ℚ(i), F = ℚ, Ω = ℂ: ℚ(i) ⊗ ℂ ≅ ℂ × ℂ` via `i ↦ (i, −i)`.
- The splitting is compatible with extension of the splitting field `Ω`; the corresponding module decomposition and its tensor functoriality are stated in 0.2.

### 0.2 Idempotent decomposition of a module over a split étale algebra

**Lemma.** For a finite set `I`, a commutative field `Ω` and any module `M` over `Ω^I`, multiplication by the coordinate idempotents gives a natural `Ω`-linear equivalence `M ≃ ⊕ᵢ eᵢM`. Applied to `E ⊗_F Ω ≃ Ω^Hom_F(E,Ω), eτM` is exactly the simultaneous eigenspace `(e ⊗ 1)m = τ(e)m`. No finite-dimensionality of `M` is required.

*Proof sketch.* The orthogonal coordinate idempotents sum to 1; `m` maps to `(eᵢm)ᵢ` and the inverse sums the components. Orthogonality proves both composites are identities; intertwining the `E`-action identifies the eigenspaces. (LV20, §2.1, proof of Lemma 2.1, p. 9.) *Needs:* 0.1.

**Checks.**

- For `ℚ(i) ⊗ ℂ`, the two idempotents split a module into the `i` and `−i` eigenspaces.
- For `I` empty, the unital module is zero; for `I` singleton the decomposition is the identity.

### 0.3 The centralizer of a semilinear automorphism

**Definition.** Let `σ` be a field automorphism of `E` and `φ : V → V` a `σ`-semilinear bijection of a finite-dimensional `E`-vector space `(φ(ev) = σ(e)φ(v))`. The centralizer `Z(φ) := {f ∈ End_E(V) : f ∘ φ = φ ∘ f}`. It is a subalgebra of `End_E(V)` over the fixed field `F = E^σ` (not an `E`-subspace in general), and its unit group `Z(φ)^× = Z(φ) ∩ GL_E(V)` is the group of `E`-linear automorphisms commuting with `φ`.

- `semilinearCentralizer`: `Z(φ)` as an `F`-subalgebra of `End_E(V), F = fixedField σ`.
- `mem_semilinearCentralizer`: `f ∈ Z(φ) ↔ f ∘ φ = φ ∘ f`.
- `semilinearCentralizer_le_pow`: `Z(φ) ⊆ Z(φ^n)` for every `n ≥ 1` (`φ^n` is `σ^n`-semilinear).
- `units_semilinearCentralizer`: `Z(φ)^× = {g ∈ GL_E(V) : gφ = φg}`; it acts on subspaces of `V` and on filtrations preserving the `φ`-stable structure.
- `semilinearCentralizer_linear`: When `σ = id, Z(φ)` is the usual centralizer of the linear map `φ` (Mathlib `Subalgebra.centralizer`).
- `semilinearCentralizer_conj`: For an `E`-linear iso `u : V ≅ W, u Z(φ) u⁻¹ = Z(uφu⁻¹)`.
- `semilinearCentralizer_scalar`: If `φ = σ ⊗ 1` on `E ⊗_F V₀` then `Z(φ) = End_F(V₀) ⊗ 1`.
- `semilinearCentralizer_fixedScalar`: For a base field `F` fixed pointwise by `σ`, scalar multiplication by every `a∈F` commutes with `φ`.

*Construction.* Define `Z(φ)` as the kernel of the `F`-linear map `End_E(V) → End(V), f ↦ fφ − φf`; check that `fφ − φf` is additive and that for `c ∈ F, (cf)φ = c(fφ)` since `σ(c) = c`. Closure under composition and identity is immediate, so `Z(φ)` is an `F`-subalgebra. (LV20, §2.1, Lemma 2.1, p. 9.)

**Checks.**

- For `V=E` and `φ=σ`, multiplication by `a` belongs to `Z(φ)` iff `σ(a)=a`.
- For `σ=id` and `φ=id` on `E², Z(φ)=M₂(E)`.
- For `E=ℂ` over `ℝ` and `φ=complex conjugation` on `E`, multiplication by `i` is not in `Z(φ)`, while multiplication by 1 is. The same obstruction occurs in `ℚ(i)`.
- `Z(id_V) = End_E(V)` when `σ = id`.
- For `E = ℚ_{p²}, φ = σ ⊕ σ` on `E², Z(φ) = M_2(ℚ_p)`.

### 0.4 Dimension of the centralizer of a semilinear automorphism (LV20 Lemma 2.1)

**Theorem.** Let `σ` be an automorphism of the field `E` of finite order `e` with fixed field `F, V` an `E`-vector space of dimension `d` and `φ` a `σ`-semilinear bijection of `V`. Then `dim_F Z(φ) = dim_E Z(φ^e)`, where `φ^e` is `E`-linear; in particular `dim_F Z(φ) ≤ d²` (`finrank_semilinearCentralizer_le`).

*Proof sketch.* By Artin's theorem `E/F` is Galois with group `⟨σ⟩` of order `e`. Let `F̄` be an algebraic closure of `F` and `Σ = Hom_F(E, F̄)`; by 0.2, `V̄ = V ⊗_F F̄ = ⊕_{τ∈Σ} V̄^τ`. `φ̄ = φ ⊗ 1` is `F̄`-linear and maps `V̄^τ` onto `V̄^{τσ^{-1}}`; the orbits of `σ` on `Σ` form one cycle `τ₀, τ₀σ^{-1}, …, τ₀σ^{-(e−1)}`. `Z(φ) ⊗_F F̄` is the centralizer of `φ̄` inside `End_{E⊗F̄}(V̄)` (kernels commute with the flat base change `F → F̄`); an element `(f_τ)` of it is determined by `f_{τ₀}`, which commutes with `φ̄^e|V̄^{τ₀}`, and every such `f_{τ₀}` extends uniquely by `f_{τ₀σ^{-i}} = φ̄^i f_{τ₀} φ̄^{-i}`. Hence `Z(φ) ⊗ F̄ ≅` centralizer of `φ̄^e` on `V̄^{τ₀}`. `(V̄^{τ₀}, φ̄^e)` is the base change of `(V, φ^e)` along `τ₀ : E → F̄`, so its centralizer has `F̄`-dimension `dim_E Z(φ^e)`. Compare dimensions using invariance of dimension under base change; the bound follows from `Z(φ^e) ⊆ End_E(V)`. (LV20, §2.1, Lemma 2.1 and proof, p. 9; §2.1, proof of Lemma 2.1, p. 9.) *Needs:* 0.1; 0.2; 0.3; Mathlib `IsGalois.of_fixed_field`; Mathlib `FixedPoints.finrank_eq_card`; Mathlib `Module.finrank_baseChange`; Mathlib `Module.Flat.ker_lTensor_eq`.

**Checks.**

- `E = ℚ_{p²}, F = ℚ_p, φ = σ ⊕ σ` on `E²`: both sides equal 4.
- If `φ^e` is a scalar then `dim_F Z(φ) = d²`.
- If `φ^e` has distinct eigenvalues in `E` then `dim_F Z(φ) ≤ d`.

### 0.5 The unit group of a finite-dimensional algebra as an algebraic group

**Construction.** Let `K` be a field and `A` a finite-dimensional associative `K`-algebra. The functor `R ↦ (A ⊗_K R)^×` on commutative `K`-algebras is represented by the principal open subscheme `GL₁(A) := {a : N(a) ≠ 0}` of the affine space `𝔸(A)`, where `N(a) = det(L_a)` is the determinant of left multiplication; multiplication is polynomial and inversion is regular, so `GL₁(A)` is an affine algebraic group over `K`. It is smooth and connected, of dimension `dim_K A`, its `K`-points are `A^×`, and its formation commutes with field extension and with products of algebras.

- `unitGroupScheme`: `GL₁(A)`, the principal open `D(det L)` of `𝔸(A)`, with its group law.
- `unitGroupScheme_points`: `GL₁(A)(R) = (A ⊗_K R)^×` naturally in `R`.
- `unitGroupScheme_smooth`: `GL₁(A)` is smooth, connected, of dimension `dim_K A`.
- `unitGroupScheme_baseChange`: `GL₁(A)_L = GL₁(A ⊗_K L)` for a field extension `L/K`.
- `unitGroupScheme_prod`: `GL₁(A × B) = GL₁(A) × GL₁(B)`.

*Construction.* An element `a` of `A ⊗ R` is a unit if and only if `L_a` is invertible, if and only if `N(a)` is a unit of `R` (Cramer’s rule gives the inverse as a polynomial in `a` divided by `N(a)`). So the functor is the basic open `D(N)` of the affine space, which is smooth and irreducible of dimension `dim A` (nonempty because `N(1) = 1`); the group law is the restriction of the algebra multiplication. Base change and products are immediate from the description. (LV20, §3.5, proof of Proposition 3.4, p. 20; §6, proof of Lemma 6.2, p. 31.) *Needs:* SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- For `A = M_n(K), GL₁(A) = GL_n` and `N(a) = det(a)^n`.
- For `A = K × K, GL₁(A) = 𝔾_m × 𝔾_m`, of dimension 2.
- For `A = K[ε]/(ε²), GL₁(A) ≅ 𝔾_m × 𝔾_a` via `a+bε ↦ (a,b/a)`, with `a ≠ 0`. It is connected of dimension 2 but not a torus; the unit group of this non-semisimple algebra is not reductive.

### 0.6 Frobenius centralizers over unramified extensions of a `p`-adic field

**Lemma.** Let `K_v/ℚ_p` be finite unramified of degree `f_v, L/K_v` finite unramified of degree `r`, and `σ_L` the arithmetic Frobenius of `L` over `ℚ_p`. Let `V` be an `L`-vector space of dimension `n` and `φ` a `σ_L`-semilinear bijection. Then `Z(φ) ⊆ Z(φ^{f_v})`; the latter is a `K_v`-subalgebra of `End_L(V)` with `dim_{K_v} Z(φ^{f_v}) = dim_L Z(φ^{f_v r}) ≤ n²`; its unit group is the group of `K_v`-points of the `K_v`-group scheme of units of this `K_v`-algebra, a smooth connected affine group of dimension `dim_{K_v} Z(φ^{f_v})`.

*Proof sketch.* `σ_L^{f_v}` generates `Gal(L/K_v)`, which is cyclic of order `r` with fixed field `K_v` (unramified extensions of local fields are Galois with Frobenius generator). `φ^{f_v}` is `σ_L^{f_v}`-semilinear; apply the dimension formula for semilinear centralizers with `e = r`. `Z(φ) ⊆ Z(φ^{f_v})` is the power relation of 0.3. The unit group of a finite-dimensional `K_v`-algebra `A` is represented by the open subscheme of the affine space of `A` where the norm is invertible; it is smooth and connected of dimension `dim A`. (LV20, §6, proof of Lemma 6.2, p. 31.) *Needs:* 0.4; 0.5; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0.

**Checks.**

- `K_v = ℚ_p, L = ℚ_{p^r}, V = L` with `φ = σ_L: Z(φ) = ℚ_p` and `Z(φ^{1}) = ℚ_p` has dimension `1 = dim_L Z(φ^r)`.
- The bound `dim ≤ 4d²` for `n = 2d` used in LV20 Lemma 6.2.

### 0.7 The faithful affine permutation action of a prime field

**Definition.** For a prime `q`, define `affinePermGroup q` as the subgroup of `Sym(𝔽_q)` consisting of permutations `x ↦ ax+b` with `a ≠ 0`. This target adds the permutation realization and its evaluation API to the semidirect-product carrier specified in the boundaries. The linear coefficient is recoverable from the action as `g(1)−g(0)`; write it `λ(g)`. The stabilizer of 0 consists exactly of the maps `x ↦ ax`. The action and these formulas also hold for `q = 2`; centre-freeness and the commutator conclusions below require `q ≥ 3`.

- `affinePermGroup`: The image of `Aff(q)` in `Equiv.Perm (ZMod q)`: the permutations `x ↦ ax + b` with a a unit.
- `affinePermGroup.mulEquiv`: `Aff(q) ≃* affinePermGroup q`, by faithfulness of the action.
- `affinePermGroup.mem_iff`: `σ ∈ affinePermGroup q ↔ ∃ a ≠ 0, ∃ b, ∀ x, σ x = a x + b`; a and `b` are then unique.
- `affineLinearPart`: `λ : affinePermGroup q →* (ZMod q)ˣ`, read off as `σ 1 − σ 0`; it is the projection to the linear factor of `Aff(q)`.
- `affineLinearPart_apply`: `λ(x ↦ ax + b) = a`.
- `affineLinearPart_surjective`: `λ` is surjective.
- `mem_ker_affineLinearPart`: `ker λ` is the translation subgroup, normal and isomorphic to `ZMod q`.
- `affinePermGroup.stabilizer_zero`: The stabilizer of 0 is the linear subgroup `H_q ≃* (ZMod q)ˣ`, of index `q`; `Aff(q)/H_q ≃ 𝔽_q` as `Aff(q)`-sets.
- `affinePermGroup.mul_apply`: `(x ↦ ax + b) ∘ (x ↦ a′x + b′) = (x ↦ aa′x + ab′ + b)`, and `(x ↦ ax + b)^{−1} = (x ↦ a^{−1}x − a^{−1}b)`.
- `affinePermGroup.commutator_eq`: The commutator of `(a, b)` and (a′, `b′`) is the translation by `b(1 − a′) − b′(1 − a)`; for `q ≥ 3` the derived subgroup is the translation subgroup.
- `existsUnique_affinePermGroup_pair`: For `x₁ ≠ x₂` and `y₁ ≠ y₂` in `𝔽_q` there is a unique `g ∈ Aff(q)` with `g(x₁) = y₁` and `g(x₂) = y₂` (sharp two-transitivity).
- `affinePermGroup_three`: `affinePermGroup 3 = ⊤: Aff(3) = Sym(𝔽₃)`.

*Construction.* Define `Aff(q)` as the image of the injective homomorphism `𝔽_q^+ ⋊ 𝔽_q^× → Sym(𝔽_q), (b, a) ↦ (x ↦ ax + b)`. `λ` is the projection to `𝔽_q^×`; its kernel is the image of `𝔽_q^+`. The stabilizer of 0 consists of the maps with `b = 0`. (LV20, §2.6, p. 14.) *Needs:* Mathlib `ZMod.card_units_eq_totient`; Mathlib `AffineEquiv`.

**Checks.**

- In `Aff(5), (2,1)(3,4)=(1,4)`, with `(a,b)` acting by `x↦ax+b`.
- The identity is `(1,0)` and `(a,b)⁻¹=(a⁻¹,−a⁻¹b)`.
- The action on `𝔽₃` identifies `Aff(3)` with `Sym(𝔽₃)`, of cardinality 6.
- `Aff(3) ≅ S_3 = Sym(𝔽_3)`.
- `|Aff(5)| = 20` and `Aff(5)` is the Frobenius group `F₂₀`.

### 0.8 Cycle types of elements of `Aff(q)`

**Lemma.** Let `q ≥ 3` be prime and `g = (x ↦ ax + b) ∈ Aff(q)`. If `a ≠ 1`, then `g` has exactly one fixed point and all its other cycles have length `ord(a)`, the order of a in `𝔽_q^×`. If `a = 1` and `b ≠ 0, g` is a `q`-cycle. If `g = 1` all cycles are fixed points. Here cycle type means the full partition, including fixed points. Mathlib’s `Equiv.Perm.cycleType` omits length-one cycles: the Lean signatures therefore encode `(1,4)` as `{4}, (1,2,2)` as `{2,2}`, and the identity as `∅`. In particular the cycle type of `g` in `Sym(𝔽_q)` determines whether `λ(g) = 1`, and when `λ(g) ≠ 1` it determines `ord(λ(g))` (`cycleType_of_affineLinearPart_ne_one`, `cycleType_of_affineLinearPart_eq_one`).

*Proof sketch.* If `a ≠ 1`, conjugating by the translation moving the unique fixed point `b/(1 − a)` to 0 turns `g` into `x ↦ ax`, whose nonzero orbits are cosets of `⟨a⟩` in `𝔽_q^×`. If `a = 1, b ≠ 0`, the orbit of any `x` is `x + 𝔽_q b = 𝔽_q` since `q` is prime. (LV20, §8.3, p. 42.) *Needs:* 0.7.

**Checks.**

- In `Aff(5), x ↦ 2x + 1` has cycle type `(1, 4)`; `x ↦ 4x` has cycle type `(1, 2, 2)`; `x ↦ x + 1` is a 5-cycle.

### 0.9 `Aff(q)` is centre-free, has trivial centralizer and is self-normalizing in `Sym(𝔽_q)`

**Lemma.** For a prime `q ≥ 3: (a)` the centralizer of `Aff(q)` in `Sym(𝔽_q)` is trivial; in particular `Aff(q)` has trivial centre; `(b)` the normalizer of `Aff(q)` in `Sym(𝔽_q)` is `Aff(q)`. Consequently, for a group `Γ`, two surjections `Γ → Aff(q)` are conjugate under `Sym(𝔽_q)` (as transitive actions on `𝔽_q` with image `Aff(q)`) if and only if they are conjugate under `Aff(q)`, and a surjection `Γ → Aff(q)` has trivial stabilizer under conjugation by `Aff(q)` (`centralizer_affinePermGroup`, `normalizer_affinePermGroup`).

*Proof sketch.* `(a)` A permutation `c` commuting with all translations satisfies `c(x + 1) = c(x) + 1`, so `c` is a translation `x ↦ x + t`; commuting with `x ↦ ax (a ≠ 1)` forces `at = t`, so `t = 0`. `(b)` If `s ∈ Sym(𝔽_q)` normalizes `Aff(q)`, it normalizes the unique Sylow `q`-subgroup `𝔽_q^+` (normal, of index `q − 1` prime to `q`); after composing with a translation `s` fixes 0 and normalizes `⟨x ↦ x+1⟩`, so `s(x + 1) = s(x) + c` for some `c ≠ 0`, whence `s(x) = cx ∈ Aff(q)`. The consequences follow: a `Sym`-conjugacy between two surjections is given by an element normalizing `Aff(q)`; the stabilizer of a surjection `φ` under conjugation is the centralizer of `φ(Γ) = Aff(q)`, which is trivial. (LV20, §8.2, footnote 5, p. 39; §8.2.1, p. 40.) *Needs:* 0.7.

**Checks.**

- `Aff(3) = S_3` is self-normalizing and centre-free.
- The translation subgroup of `Aff(q)` has nontrivial centralizer (itself), so surjectivity is needed.

### 0.10 The commutator-product map on `Aff(q)^{2s}` (LV20 Lemma 2.11)

**Theorem.** Let `q ≥ 3` be prime, `s ≥ 1` and `f : Aff(q)^{2s} → 𝔽_q^+, f(g₁, g₁', …, g_s, g_s') = [g₁, g₁'] ⋯ [g_s, g_s']` (with `[x, y] = xyx⁻¹y⁻¹`). Let `Λ : Aff(q)^{2s} → (𝔽_q^×)^{2s}` apply `λ` componentwise. Then: `(a)` if `f(g) ≠ 0`, then `g` generates `Aff(q)` if and only if the entries of `Λ(g)` generate `𝔽_q^×`, and in that case `g` generates `Aff(q)`; `(b)` the image of `{g : f(g) ≠ 0, g generates Aff(q)}` under `Λ` is exactly the set of `2s`-tuples whose entries generate `𝔽_q^×`; `(c)` every fibre of this restricted map over a point of its image has exactly `q^{2s−1}(q − 1)` elements.

*Proof sketch.* Write `g_i = mk(y_i, b_i), g_i' = mk(y_i', b_i')`; by the commutator formula `f(g) = Σ_i (b_i(1 − y_i') − b_i'(1 − y_i))`, an affine-linear function of `b = (b_i, b_i') ∈ 𝔽_q^{2s}` for fixed `y`. If the `y`'s generate `𝔽_q^×` then some `y_i` or `y_i'` differs from `1 (q ≥ 3)`, so `f` is a nonconstant affine-linear function on the fibre `𝔽_q^{2s}`; exactly `q^{2s} − q^{2s−1}` points have `f ≠ 0`. If `f(g) ≠ 0`, the subgroup `⟨g⟩` contains a nonzero translation, hence all of `𝔽_q^+` (prime order), and `⟨g⟩ = Aff(q)` iff its image `λ(⟨g⟩) = ⟨y⟩` is `𝔽_q^×`. Conversely a generating tuple has generating image under the surjection `λ`. Combine. (LV20, §2.6, Lemma 2.11, p. 14; §2.6, proof of Lemma 2.11, p. 14.) *Needs:* 0.7.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, s = 1`: the image consists of the 3 pairs in `{±1}²` generating `{±1}`, each fibre has `3·2 = 6` elements, giving the 18 generating pairs of `S_3` (all with nonzero commutator).
- `q = 3, s = 2: 15 · 54 = 810` tuples, giving 135 conjugacy classes.

### 0.11 Counting generating tuples of `ℤ/N`

**Lemma.** For integers `N ≥ 1` and `k ≥ 0`, the number of `k`-tuples `(y₁, …, y_k) ∈ (ℤ/N)^k` whose entries generate `ℤ/N` as a group is `J_k(N) = N^k ∏_{ℓ | N prime} (1 − ℓ^{−k})`, and for `k ≥ 2` one has `J_k(N) ≥ N^k/2` (`generatingTuples`, `card_generatingTuples`, `two_mul_card_generatingTuples_ge`).

*Proof sketch.* A tuple generates `ℤ/N` iff for every prime `ℓ | N` not all entries lie in `ℓℤ/N` (the maximal subgroups of `ℤ/N` are the `ℓℤ/N`). By the Chinese remainder theorem the count is multiplicative in `N`; for `N = ℓ^a` the non-generating tuples are those in `(ℓℤ/ℓ^a)^k`, of which there are `ℓ^{(a−1)k}`, giving `ℓ^{ak}(1 − ℓ^{−k})`. `∏_ℓ (1 − ℓ^{−k}) ≥ ∏_ℓ (1 − ℓ^{−2}) ≥ 1 − Σ_ℓ ℓ^{−2}`. `Σ_{ℓ prime} ℓ^{−2} ≤ 1/4 + Σ_{j≥1} (2j+1)^{−2} < 1/4 + Σ_{j≥1} 1/(4j(j+1)) = 1/2`, since `(2j+1)² > 4j(j+1)`. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 28.) *Needs:* Mathlib `Ideal.quotientInfRingEquivPiQuotient`; Mathlib `ZMod.card_units_eq_totient`.

**Checks.**

- `N = 6, k = 2: J₂(6) = 36 · (3/4) · (8/9) = 24 ≥ 18`.
- `N` prime: `J_k(N) = N^k − 1`.
- Empty tuples generate the trivial group but not `ℤ/2`; the respective counts are `1` and `0` (`card_generatingTuples_empty`).

### 0.12 Isotropic subgroups for a perfect pairing on a finite abelian group

Unlike Tau Ceti’s `FiniteBilinearModule`, this target does not assume a symmetric pairing; its application is alternating.

**Lemma.** Let `A` be a finite abelian group and `⟨·,·⟩ : A × A → ℚ/ℤ` a biadditive pairing that is perfect in the sense that `a ↦ ⟨·, a⟩` is a bijection from `A` to its character module `Hom(A, ℚ/ℤ)`. If `B ≤` A satisfies `⟨B, B⟩ = 0` then `|B|² ≤ |A|`. No symmetry of the pairing is assumed. (A pairing with values in `ℤ/N ⊂ ℚ/ℤ`, or in a cyclic group `μ_N^∨ ≅ ℤ/N` after a choice of generator, is a special case.) (`card_sq_le_of_isotropic`).

*Proof sketch.* `B^⊥ := {a : ⟨b, a⟩ = 0 for all b ∈ B}` contains `B` by isotropy. The composite `A → Hom(A, ℚ/ℤ) → Hom(B, ℚ/ℤ)` is surjective: the first map is bijective and restriction of `ℚ/ℤ`-valued characters along the injection `B ↪ A` is surjective. Its kernel is `B^⊥`, so `|B^⊥| = |A| / |Hom(B, ℚ/ℤ)|`; and `|Hom(B, ℚ/ℤ)| = |B|` because `ℚ/ℤ`-valued characters of the finite group `B` are the same as `ℂ`-valued ones (`t ↦ exp(2πit)` identifies `ℚ/ℤ` with the roots of unity in `ℂ^×`) and there are `|B|` of those. Hence `|B| ≤ |B^⊥| = |A|/|B|`. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 28.) *Needs:* Mathlib `CharacterModule`; Mathlib `CharacterModule.dual_surjective_of_injective`; Mathlib `AddChar.card_eq`.

**Checks.**

- `A = (ℤ/N)²` with the determinant pairing: a cyclic subgroup of order `N` is isotropic and `|B|² = |A|`.
- The symmetric case agrees with Tau Ceti's `IsNondegenerate.card_orthogonalQuotient_mul_card_sq`.

### 0.13 Minimal subrepresentations of a representation with an invariant form up to similitude

**Lemma.** Let `G` be a group, `k` a field, `V` a finite-dimensional `k`-linear representation of `G` and `B` a nondegenerate bilinear form on `V` that is symmetric or alternating, with `B`(gx, gy) `= χ(g)B(x, y)` for a character `χ : G → k^×`. If `V` is not irreducible and `W ⊆ V` is a nonzero subrepresentation of minimal dimension, then `dim W ≤ dim V / 2` (`finrank_le_half_of_minimal`).

*Proof sketch.* `W^⊥` is a subrepresentation because `G` preserves `B` up to scalars, and so is `W ∩ W^⊥`. By minimality `W ∩ W^⊥` is 0 or `W`. If `W ⊆ W^⊥` then `dim W ≤ dim V − dim W`. If `W ∩ W^⊥ = 0` then `W^⊥` is a nonzero subrepresentation of dimension `dim V − dim W` (`V ≠ W` since `V` is reducible), so by minimality `dim W ≤ dim W^⊥ = dim V − dim W`. (LV20, §6, proof of Lemma 6.1, p. 32.) *Needs:* Mathlib `LinearMap.BilinForm.finrank_orthogonal`.

**Checks.**

- `V = V₁ ⊕ V₂` orthogonal with `dim V₁ = 1, dim V₂ = 3`: the minimal `W` has `dim 1 ≤ 2`.
- The Tate module of an abelian variety with the Weil pairing (`χ` the cyclotomic character).

### 0.14 Symplectic transvections

**Definition.** Let `(V, ω)` be a finite-dimensional symplectic space over any field `k` (`ω` nondegenerate alternating, written `⟨·,·⟩`). For `v ∈ V` and `r ∈ k, T_v^r : V → V` is `x ↦ x + r⟨v, x⟩v`, and `T_v := T_v^1`. It is the Mathlib transvection with linear form `r⟨v, ·⟩` and vector `v` (the form vanishes on `v`).

- `symplecticTransvection`: `T_v^r ∈ Sp(V, ω)` (as an element of `TauCeti.BilinForm.isometryGroup ω`).
- `symplecticTransvection_apply`: `T_v^r x = x + r⟨v, x⟩ v`.
- `symplecticTransvection_add`: `T_v^r T_v^s = T_v^{r+s}`; `T_v^0 = 1`; `(T_v^r)⁻¹ = T_v^{−r}`.
- `symplecticTransvection_smul`: `T_{cv}^r = T_v^{c² r}`.
- `conj_symplecticTransvection`: `g T_v^r g⁻¹ = T_{gv}^r` for `g ∈ Sp(V, ω)`.
- `isUnipotent_symplecticTransvection`: `T_v^r` is unipotent: `(T_v^r − 1)² = 0`.
- `fixedPoints_symplecticTransvection`: For `v ≠ 0` and `r ≠ 0` the fixed space of `T_v^r` is `v^⊥`, of codimension 1, and the image of `T_v^r − 1` is `k·v`.
- `eq_symplecticTransvection_of_codim_one`: A unipotent element of `Sp(V, ω)` whose fixed space has codimension 1 equals `T_v^r` for some `v ≠ 0, r ≠ 0`.
- `symplecticTransvection_eq_transvection`: `T_v^r = LinearMap.transvection (r • ω v) v`.
- `symplecticTransvection_sl2`: On `(k², det)` the transvections `T_e^r, T_f^r` are the elementary matrices of `SL(2, k)`.
- `symplecticTransvection_isometry`: `T_v^r` preserves `ω: ω(T_v^r x, T_v^r y) = ω(x, y)`.
- `symplecticTransvection_sub_one_sq`: `(T_v^r − 1)² = 0`.

*Construction.* Define `T_v^r := LinearMap.transvection (r·⟨v, ·⟩) v`; since `⟨v, v⟩ = 0` it is invertible with inverse `T_v^{−r}`. Check `⟨T_v^r x, T_v^r y⟩ = ⟨x, y⟩` using bilinearity and `⟨v, v⟩ = 0`. (LV20, §2.7, (2.4), p. 15.) *Needs:* Mathlib `LinearMap.transvection`; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.BilinForm.isometryGroup`; Mathlib `LinearEquiv.transvection`.

**Checks.**

- For `ω(e,f)=1, T_e^2(f)=f+2e` and `T_e^2(e)=e`.
- `T_0^r=id` and `T_v^0=id`.
- As a linear map, `T_v^r` equals `LinearMap.transvection (r • ω(v,·)) v`; its inverse is `T_v^(−r)`.
- In the basis `(e, f)` of `k²` with `⟨e, f⟩ = 1, T_e^r = [[1, r], [0, 1]]` and `T_f^r = [[1, 0], [−r, 1]]`.
- `T_0^r = id`.

### 0.15 Finite-index, normality and connectedness properties of subgroup closures

Compared with the closure and image API of ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §7.6, this target adds finite-index invariance, normality and connected generation.

**Theorem.** Let `G` be an affine algebraic group over an algebraically closed field `k` and `Γ ⊆ G(k)` a subgroup, with reduced Zariski closure `Γ̄`. `(i)` If `Γ′ ⊆ Γ` has finite index then `Γ̄′` has finite index in `Γ̄` and their identity components agree. (ii) If `Γ′` is normal in `Γ` then `Γ̄′` is normal in `Γ̄`; more generally every element of `G(k)` normalizing `Γ′` normalizes `Γ̄′`. (iii) The closure of a subgroup generated by closed connected subgroups through the identity is connected.

*Proof sketch.* `(i): Γ` is a finite union of cosets of `Γ′`, so `Γ̄` is the finite union of translates of `Γ̄′`; the connected component of the identity is therefore contained in `Γ̄′`. (ii): conjugation by an element of `Γ` preserves the closure of `Γ′`, and the normalizer of a closed subgroup is closed, so it contains `Γ̄`. (iii): finite products of connected subgroups through the identity are connected, their union is connected, and its closure is connected. These arguments use the supplied closure as an algebraic subgroup; multiplication is not justified by identifying the Zariski topology on `(G×G)(k)` with the point-set product topology. (Mil-iAG, Proposition 1.14, p. 21; Lemma 1.26, p. 24; Definition 1.34, p. 25; Proposition 1.59, p. 33; Corollary 5.22, p. 86.) *Needs:* ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §7.6; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

### 0.16 The symplectic Lie algebra in characteristic zero

**Theorem.** Let `k` be a field of characteristic zero and `(V, ω)` a symplectic space of dimension `2n ≥ 2`. `(i) 𝔰𝔭(V, ω)` (Mathlib `skewAdjointLieSubalgebra ω`, isomorphic to `LieAlgebra.Symplectic.sp` after a choice of symplectic basis) is a simple Lie algebra of dimension `n(2n + 1)`. (ii) If `k` is algebraically closed, every Lie algebra automorphism of `𝔰𝔭(V, ω)` is `Ad(h)` for some `h ∈ Sp(V, ω)(k)`, and `h` is unique up to sign. (iii) The adjoint representation identifies `Sp(V, ω)/{±1}` with the identity component of the automorphism group scheme of `𝔰𝔭(V, ω)` (`isSimple_skewAdjointLieSubalgebra`).

*Proof sketch.* After a symplectic basis, `𝔰𝔭(V, ω)` is the split simple Lie algebra of type `C_n` (for `n = 1` it is `𝔰𝔩₂`); simplicity follows from the root decomposition: every nonzero ideal contains a root vector, hence all root vectors, since the root system is irreducible. An automorphism of a split semisimple Lie algebra is the product of an inner automorphism and a diagram automorphism; the Dynkin diagram of type `C_n` has no nontrivial automorphisms, so every automorphism is inner, `Ad(g)` for `g` in the adjoint group `PSp(V)(k) = Sp(V)(k)/{±1}` (`k` algebraically closed). (Mil-LAG, Example `(C_n)`, pp. 94–95; Automorphisms of split semisimple Lie algebras, p. 100; Mil-iAG, Corollary 22.120, p. 418; forms of `Sp_{2n}`, p. 482.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 2; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 7; Mathlib `LieAlgebra.IsSimple`; Mathlib `LieAlgebra.Symplectic.sp`.

### 0.17 Centre and geometric normal subgroups of the symplectic group

This target specializes the reductive-group structure theory to geometric closed normal subgroups of `Sp`; it does not concern arbitrary normal subgroups of the abstract group of `k`-points.

**Theorem.** Let `k` be algebraically closed of characteristic zero and `(V, ω)` a symplectic space of dimension `2n ≥ 2`. The centre of `Sp(V, ω)` is `{±1}`, and every proper Zariski-closed normal subgroup of `Sp(V, ω)` is contained in `{±1}`.

*Proof sketch.* An element commuting with all transvections `T_v` preserves every line kv, so it is a scalar `c` with `c²=1`. For a closed normal subgroup `N, Lie(N)` is an ideal of the simple Lie algebra `𝔰𝔭(V)`. If `Lie(N)=𝔰𝔭(V)`, equality of Lie algebras in characteristic zero and connectedness give `N°=Sp(V)`. Otherwise `Lie(N)=0`, hence `N` is finite; a finite normal subgroup of a connected group is central. (LV20, §8.5, p. 46; Mil-iAG, 5.58, p. 95; Proposition 12.13, p. 187; almost-simple groups, p. 418.) *Needs:* 0.14; 0.16; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layers 6–7.

### 0.18 Orbit maps, transporters and dimensions of orbit closures

**Theorem.** Let `G` be an algebraic group over a field `k` acting on a variety `X`, and `x ∈ X(k)`. `(i)` The orbit map `g ↦ g·x` is a morphism `G → X`; its geometric orbit `G·x` (the reduced locally closed orbit obtained from the constructible image after geometric base change, rather than just `G(k)·x`) is locally closed, open in its closure, and `dim` closure`(G·x) = dim G − dim G_x ≤ dim G`. (ii) For closed subvarieties `Y, Z ⊆ X` the transporter `{g ∈ G : g·Y ⊆ Z}` is closed. (iii) The same bound holds for a finite union of orbits, and for orbits of a subgroup of `G(k)` replaced by its Zariski closure.

*Proof sketch.* The orbit map is the composite of `g ↦ (g, x)` with the action morphism. Its image is constructible (Chevalley) and homogeneous, hence open in its closure; the fibres are cosets of the stabilizer, so the dimension formula follows from the fibre-dimension theorem. The transporter is the functor of `g` carrying `Y_R` into `Z_R` for every `k`-algebra `R`; Milne’s representability argument makes it a closed subscheme. Over an algebraic closure, its points are also obtained by intersecting the pointwise transport conditions. Testing only `Y(k)` would be insufficient over a finite field. (Mil-iAG, Proposition 1.58, p. 32; Definition 9.5, p. 142; (9.40), p. 148.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0.

### 0.19 The Zariski closure of the powers of a transvection

**Lemma.** Let `(V, ω)` be symplectic over a field `k` of characteristic zero and `v ∈ V` nonzero. The Zariski closure in `Sp(V)` of the cyclic group `{T_v^n : n ∈ ℤ}` is the one-parameter unipotent subgroup `U_v = {T_v^r : r ∈ k}`, the image of the closed immersion `𝔾_a → Sp(V), r ↦ T_v^r`. More generally the Zariski closure of `{T_v^{nr₀} : n ∈ ℤ}` is `U_v` for every `r₀ ≠ 0`.

*Proof sketch.* `r ↦ T_v^r` is a homomorphism `𝔾_a → Sp(V)` and a closed immersion (its coordinate functions include `r·`(a linear form) with nonzero coefficient). The image of `ℤ r₀` is an infinite subset of `𝔾_a(k) = k` because char `k = 0`; an infinite subset of the affine line is Zariski dense. The Zariski closure of the image equals the image of the closure under a closed immersion. (LV20, §2.7, proof of Lemma 2.13, p. 15.) *Needs:* 0.14; 0.15; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- `V = k², v = e`: the closure of `{[[1, n], [0, 1]]}` is the upper unipotent subgroup.
- In characteristic `p` the closure of `ℤ` is finite; the hypothesis char `k = 0` is needed.

### 0.20 Two transvections with nonzero pairing (LV20 Lemma 2.13)

**Lemma.** Let `(V, ω)` be symplectic over a field `k` of characteristic zero and `v₁, v₂ ∈ V` linearly independent with `⟨v₁, v₂⟩ ≠ 0`. The Zariski closure of the subgroup generated by `T_{v₁}` and `T_{v₂}` contains `T_v^r` for every `v ∈ span(v₁, v₂)` and every `r ∈ k`; in fact it contains the subgroup `Sp(P) × 1` of `Sp(V)` for `P = span(v₁, v₂)`, acting trivially on `P^⊥`.

*Proof sketch.* `P = span(v₁, v₂)` is a nondegenerate plane, `V = P ⊕ P^⊥`, and `T_{v₁}, T_{v₂}` act trivially on `P^⊥`. The closure contains `U_{v₁}` and `U_{v₂}` (Zariski closure of transvection powers). In the basis `(v₁, v₂/⟨v₁,v₂⟩)` of `P, U_{v₁}` and `U_{v₂}` are the upper and lower elementary unipotent subgroups of `SL(P) = Sp(P)`; every element of `SL(2, k)` is a product of elementary transvections, so the closure contains `SL(P) × 1`. For `v ∈ P, T_v^r` restricted to `P` lies in `Sp(P)` and is the identity on `P^⊥`. (LV20, §2.7, Lemma 2.13, p. 15.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.17; 0.19; Mathlib `Matrix.SL2.transvection_induction`; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- `V = k², v₁ = e, v₂ = f`: the closure of `⟨[[1,1],[0,1]], [[1,0],[−1,1]]⟩` (a finite-index subgroup of `SL₂(ℤ)`) is `SL₂`.
- If `⟨v₁, v₂⟩ = 0` the two transvections commute and the closure is two-dimensional unipotent.

### 0.21 Transvections along a connected intersection graph (LV20 Lemma 2.14)

**Lemma.** Let `(V, ω)` be symplectic over a field `k` of characteristic zero and `S ⊆ V` a finite set of vectors. Form the graph on `S` with an edge between `v, v'` when `⟨v, v'⟩ ≠ 0`. If this graph is connected then the Zariski closure of the subgroup generated by `{T_v : v ∈ S}` contains `T_w` for every `w` in the span of `S`. If moreover `S` spans `V`, the closure is `Sp(V)`.

*Proof sketch.* Induct on `|S|`: remove a vertex `v` whose deletion leaves `S₀` connected (a leaf of a spanning tree); by induction the closure `G` contains `T_w` for all `w ∈ W = span(S₀)`. For `w ∈ W` with `⟨w, v⟩ ≠ 0`, 0.20 applied to `T_w, T_v` (both in `G`) gives `T_x ∈ G` for `x = w + v`, and `T_{cx}` for all `c`. The set `{w ∈ W : ⟨w, v⟩ ≠ 0}` is a nonempty Zariski-open subset of `W` (nonempty since `v` has a neighbour in `S₀`), so `{T_{w+v}}` is dense in `{T_{w+v} : w ∈ W}` and the closedness of `G` gives all `T_x` for `x ∈ W +` kv. If `S` spans `V`, the transvections generate `Sp(V)` as an abstract group, so the closure is `Sp(V)`. (LV20, §2.7, Lemma 2.14, p. 15.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.17; 0.20; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 7.

**Checks.**

- A symplectic basis `e₁, f₁, e₂, f₂` is not connected; adding `e₁ + e₂` connects it.
- For a chain of vanishing cycles with nonzero adjacent pairings, the closure contains all transvections centred in span `S`. If `S` spans the ambient nondegenerate `V`, the closure is `Sp(V)`; without that spanning condition, span `S` can be degenerate and no embedded `Sp`(span `S`) is asserted.

### 0.22 Two-factor Goursat dichotomy for simple Lie algebras

**Lemma.** Let `k` be a field and `s,t` nonabelian simple `k`-Lie algebras. If `h ≤ s × t` projects surjectively to both factors, either `h = s × t` or `h` is the graph of a `k`-Lie equivalence `s ≃ t` (`lieSubalgebra_prod_eq_top_or_graph`).

*Proof sketch.* The kernels of the two restricted projections induce ideals `I = {x | (x,0) ∈ h}` in `s` and `J = {y | (0,y) ∈ h}` in `t`: lift any element of a factor to `h` before taking the bracket. Simplicity makes `I` and `J` either zero or the whole factor. If either is whole, surjectivity shows both are whole and `h` is the product. Otherwise both projections are bijective; compose one inverse with the other to obtain the equivalence and graph description. (LV20, §2.7, discussion and Lemma 2.12, p. 14.)

**Checks.**

- The diagonal in `sl₂(k) × sl₂(k)` is the graph case.
- The product has both kernel ideals nonzero.
- Nonabelian simplicity is essential: an abelian diagonal falls outside the product-ideal argument of the next target.

### 0.23 Ideals in a finite product of nonabelian simple Lie algebras

**Lemma.** If `(sᵢ)ᵢ∈I` is a finite family of nonabelian simple `k`-Lie algebras, every ideal in `∏ᵢ sᵢ` is the product of a subset of the factors.

*Proof sketch.* For `x` in the ideal and `xᵢ ≠ 0`, bracket `x` with vectors supported in coordinate `i`. Since the centre of a nonabelian simple Lie algebra is zero, one such bracket is nonzero. These brackets lie in the intersection of the ideal with the `i`-th factor, which is an ideal there and hence the whole factor. Subtract all the resulting coordinate vectors from `x`; finiteness proves that the original ideal is precisely the sum/product of its nonzero coordinate factors. (LV20, §2.7, discussion and Lemma 2.12, p. 14.)

**Checks.**

- There is no diagonal ideal in `s × s` when `s` is nonabelian simple.
- Zero and the whole product correspond to the empty and full subsets.

### 0.24 Goursat's lemma for simple Lie algebras (Ribet)

**Lemma.** Let `𝔤₁, …, 𝔤_N` be nonabelian simple Lie algebras over a field `k` and `𝔥 ⊆ 𝔤₁ × ⋯ × 𝔤_N` a Lie subalgebra whose projection to `𝔤_i × 𝔤_j` is surjective for every pair `i < j` (and to each `𝔤_i` when `N = 1`). Then `𝔥 = 𝔤₁ × ⋯ × 𝔤_N`.

*Proof sketch.* Induct on the number of factors. The projection to the first `n−1` factors is the whole product by the induction hypothesis. Apply the general kernel/quotient Goursat construction to `h ≤ (∏ᵢ<n sᵢ) × sₙ`. Its kernel in the first product is an ideal, hence a product of factors by 0.23. If the last projection kernel is all of `sₙ, h` is the product. Otherwise the quotient of the first product is simple and isomorphic to `sₙ`, hence exactly one surviving factor; the corresponding pairwise projection is a graph, contradicting pairwise surjectivity. (LV20, §2.7, before Lemma 2.12, p. 14.) *Needs:* 0.22; 0.23; Mathlib `LieAlgebra.IsSimple`.

**Checks.**

- `𝔤 × 𝔤` with the diagonal: not surjective onto the pair, and indeed a proper subalgebra.
- `N = 3` with `𝔥 = {(x, y, x)}`: fails the hypothesis for the pair `(1, 3)`.
- If one-dimensional abelian Lie algebras were admitted as simple, `{(x,y,z) : x+y+z=0} ⊂ k³` would surject onto every pair while remaining proper. Mathlib’s `LieAlgebra.IsSimple` excludes abelian algebras.

### 0.25 A closed subgroup of `Sp(V) × Sp(V)` with surjective projections and an unbalanced unipotent pair

**Lemma.** Let `k` be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, `(V, ω)` symplectic of dimension `≥ 2` and `G ⊆ Sp(V) × Sp(V)` a Zariski-closed subgroup whose projections `π₁, π₂` to both factors are surjective. If some `g ∈ G` has `π₁(g), π₂(g)` unipotent with fixed spaces of different dimensions, then `Lie(G) = 𝔰𝔭(V) × 𝔰𝔭(V)` and `G = Sp(V) × Sp(V)`.

*Proof sketch.* `Lie(G) ⊆ 𝔰𝔭(V) × 𝔰𝔭(V)` projects onto both factors (surjective homomorphisms of smooth groups in characteristic zero are surjective on Lie algebras). By Goursat for the simple Lie algebra `𝔰𝔭(V)`, either `Lie(G)` is the whole product (then `G° = Sp(V)²` since `Sp(V)` is connected, so `G = Sp(V)²`), or `Lie(G)` is the graph of an automorphism `θ` of `𝔰𝔭(V)`. In the second case `θ = Ad(h)` for some `h ∈ Sp(V)(k)` (automorphisms of `𝔰𝔭(V)` over an algebraically closed field are inner), and `G°` is the graph `{(x, hxh^{-1})}` (connected subgroups are determined by their Lie algebras in characteristic zero). Every `(a, b) ∈ G` normalizes `G°`, so `a⁻¹h⁻¹bh` centralizes `Sp(V)` and `b = ±hah⁻¹` (centre `{±1}`). Apply this to `g: π₂(g) = ±hπ₁(g)h⁻¹`; a unipotent element is not the negative of a unipotent element in characteristic zero, so `π₂(g) = hπ₁(g)h⁻¹` has a fixed space of the same dimension — a contradiction. (LV20, §2.7, Lemma 2.12, p. 14–15.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.16; 0.17; 0.22; 0.24; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.Symplectic.geometricallyConnectedCommHopfAlgProperty_coordinateHopfAlgebra`; Tau Ceti `LinearMap.GeneralLinearGroup.IsUnipotent`; Mathlib `LieAlgebra.Symplectic.sp`; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 2; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 7; Tau Ceti `Derivation.tensorProductLieEquiv`, `TauCeti.HopfIdeal.eq_of_le_of_finrank_quotientLie_le`.

**Checks.**

- The diagonal subgroup satisfies the projection hypothesis but has no unbalanced pair.
- For `V = k²` with `g = (u, 1), u` a nontrivial transvection, the lemma gives `SL₂ × SL₂`.

### 0.26 Zariski-closed subgroups of `Sp(V)^N` (LV20 Lemma 2.12)

**Theorem.** Let `k` be a field of characteristic zero, `(V, ω)` a symplectic space of dimension `≥ 2` and `G ⊆ Sp(V)^N` a Zariski-closed subgroup such that `(i)` each projection `π_i : G → Sp(V)` is surjective and (ii) for all `i ≠ j` there is `g ∈ G` with `π_i(g), π_j(g)` unipotent with fixed spaces of different dimensions. Then `G = Sp(V)^N`.

*Proof sketch.* Base change to an algebraic closure `k̄`: the hypotheses persist (surjectivity and closedness are geometric; the elements `g` are `k`-points) and `G = Sp(V)^N` can be checked over `k̄`. For each pair `i < j` apply 0.25 to the image of `G` in `Sp(V) × Sp(V)` (closed, as the image of a homomorphism of algebraic groups): Lie of that image is `𝔰𝔭(V)²`. Hence `Lie(G) ⊆ 𝔰𝔭(V)^N` surjects onto every pair of factors; by 0.24 `Lie(G) = 𝔰𝔭(V)^N`. `G°` is a connected closed subgroup of the connected group `Sp(V)^N` with the same Lie algebra, hence `G° = Sp(V)^N` and `G = Sp(V)^N`. (LV20, §2.7, Lemma 2.12, p. 14–15.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.16; 0.24; 0.25; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- `N = 1`: the statement reduces to hypothesis `(i)`.
- The variant in LV20 Lemma 4.3: `SL₂ = Sp₂`, and an element `(1, …, u, …, 1)` with `u` a nontrivial unipotent gives the unbalanced pairs.

### Examples

- For `E = ℚ_{p²}` with Frobenius `σ` and `φ = σ ⊕ σ` on `E²`, `Z(φ) = M₂(ℚ_p)`, of `ℚ_p`-dimension `4 = dim_E Z(φ²)`.
- In `Aff(5)`, `(x ↦ 2x + 1) ∘ (x ↦ 3x + 4) = (x ↦ x + 4)`; `Aff(3) = Sym(𝔽₃)` has order `6`, and
  `x ↦ 2x + 1` in `Aff(5)` has one fixed point (`x = 4`) and one `4`-cycle, since `2` has order `4` in `𝔽₅^×`.
- `J_2(6) = 36·(1 − 1/4)(1 − 1/9) = 24` generating pairs of `ℤ/6`, and `J_k(N) ≥ N^k/2` because
  `∏_ℓ (1 − ℓ^{−2}) = 6/π² > 1/2`.
- For `ω(e, f) = 1`, `T_e²(f) = f + 2e` and `T_e²(e) = e`; `T_{ce}^r = T_e^{c²r}`.

### Dependencies

SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §7.6; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 2; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 7; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.1: Galois representations, friendly places and Hodge weights

The global input is Faltings's finiteness lemma: given the ramification set, the dimension and the
weight, there are only finitely many semisimple `p`-adic representations that are pure with integral
Frobenius polynomials. The local input is purity of Hodge weights at a friendly place: a de Rham
character that is pure of weight `w` has its Hodge–Tate exponents constrained by the largest
CM-or-totally-real subfield of `K`, and at a place inert in the CM quadratic extension this forces the
exponent `w/2`. Taking determinants turns this into the statement that the average Hodge weight of a
pure representation is `w/2`, and finite induction adds these averages over the places above `v` with
their local degrees; the integer-indexed filtration is kept throughout, because truncating at zero
would break the determinant and twist identities. The layer ends with the properties of `H¹` of an
abelian variety that Layers LV.4, LV.6 and LV.7 use.

### 1.1 A finite Frobenius test set

This target extends FaltingsFinitenessAndIsogenyTheorems §R28.5.2 from semisimple isomorphism
to equality of traces without a semisimplicity assumption. ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations
§1.7'`s` semisimplification preserves traces and ramification, so the supplied criterion also
proves this extension; the direct image-algebra proof below fixes the test-set uniformity.

**Theorem.** Let `K` be a number field, `T` a finite set of finite places, `p` a prime and `d ≥ 1`. There is a finite set `T′` disjoint from `T` such that, for any two continuous representations `ρ₁, ρ₂ : G_K → GL_d(ℚ_p)` unramified outside `T`, equality of their traces on arithmetic Frobenius at every `v ∈ T′` implies equality of their traces on every `g ∈ G_K`. If the two representations are semisimple, they are isomorphic. Frobenius traces are independent of the chosen lift because inertia acts trivially.

*Proof sketch.* Hermite–Minkowski makes the set of Galois extensions of `K` of degree at most `p^{2d²}`, unramified outside `T`, finite. For each such extension and each conjugacy class choose a Frobenius place outside `T` by Chebotarev. Their union is `T′`. Choose stable `ℤ_p`-lattices for the two representations, possible because their images are compact. Let `M` be the `ℤ_p`-span of the image of `ρ₁ × ρ₂` in `M_d(ℤ_p) × M_d(ℤ_p)`. It is a finite free algebra of rank at most `2d²`. The finite image in `(M/pM)^×` has order at most `p^{2d²}`, and its corresponding extension is unramified outside `T`. Every element of that image is conjugate to a chosen Frobenius image. Take all `G_K`-conjugates of the chosen Frobenius lifts, not just one lift per conjugacy class. The trace difference vanishes on them by conjugation invariance, and their classes span `M/pM` because group images span `M`. Nakayama shows that these lifts span `M`. The trace difference therefore vanishes on `M` and on every Galois element. Apply the semisimple trace criterion for the final assertion. (Del85, Théorème 3.1 and proof, printed p. 40, PDF p. 17; Fal83, §6, proof of Satz 5, printed pp. 362–363.) *Needs:* ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §§1.6, 1.8; ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1; TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev Layer 10; compact-image stable lattices from ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations; Mathlib `Submodule.le_of_le_smul_of_le_jacobson_bot`.

**Checks.**

- For `d = 1`, equal test traces determine continuous characters themselves, since each trace is its value.
- Without semisimplicity, a nontrivial unipotent extension and the split trivial representation have equal traces; the conclusion is equality of characters, not isomorphism.
- No place in `T` is tested, and enlarging `T` still permits a new finite test set; places above `p` may belong to `T`.

### 1.2 Faltings's finiteness lemma (LV20 Lemma 2.3)

**Theorem.** Let `K` be a number field, `T` a finite set of finite places of `K, p` a prime and `w, d ≥ 0` integers. Up to isomorphism there are only finitely many semisimple continuous representations `ρ : G_K → GL_d(ℚ_p)` that are unramified outside `T`, pure of weight `w` outside `T` and have integral geometric-Frobenius polynomials outside `T`. (Lawrence and Venkatesh state the lemma with 'unramified outside `S'` for a set `S` not containing the places above `p`, while applying it to `p`-adic cohomology; the correct and intended hypothesis allows `T` to contain the places above `p`.)

*Proof sketch.* If `d = 0` there is only the zero representation. For the remaining proof assume `d ≥ 1`, as required by the Frobenius test-set lemma. Let `T'` be a Frobenius test set for `(K, T, p, d)`. For `v ∈ T'` the characteristic polynomial of `ρ(Frob_v^geom)` is one of the finitely many integer Weil polynomials of degree `d` and weight `w` for `q = q_v`; it determines the characteristic polynomial, hence the trace, of `ρ(Frob_v) = ρ(Frob_v^geom)^{-1}`. So the tuple (tr `ρ(Frob_v)`)`_{v∈T'}` takes finitely many values, and representations with the same tuple are isomorphic by the supplied trace criterion and the Frobenius test set of this layer. (LV20, §2.3, Lemma 2.3, p. 9.) *Needs:* FaltingsFinitenessAndIsogenyTheorems §R28.5.1 (`finite_weilPolynomials`); 1.1; ArithmeticGaloisRepresentations §1.8; WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.1; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §§0.1–0.2.

**Checks.**

- `d = 1, w = 0, T = ∅` over `ℚ`: only the trivial character (finite order characters unramified everywhere).
- Integrality is necessary: over `ℚ(i)`, with `p=5`, the quotient of the two split 5-adic CM characters and all its powers are pure of weight zero with the same fixed ramification set. At a prime generated by `3+2i` their Frobenius values include `(3+2i)/(3−2i)=(5+12i)/13`, of absolute value 1 but nonintegral. The powers give infinitely many distinct rank-one representations.

### 1.3 CM or totally real fields via complex conjugations

**Lemma.** Fix an algebraic closure `ℚ̄` and let `C ⊆ Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ)` be the set of complex conjugations (the automorphisms `ι⁻¹ ∘ conj ∘ ι` for embeddings `ι : ℚ̄ → ℂ`). A number field `F ⊆ ℚ̄` is totally real or CM if and only if `c₁c₂` fixes `F` pointwise for all `c₁, c₂ ∈ C`. In that case there is an automorphism `s_F` of `F` with `s_F² = 1` and `c ∘ τ = τ ∘ s_F` for every `c ∈ C` and every embedding `τ : F → ℚ̄`; `s_F = 1` iff `F` is totally real, and `s_F` is the complex conjugation of `F` when `F` is CM.

*Proof sketch.* If `F` is CM, every complex embedding `φ` satisfies `φ ∘ complexConj = conj ∘ φ`; translating through `ι` gives `c ∘ τ = τ ∘ s_F` for all `c ∈ C` and `τ`, hence `c₁c₂τ = τ`. If `F` is totally real, every `c ∈ C` fixes every `τ(F)`. Conversely the hypothesis is conjugation invariant, so `c₁c₂ ∘ τ = τ` for every `τ`, i.e. `c ∘ τ` is independent of `c ∈ C`. Taking `τ` the inclusion and `c' = gcg⁻¹` with `g ∈ Gal(ℚ̄/F)` shows `c(F)` is fixed by `Gal(ℚ̄/F)`, so `c(F) = F` and `s_F := c|_F` is an automorphism with `c ∘ τ = τ ∘ s_F` for all `τ`. If `s_F = 1` all embeddings are real; otherwise `F^{s_F}` is totally real, `[F : F^{s_F}] = 2` and no embedding of `F` is real, so `F` is CM (Milne, Proposition 1.4 `(b) ⇒ (a)`). (Mil-CM, Chapter `I`, Proposition 1.4 and Remark 1.6, p. 10; Chapter `I`, Remark 1.6, p. 10.) *Needs:* Mathlib `NumberField.IsCMField`; Mathlib `NumberField.IsCMField.complexEmbedding_complexConj`; Mathlib `NumberField.maximalRealSubfield`.

**Checks.**

- `ℚ(i)` is CM with `s =` conjugation; `ℚ(√2)` is totally real; `ℚ(2^{1/3})` is neither, and indeed two complex conjugations act differently on its conjugate fields.

### 1.4 The largest CM-or-totally-real subfield of a number field

**Definition.** Let `K ⊆ ℚ̄` be a number field, `H ⊆ Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ)` the closed subgroup topologically generated by `{c₁c₂ : c₁, c₂ ∈ C}` and `H⁺` the closed subgroup generated by `C`. Put `E_K := K ∩ ℚ̄^H` and use `E_K⁺ := NumberField.maximalRealSubfield K`, identified with `K ∩ ℚ̄^{H⁺}`. Then `E_K⁺` is the maximal totally real subfield of `K, E_K` is the largest subfield of `K` that is totally real or CM, `[E_K : E_K⁺] ≤ 2`, and `E_K` is CM if and only if `K` has a CM subfield (in which case `E_K` is the maximal CM subfield of LV20 Definition 2.7 and `E_K⁺` its maximal totally real subfield) (`largestCMSubfield_isTotallyReal_or_isCMField`).

- `largestCMSubfield`: `E_K` as an IntermediateField `ℚ K`.
- `le_largestCMSubfield_iff`: A subfield `F ≤ K` satisfies `F ≤ E_K` iff `F` is totally real or CM.
- `isCMField_largestCMSubfield_iff`: IsCMField `E_K ↔ K` has a CM subfield `↔ E_K ≠ E_K⁺`.
- `finrank_largestCMSubfield_div`: `[E_K : E_K⁺] ∈ {1, 2}`.
- `largestCMSubfield_map`: For a field isomorphism `σ : K ≃ K', σ(E_K) = E_{K'}`.
- `embeddings_eq_on_largestCMSubfield_iff`: Two embeddings `τ, τ' : K → ℚ̄` agree on `E_K` iff `τ' = hτ` for some `h` in the closure `H`.
- `largestCMSubfield_cyclotomic`: `E_{ℚ(ζ_n)} = ℚ(ζ_n)` for `n ≥ 3`; `E_{ℚ(2^{1/3})} = ℚ`.

*Construction.* A subfield `F ⊆ K` is fixed by `H` iff it is totally real or CM (1.3), so `E_K` is the largest such subfield; similarly `F` is fixed by `H⁺` iff it is totally real. `H` has index at most 2 in `H⁺` (every `c ∈ C` lies in `cH` for a fixed `c`), so `[E_K : E_K⁺] ≤ 2`. If `K` contains a CM field `F` then `F ⊆ E_K` is not totally real, so `E_K ≠ E_K⁺` and `E_K` is CM; conversely `E_K` CM gives a CM subfield. (Mil-CM, Chapter `I`, Remark 1.7, pp. 10–11; Chapter `I`, Corollary 1.5, p. 10.) *Needs:* 1.3; Mathlib `InfiniteGalois.fixedField_fixingSubgroup`; Mathlib `InfiniteGalois.fixingSubgroup_fixedField`; Mathlib `NumberField.maximalRealSubfield`.

**Checks.**

- For every CM number field `K, E_K=K` (`largestCMSubfield_eq_top_of_isCMField`); in particular this applies to `ℚ(i,√2)`.
- For every totally real number field `K, E_K=K` and `E_K⁺=K`.
- If `[K:ℚ]=3` and `K` is not totally real, `E_K=ℚ` (`largestCMSubfield_eq_bot_of_cubic`); this distinguishes `ℚ`(real cube root of 2) from a totally real cubic field.
- `K = ℚ(ζ_n), n ≥ 3: E_K = K`.
- `K = ℚ(2^{1/3}): E_K = E_K⁺ = ℚ`.
- `E_{ℚ(i,√2)}=ℚ(i,√2)` and `E⁺=ℚ(√2)`.

### 1.5 Friendly places (LV20 Definition 2.7)

**Definition.** Let `K` be a number field and `v` a finite place of `K`. If `K` has a CM subfield, `v` is friendly when it is unramified over `ℚ` and lies above a place of `E_K⁺` that is inert in the CM field `E_K`. If `K` has no CM subfield, `v` is friendly when it is unramified over `ℚ`.

- `IsFriendly`: The predicate on HeightOneSpectrum `(𝓞 K)`.
- `IsFriendly.isUnramified`: A friendly place is unramified over `ℚ`.
- `isFriendly_iff_of_not_hasCMSubfield`: If `K` has no CM subfield, IsFriendly `v ↔ v` is unramified over `ℚ`.
- `isFriendly_iff_exists_decomposition`: IsFriendly `v ↔ v` unramified over `ℚ ∧` (`E_K` CM `→ ∃ δ` in the decomposition group at `v` with `δ` acting on `E_K` as its complex conjugation).
- `isFriendly_of_frobenius`: If `Frob_℘ ∈ Gal(K'/ℚ)` (`K'` Galois over `ℚ` containing `K`) restricts to the nontrivial automorphism of `E_K` over `E_K⁺`, then the place of `K` below `℘` is friendly when unramified over `ℚ` (used with Chebotarev in Layer LV.11).
- `isFriendly_map`: Friendliness is preserved by isomorphisms of number fields.
- `isFriendly_rat`: Over `ℚ` every prime is friendly; over `ℚ(i)` exactly the primes above `p ≡ 3 mod 4`.

*Construction.* Define IsFriendly `v` by cases on whether `E_K` is CM, using the largest CM subfield and the decomposition behaviour of the place of `E_K⁺` below `v`. Equivalently, in group-theoretic terms: for an embedding `ι_p : ℚ̄ → ℚ̄_p` inducing `v` on `K` and its decomposition group `D ⊆ Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ), v` is friendly iff `v` is unramified over `ℚ` and, when `E_K` is CM, some `δ ∈ D` satisfies `δ ∘ τ = τ ∘ s_{E_K}` on `E_K` for the embedding `τ =` inclusion. (LV20, §2.4, Definition 2.7, p. 12.) *Needs:* 1.4; Mathlib `IsArithFrobAt`.

**Checks.**

- For `K=ℚ(i)`, the place above 3 is friendly, while either place above 5 is not.
- Every unramified finite place of a number field with no CM subfield is friendly.
- The ramified place above 2 of `ℚ(i)` is not friendly.
- `K = ℚ`: every prime is friendly.
- `K = ℚ(i): v` is friendly iff it lies above `p ≡ 3 mod 4`.
- `K = ℚ(2^{1/3})`: every place unramified over `ℚ` is friendly.

### 1.6 Functions on embeddings with constant conjugate sum factor through the largest CM subfield (Artin–Weil)

**Lemma.** Let `K ⊆ ℚ̄` be a number field, `w ∈ ℤ` and `m : Hom(K, ℚ̄) → ℤ` with `m(c ∘ τ) + m(τ) = w` for all `τ` and all complex conjugations `c ∈ C`. Then `m(τ)` depends only on `τ|_{E_K}`. If `K` has no CM subfield then `m(τ) = w/2` for all `τ`; in particular `w` is even.

*Proof sketch.* For `c₁, c₂ ∈ C, m(c₁c₂τ) = w − m(c₂τ) = m(τ)`; so `m` is invariant under the subgroup generated by the `c₁c₂`, and, being locally constant for the (continuous) action on the finite set `Hom(K, ℚ̄)`, under its closure `H`. By the infinite Galois correspondence, two embeddings that agree on `E_K = K ∩ ℚ̄^H` differ by an element of `H`; hence `m` factors through restriction to `E_K`. If `K` has no CM subfield then `E_K` is totally real, so `c ∘ τ` and `τ` agree on `E_K`; hence `m(cτ) = m(τ)` and `2m(τ) = w`. (Mil-CM, Chapter `I`, Infinity types, proof of Proposition 4.9, pp. 37–38; Chapter `I`, Infinity types, proof of Proposition 4.9, p. 38; LV20, §2.4, before the proof of Lemma 2.8, p. 12.) *Needs:* 1.4; Mathlib `InfiniteGalois.fixedField_fixingSubgroup`.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ(i), m(id) = 1, m(conj) = 0, w = 1`: the infinity type of the CM elliptic curve with multiplication by `ℤ[i]`.
- `K = ℚ: m ≡ w/2`.

### 1.7 The local Artin map on units is onto inertia

**Lemma.** Let `F` be a finite extension of `ℚ_p` and `Art_F : F^× → G_F^ab` the local Artin map. Then `Art_F` restricts to an isomorphism of the unit group `𝒪_F^×` onto the inertia subgroup `I(F^ab/F)` of `G_F^ab`, compatibly with the filtrations by higher unit groups and upper ramification groups. Consequently a continuous character `ψ : G_F → ℚ_p^×` has finite image on inertia if and only if `ψ ∘ Art_F` is trivial on an open subgroup of `𝒪_F^×`.

*Proof sketch.* Local reciprocity identifies `F^ab ∩ F^un` with the fixed field of `Art_F(𝒪_F^×)` and makes `Art_F` a continuous homomorphism with dense image whose restriction to `𝒪_F^×` is injective with closed image; the image of `𝒪_F^×` is therefore the inertia group of `F^ab/F`. The consequence follows because `ψ` factors through `G_F^ab`, and on the inertia quotient the unit restriction is an isomorphism, and a continuous homomorphism from a compact group has finite image exactly when its kernel is open. (Mil-CFT, Chapter `I`, Theorem 1.1 and §1.11, pp. 20–24.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap ClassFieldTheory Layer 7.

### 1.8 Continuous Galois characters as idele class characters

**Lemma.** Let `K` be a number field. There is a unique continuous homomorphism `Art_K : 𝔸_K^× → G_K^ab`, trivial on `K^×` and on the identity component at infinity, whose composite with restriction to every finite abelian `L/K` is the global Artin map of `L/K` and whose restriction to `K_v^×` is the local Artin map at every place `v`. Hence for a continuous character `η : G_K → ℚ_p^×` unramified outside a finite set `T` of finite places, `η ∘ Art_K` is a continuous character of `𝔸_K^×/K^×`; at a finite place `℘ ∉ T` it is unramified with value `η(Frob_℘)` (arithmetic Frobenius) on a uniformizer, at a place above `p` it is `η|_{G_{K_℘}} ∘ Art_{K_℘}`, and it is trivial on the identity component at the archimedean places.

*Proof sketch.* Take the inverse limit of the finite-level global Artin maps over the finite abelian extensions of `K`; compatibility in towers makes them a projective system, continuity holds at finite level, and local–global compatibility passes to the limit. For `η`, factor through `G_K^ab` and compose; the description at `℘ ∉ T` is the arithmetic Frobenius normalization of the finite-level maps. (Mil-CFT, Chapter `V`, Proposition 5.2 and Theorem 5.3, pp. 178–179.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap ClassFieldTheory Layer 11; TauCetiRoadmap ClassFieldTheory Layer 7.

### 1.9 At a friendly place a place-constant function with the conjugation relation takes the value `w/2`

**Lemma.** Let `K ⊆ ℚ̄` be a number field, `p` a prime, `ι_p : ℚ̄ → ℚ̄_p` an embedding, and for each `τ ∈ Hom(K, ℚ̄)` let `v(τ)` be the place of `K` above `p` induced by `ι_p ∘ τ`. Let `m : Hom(K, ℚ̄) → ℤ` satisfy `m(c∘τ) + m(τ) = w` for all `c ∈ C` and `τ`, and assume `m(τ)` depends only on `v(τ)`. Then for every friendly place `v` above `p, m(τ) = w/2` whenever `v(τ) = v`.

*Proof sketch.* If `K` has no CM subfield, 1.6 gives `m ≡ w/2`. Otherwise fix `τ₀` with `v(τ₀) = v`. Friendliness gives `δ` in the decomposition group of `ι_p` with `δ ∘ τ₀ = τ₀ ∘ s_{E_K}` on `E_K`; then `v(δ ∘ τ₀) = v`. For any `c ∈ C, c ∘ τ₀ = τ₀ ∘ s_{E_K}` on `E_K` (1.3), so `δ ∘ τ₀` and `c ∘ τ₀` agree on `E_K` and `m(δτ₀) = m(cτ₀)` by 1.7. Place-constancy gives `m(δτ₀) = m(τ₀)`; hence `m(τ₀) = m(cτ₀) = w − m(τ₀)`. (LV20, §2.4, proof of Lemma 2.8, pp. 12–13.) *Needs:* 1.3; 1.4; 1.5; 1.7.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ(i), p ≡ 3 mod 4`: the unique place above `p` is friendly and the conclusion is `m ≡ w/2` on both embeddings.
- `K = ℚ(i), p ≡ 1 mod 4`: the two places above `p` may carry exponents `(1, 0)` — the CM elliptic curve — so friendliness is necessary.

### 1.10 Global purity forces the conjugation relation on Hodge–Tate exponents

**Lemma.** Let `K` be a number field, `p` a prime, `ι_p : ℚ̄ → ℚ̄_p` an embedding, and `η : G_K → ℚ_p^×` a continuous character that is unramified outside a finite set `T` of finite places containing those above `p`, pure of weight `w` outside `T`, and de Rham at every place `v' | p` with filtration jump `k_{v'}`. For `τ ∈ Hom(K, ℚ̄)` put `m(τ) := k_{v(τ)}`. Then `m(c ∘ τ) + m(τ) = w` for every complex conjugation `c ∈ C` and every `τ`.

*Proof sketch.* By class field theory `η ∘ Art_K` is a continuous character of the idele class group, trivial on principal ideles, whose component at `℘ ∉ T` is unramified with `η(Art_℘(ϖ_℘)) = η(Frob_℘)`. By local algebraicity there is an open subgroup `U ⊆ ∏_{v'|p} 𝒪_{v'}^×` on which the `p`-component equals `x ↦ ∏_{v'|p} N_{K_{v'}/ℚ_p}(x_{v'})^{k_{v'}} = ∏_τ ι_p(τ(x))^{m(τ)}` for `x ∈ K`. Let `Λ ⊆ K^×` be the subgroup of totally positive `α` with `α ∈ U` and `α` a unit in the kernel of `η_℘` at every `℘ ∈ T` above no p. Reciprocity gives `∏_τ τ(α)^{m(τ)} = ∏_{℘∉T} η(Frob_℘)^{−v_℘(α)}` for `α ∈ Λ`, an algebraic number. At a place of residue characteristic `ℓ≠p`, the local units have a pro-`ℓ` subgroup of finite index, whose continuous image in `ℚ_p^×` is finite; thus those local kernels are open. The intersection of `Λ` with the global units consequently has finite index in the unit group, whereas `Λ` need not have finite index in `K^×`.
