The full-monodromy statement is LV20 Lemma 3.3 with density. `(c)` Apply `(a)` to `Z' = pr₁^{-1}(B₁)`: if `Φ_v(Ω_v) ⊆ pr₁^{-1}(B₁)`, then `pr₁Φ_v(Ω_v) ⊆ B₁` and `Z₁ ⊆ B₁`, contradicting `dim B₁ < dim Z₁`. Under full monodromy the projection of a dense set is dense (3.9). (LV20, §3.4, after Lemma 3.3, p. 19; §1.2, p. 3.) *Needs:* 3.9; 3.14; 3.18; RestrictedPowerSeries §2.4 (Strassmann bound); Mathlib `MvPowerSeries.IsRestricted.subring`; Mathlib `MvPowerSeries.IsRestricted`.

**Checks.**

- Legendre family, `Z' = {a point}`: only finitely many `λ` in a residue disk have `Φ_v(λ)` equal to a given line.
- The hypothesis `m = 1` is needed: for `m ≥ 2` the preimage of a proper closed subset is in general an analytic subset of positive dimension.

### Examples

- A symplectic plane gives `LGr = ℙ¹`; the zero space gives `Spec k`.
- For `E = k × k` and `V = E²` with the standard form, `LGr_E(V, ω) = ℙ¹ × ℙ¹`; the `E`-stable subspace
  `k² × 0` has half the total dimension but component ranks `(2, 0)` and is not a point of the
  componentwise-rank-one Grassmannian.
- The Legendre family has algebraic monodromy `SL₂ = Sp₂` and a nonconstant period map, while a constant
  family has trivial monodromy and constant period maps.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.2; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §§1.3, 1.7; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; ComplexComparisonPartII §C0.4; RestrictedPowerSeries §2.4 (Strassmann bound); Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.4: The finiteness criterion on a residue disk

For a point of the residue disk the crystalline comparison and transport identify each local
representation `ρ_{y', w}` with the fixed `φ`-module at the base point equipped with the filtration given
by the period map. Two filtrations give isomorphic filtered `φ`-modules exactly when they differ by a unit
of the Frobenius centralizer, and these orbits have dimension at most `dim_{K_v} Z(φ^{f_v}) ≤ 4d²`. When
this is smaller than the dimension of the closure of the period image, only finitely many points of the
disk can have local representations in finitely many isomorphism classes. With Faltings's finiteness
lemma this proves Lawrence–Venkatesh's Proposition 3.4 for abelian schemes.

### 4.1 The fibre representations are crystalline with the de Rham cohomology of the fibre as filtered `φ`-module

**Comparison.** In setting `P`, for `y ∈ Y(K) ∩ Ω_v` (such a point extends to an `𝒪_{S_y}`-point of `𝒴` for a finite set `S_y ⊇ S` of places not containing `v`; `S_y = S` when `y ∈ 𝒴(𝒪)`) and a closed point `y'` of `π^{-1}(y)` with residue field `K(y'), X_{y'}` is an abelian variety over `K(y')` with good reduction at the places not above `S_y`, in particular at the places above `v`, and `ρ_{y'} := H¹_et(X_{y'} ×_{K(y')} K̄, ℚ_p)` is a representation of `G_{K(y')}`; for a place `w` of `K(y')` above `v, K(y')_w` is unramified over `K_v`, and `ρ_{y',w}` is the restriction of `ρ_{y'}` to `G_{K(y')_w}`. Then `ρ_{y',w}` is crystalline, and there is an isomorphism of filtered `φ`-modules over `L := K(y')_w, D_cris(ρ_{y',w}) ≅ (H¹_dR(X_{y'} ⊗ L / L), φ_{y',w}, F¹)`, where `D_cris(V) = (V ⊗_{ℚ_p} B_cris)^{G_L}` is the covariant functor, `φ_{y',w}` is the crystalline Frobenius of the good-reduction model `𝒳_{y'} ⊗ 𝒪_L` transported through the Berthelot–Ogus isomorphism (the `(y', w)`-factor of `φ_y` from 2.11), and `F¹` is the Hodge filtration (jumps 0 and 1). The isomorphism is functorial in the abelian scheme and compatible with the polarization pairings.

*Proof sketch.* Since `y` lies in the residue disk it is a point of `𝒴(𝒪_v) ∩ Y(K) = 𝒴(𝒪_(v))` (`𝒴` separated), so it spreads out to `𝒴(𝒪_{S_y})`; the fibre of the good model over the `𝒪_L`-point induced by `y'` is an abelian scheme `𝒳_{y'} ⊗ 𝒪_L` over `𝒪_L = W(k_w)` with generic fibre `X_{y'} ⊗ L` (2.2). The crystalline comparison for its `p`-adic completion (PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2) gives a `G_L`-equivariant `B_cris`-linear isomorphism `H¹_et ⊗ B_cris ≅ H¹_cris(𝒳_{k_w}/W(k_w)) ⊗ B_cris` compatible with Frobenius and filtrations; the étale cohomology of the rigid generic fibre is that of the algebraic variety `X_{y'} ⊗ L`, and restricting `ρ_{y'}` to `G_L` computes `H¹_et((X_{y'} ⊗ L)_{L̄}, ℚ_p)`. Taking `G_L`-invariants with `B_cris^{G_L} = W(k_w)[1/p] = L` gives `D_cris(ρ_{y',w}) ≅ H¹_cris(𝒳_{k_w}/W(k_w))[1/p]` with its Frobenius, and `dim_L D_cris = 2d`, so `ρ_{y',w}` is crystalline. Because `L` is unramified, `D_cris(ρ)_L = D_dR(ρ)` as filtered spaces (Brinon–Conrad Proposition 9.1.9), and the comparison identifies `D_dR(ρ_{y',w})` with `H¹_dR(X_{y'} ⊗ L/L)` and its Hodge filtration; under this identification the crystalline Frobenius is the Berthelot–Ogus transport of 2.11 (PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2). (LV20, §3.5, p. 19; BC09, §9.1, Proposition 9.1.9, p. 133.) *Needs:* 1.16; 2.2; 2.11; PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2; PadicHodgeTheory §8.3; CrystallineCohomology §CR.2.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.2.1–F.2.2, `F`.4; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- A good-reduction elliptic curve `E` over `ℚ_p: D_cris(H¹_et(E_{ℚ̄_p}, ℚ_p)) = H¹_dR(E/ℚ_p)` with `φ` of characteristic polynomial `X² − a_pX + p` and `F¹ = H⁰(E, Ω¹)`, a line.
- For the Tate twist `ℚ_p(−1) = H²_et(ℙ¹): D_cris` is one-dimensional with `φ = p` and the filtration jump in degree 1.

### 4.2 The filtered `φ`-module of a fibre read off from the period map (LV20 (6.6)–(6.7))

**Lemma.** In setting `P`, for `y ∈ Y(K) ∩ Ω_v` (such a point extends to an `𝒪_{S_y}`-point of `𝒴` for a finite set `S_y ⊇ S` of places not containing `v`; `S_y = S` when `y ∈ 𝒴(𝒪)`) and a closed point `y'` of `π^{-1}(y)` with residue field `K(y'), X_{y'}` is an abelian variety over `K(y')` with good reduction at the places not above `S_y`, in particular at the places above `v`, and `ρ_{y'} := H¹_et(X_{y'} ×_{K(y')} K̄, ℚ_p)` is a representation of `G_{K(y')}`; for a place `w` of `K(y')` above `v, K(y')_w` is unramified over `K_v`, and `ρ_{y',w}` is the restriction of `ρ_{y'}` to `G_{K(y')_w}`. Let `(y₀', w₀)` be the pair over `(y₀, v)` corresponding to `(y', w)` under the transport bijection of 2.9, `τ : K(y')_w ≅ K(y₀')_{w₀} =: L₀` the transported field isomorphism, `V₀` the `(y₀', w₀)`-factor of `V_v = ℋ_{y₀} ⊗ K_v` with the restriction `φ₀` of `φ_{y₀}` (`σ_{L₀}`-semilinear), and `pr₀ : H_v → H₀ := LGr_{L₀}(V₀, ω₀)` the factor projection. Then `pr₀Φ_v(y) = T_v(y)^{-1}(F¹H¹_dR(X_{y'} ⊗ K(y')_w))` (LV20 (6.7)), and `D_cris(ρ_{y',w})` transported along `τ` is isomorphic in `MF^φ_{L₀}` to `(V₀, φ₀, pr₀Φ_v(y))`.

*Proof sketch.* The transport `T_v(y)` is semilinear over the ring isomorphism `E₀ ⊗ K_v ≅ E_y ⊗ K_v`, which is the product of the field isomorphisms of the corresponding factors; hence it maps the factor `V₀ τ^{-1}`-semilinearly onto the factor `H¹_dR(X_{y'} ⊗ K(y')_w)` of `ℋ_y ⊗ K_v` and commutes with the factor projections of the period varieties. `Φ_v(y) = T_v(y)^{-1}(F¹ℋ_y ⊗ K_v)` and `F¹` is the product of the factorwise Hodge filtrations, which gives (6.7). `T_v(y)` commutes with Frobenius (2.11), and `τ` commutes with the absolute Frobenii because both fields are unramified over `ℚ_p`; so `T_v(y)` restricted to `V₀` is an isomorphism of filtered `φ`-modules from `(V₀, φ₀, pr₀Φ_v(y))` to the `τ`-transport of `(H¹_dR(X_{y'} ⊗ K(y')_w), φ_{y',w}, F¹)`. Compose with the fibre comparison (4.1). (LV20, §6, (6.7), p. 30; §6, proof of Lemma 6.2, p. 31; §3.5, p. 19.) *Needs:* 2.9; 2.11; 3.8; 3.12; 4.1; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4.

**Checks.**

- `π = id: y' = y, E₀ = K`, and the lemma says that `T_v(y)` is an isomorphism `(V_v, φ_{y₀}, Φ_v(y)) ≅ D_cris(ρ_y|G_{K_v})` (LV20 §3.5).
- The Legendre variant family of LV20 §4.2 with `v` inert in `K(t₀^{1/m})`: one factor, `L₀ = K_v(t₀^{1/m})` of degree `m` over `K_v`.

### 4.3 Filtrations with isomorphic filtered `φ`-modules form a Frobenius-centralizer orbit of bounded dimension

**Lemma.** Let `K_v/ℚ_p` be finite unramified of degree `f_v, L ⊇ K_v` finite unramified, `W` a finite-dimensional `L`-vector space with a `σ_L`-semilinear bijection `φ`, and `F, F' L`-subspaces of `W` of the same dimension `k`, viewed as two-step filtrations `W ⊇ F ⊇ 0`. `(a) (W, φ, F) ≅ (W, φ, F')` in `MF^φ_L` if and only if `F' = gF` for some `g ∈ Z(φ)^×`. `(b)` Let `A = Z(φ^{f_v})`, a `K_v`-subalgebra of `End_L(W)` with `dim_{K_v} A ≤ (dim_L W)²`, and `G` its `K_v`-group of units, which acts on the `K_v`-variety `Gr_L(W, k)` of `L`-stable subspaces. Then `Z(φ)^× ⊆ G(K_v)`, so every `F'` as in `(a)` lies in `G(K_v)·F`, and the Zariski closure of `G(K_v)·F` in `Gr_L(W, k)` has dimension `≤ dim_{K_v} A`. `(c)` Consequently, for filtrations `F_1, …, F_s`, the set of `F'` with `(W, φ, F')` isomorphic to one of the `(W, φ, F_i)` is contained in a closed subset of `Gr_L(W, k)` of dimension `≤ dim_{K_v} A`.

*Proof sketch.* `(a)` An isomorphism `(W, φ, F) → (W, φ, F')` is an `L`-linear bijection `g` commuting with `φ` and carrying `F` onto `F'`. `(b) Z(φ) ⊆ Z(φ^{f_v})` and the dimension bound are LV20 Lemma 2.1 in the unramified form 0.6; elements of `A` are `L`-linear, so `G` acts on the `L`-stable Grassmannian; the Zariski closure of an orbit of a `K_v`-group has dimension at most the dimension of the group (orbit morphism, ReductiveGroups Layer 3). `(c)` A finite union of closed sets of dimension `≤ dim A`. (LV20, §3.5, proof of Proposition 3.4, pp. 19–20; §6, proof of Lemma 6.2, p. 31; BC09, §7.3, Definition 7.3.4, p. 98.) *Needs:* 0.3; 0.5; 0.6; 0.18; 3.8; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- For a good-reduction elliptic curve over `ℚ_p`, the distinct-root Frobenius has centralizer dimension 2, exceeding `dim ℙ¹=1`. `A` Frobenius eigenline is fixed by that centralizer; a non-eigenline has an open orbit. Thus openness of the Hodge-line orbit requires the additional non-eigenline hypothesis.
- `K_v = ℚ_{p²}` and `E/ℚ_p` supersingular with `a_p = 0: φ² = −p` is scalar and `dim_{K_v} Z(φ²) = 4`; if `E` is ordinary, `φ²` has distinct eigenvalues and `dim_{K_v} Z(φ²) = 2`.

### 4.4 Finiteness criterion on a residue disk of a curve

**Theorem.** In setting `P`, for `y ∈ Y(K) ∩ Ω_v` (such a point extends to an `𝒪_{S_y}`-point of `𝒴` for a finite set `S_y ⊇ S` of places not containing `v`; `S_y = S` when `y ∈ 𝒴(𝒪)`) and a closed point `y'` of `π^{-1}(y)` with residue field `K(y'), X_{y'}` is an abelian variety over `K(y')` with good reduction at the places not above `S_y`, in particular at the places above `v`, and `ρ_{y'} := H¹_et(X_{y'} ×_{K(y')} K̄, ℚ_p)` is a representation of `G_{K(y')}`; for a place `w` of `K(y')` above `v, K(y')_w` is unramified over `K_v`, and `ρ_{y',w}` is the restriction of `ρ_{y'}` to `G_{K(y')_w}`. Assume `m = 1`. Fix `(y₀', w₀)` over `(y₀, v)`, put `L₀ := K(y₀')_{w₀}`, let `V₀, φ₀, H₀` and `pr₀` be as in 4.2, `Z₀` the Zariski closure of `pr₀Φ_v(Ω_v)` in `H₀`, and `c := dim_{K_v} Z(φ₀^{f_v})` (so `c ≤ 4d²`). Let `𝒮 ⊆ Y(K) ∩ Ω_v` be a set such that, for `y ∈ 𝒮` and `(y', w)` the pair corresponding to `(y₀', w₀)`, the pairs `(K(y')_w, ρ_{y',w})` lie in finitely many isomorphism classes (an isomorphism of pairs is a field isomorphism with a compatible isomorphism of representations). If `c < dim Z₀`, then `𝒮` is finite. If `Y(ℂ)` is connected and the family has full monodromy, then `dim Z₀ = [L₀ : K_v]·d(d+1)/2`, so the hypothesis `c < dim Z₀` holds whenever `[L₀ : K_v] ≥ 8`. Assume `d ≥ 1` throughout this criterion.

*Proof sketch.* Transport of structure: an isomorphism of pairs `(α, ρ₁ ≅ ρ₂)` induces `D_cris(ρ₂) ≅ α_*D_cris(ρ₁)`. After transport to `L₀` by the field isomorphisms `τ` of 4.2, two points of `𝒮` in the same class of pairs give filtered `φ`-modules over `L₀` that differ by transport along an element of `Aut(L₀/ℚ_p)`, a group of order `[L₀ : ℚ_p]`. So the modules `(V₀, φ₀, pr₀Φ_v(y)), y ∈ 𝒮`, lie in finitely many isomorphism classes of `MF^φ_{L₀}`. By 4.3 there are `F_1, …, F_s` with `pr₀Φ_v(y) ∈ ⋃ G(K_v)·F_i` for all `y ∈ 𝒮`, and `B₀ := H₀ ∩ ⋃` closure`(G(K_v)·F_i)` is closed of dimension `≤ c < dim Z₀`. By 3.19 `(c), {y ∈ Ω_v : pr₀Φ_v(y) ∈ B₀}` is finite, and it contains `𝒮`. Under full monodromy `Z₀ = H₀`, which has dimension `[L₀ : K_v]·d(d+1)/2` by 3.9; for `[L₀ : K_v] ≥ 8` this is `≥ 4d(d+1) > 4d² ≥ c`. (LV20, §3.5, proof of Proposition 3.4, p. 20; §6, proof of Lemma 6.2, p. 31.) *Needs:* 0.6; 2.9; 3.9; 3.18; 3.19; 4.2; 4.3.

**Checks.**

- The variant Legendre family of LV20 §4 (`d = 1, L₀` of degree `m ≥ 8`): `c ≤ 4 < m = dim Z₀`.
- `d = 1` and `L₀ = K_v` (the Legendre family itself): `c = 2 > 1 = dim Z₀`, and the criterion does not apply (LV20 §1.3).

### 4.5 Fibres with good reduction and semisimple Galois representation (LV20 Proposition 3.4, for curves)

**Theorem.** In setting `P`, assume `n = 1` (`π = id`, so `X → Y` is a polarized abelian scheme, `E₀ = K` and `H = LGr(V, ω₀)`), `m = 1` and `Y(ℂ)` connected, and write `ρ_y = H¹_et(X_y ×_K K̄, ℚ_p)`. If `dim_{K_v} Z(φ_{y₀}^{f_v}) < dim_ℂ(Γ·h₀)` (LV20 (3.13)), then the set `{y ∈ 𝒴(𝒪) : y ≡ y₀ mod v, ρ_y semisimple}` is finite.

*Proof sketch.* For `y ∈ 𝒴(𝒪), X_y` has good reduction outside `S`, so `ρ_y` has dimension `2d`, is unramified outside `T := S ∪ {places above p}` and pure of weight 1 with integral Frobenius polynomials outside `T` (Layer LV.1). Faltings's finiteness lemma (Layer LV.1) leaves finitely many isomorphism classes of semisimple `ρ_y`, hence finitely many classes of pairs `(K_v, ρ_y|G_{K_v})`. With `(y₀', w₀) = (y₀, v), Z₀` is the closure `Z_v` of `Φ_v(Ω_v)`, and `dim Z_v ≥ dim(Γ·h₀)` by LV20 Lemma 3.3; so `c < dim Z₀` and the finiteness criterion applies. (LV20, §3.5, Proposition 3.4, pp. 19–20; §3.5, proof of Proposition 3.4, p. 20; §1.3, p. 4.) *Needs:* 1.2; 1.16; 3.18; 4.4; NeronModelsAndSemistableAbelianVarieties §5.1.

**Checks.**

- The Legendre family over `K` with `K_v = ℚ_p: dim Z(φ) = 2 > 1 = dim(Γ·h₀) = dim ℙ¹`, so (3.13) fails; this is why Lawrence and Venkatesh pass to abelian-by-finite families.
- `π = id, K_v = ℚ_p` and `φ_{y₀}` with `2d` distinct eigenvalues: `c = 2d`, so under full monodromy (3.13) holds exactly when `d(d+1)/2 > 2d`, i.e. `d ≥ 4`.

### Examples

- For `d = 1` and a local factor of degree `r` over `K_v`, the centralizer bound is `4` and the period
  variety has dimension `r`, so the criterion needs `r > 4`; for general `d` the bound `4d²` against
  `r·d(d+1)/2` is beaten as soon as `r ≥ 8`.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.1, LV.2, LV.3; PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; PadicHodgeTheory §§8.1–8.2; PadicHodgeTheory §8.3; CrystallineCohomology §CR.2.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.2.1–F.2.2, `F`.4; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; NeronModelsAndSemistableAbelianVarieties §5.1; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.5: Surfaces, mapping class groups and lifted twists

The monodromy theorem for the Kodaira–Parshin family is a statement about the mapping class group of a
punctured surface acting on the homology of its finite covers, so this layer builds the surface topology
it needs. Surfaces carry their topology and orientation as data; classification, genus and homology are
theorems about them. The mapping class group is the group of isotopy classes of orientation-preserving
homeomorphisms fixing the boundary pointwise; Dehn twists act on homology by transvections; pushing a
marked point around a loop gives the Birman exact sequence; capping boundary circles and the surjectivity
of the symplectic representation give the integral realization results used for normal forms. The last
targets lift powers of Dehn twists and point-pushing maps to finite covers and identify the monodromy of a
family of branched covers over a configuration space with lifted point-pushing.

### 5.1 Surfaces with boundary and punctures

**Definition.** A surface is `Σ ∖ P`, where `Σ` is a compact connected oriented Hausdorff second-countable topological 2-manifold with boundary and `P` is a finite subset of its interior. Boundary charts and orientation are actual topological data; genus, Euler characteristic, integral homology and intersection perfectness are consequences, not fields of this structure.

- `Surface`: A surface as a punctured compact connected oriented 2-manifold with boundary.
- `Surface.numPunctures`: The number `n` of punctures.
- `Surface.cut`: The surface `S_α` obtained by cutting along a simple closed curve or proper arc, with its gluing map (GeometricTopology Layer 1).
- `Surface.examples`: Closed surfaces, the thrice- and four-times-punctured spheres, the torus with one boundary circle.

*Construction.* Form the complement of `P` with its subspace topology and induced oriented manifold-with-boundary atlas. No classification or homology theorem is used to define a surface. (LV20, §8.1, p. 39; FM12, §1.1, p. 18.)

**Checks.**

- The compact annulus with `P` empty is a surface with two boundary components.
- Removing one interior point from an oriented compact torus gives the once-punctured torus with its induced orientation.
- The real projective plane admits no orientation and is excluded.
- The definition includes closed oriented surfaces, annuli and punctured surfaces; their type and homology are provided separately.

### 5.2 The Jordan–Schönflies theorem

**Theorem.** Every homeomorphism between two simple closed curves in the plane `ℝ²` extends to a homeomorphism of `ℝ²`. In particular the closure of the bounded complementary component of a simple closed curve is homeomorphic to the closed disk (`schoenflies_extension`). The signature uses embeddings of Mathlib’s `Circle` into the real plane, their range homeomorphism, and its extension to a plane homeomorphism.

*Proof sketch.* Use the Jordan curve theorem for the two complementary components, then extend the homeomorphism over the bounded component by a sequence of chords dividing it into small disks (Thomassen’s graph-theoretic proof) or by homology and the Riemann mapping theorem with Carathéodory extension. (GX13, Appendix `E`, Theorem `E`.1, p. 160.)

### 5.3 Every compact surface can be triangulated (Radó)

**Theorem.** Every compact connected topological surface without boundary is homeomorphic to the geometric realization of a finite two-dimensional simplicial complex in which every edge lies in exactly two triangles and the link of every vertex is a circle. Every compact connected surface `S` with boundary admits such a triangulation (edges in one or two triangles, vertex links circles or arcs) in which `∂S` is a subcomplex.

*Proof sketch.* Closed case: cover the surface by finitely many closed quadrilaterals in charts and use Jordan–Schönflies to modify them until any two meet in finitely many arcs and points; the resulting graph subdivides the surface into disks, which are triangulated. Boundary case: `∂S` has a collar (Brown’s collaring theorem; locally the half-plane charts are collars), so capping each boundary circle by a disk gives a closed surface `Ŝ` in which the capping circles are tame; run the closed-case argument with these circles in the graph, and remove the open capping disks. (GX13, Appendix `E`, Theorem `E`.3 and its proof, pp. 161–163.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.2; Tau Ceti `IsLocallyCollared.isCollared` (Brown’s collaring theorem, pinned library).

### 5.4 Classification of compact surfaces

**Theorem.** Every closed connected orientable surface is homeomorphic to the connected sum of a sphere with `g ≥ 0` tori, for a unique `g`; every compact connected orientable surface is obtained from a closed one by removing `b ≥ 0` open disks with disjoint closures, and the pair `(g, b)` determines it up to homeomorphism. Consequently two surfaces (in the sense of 5.1) are homeomorphic by an orientation-preserving homeomorphism if and only if they have the same type `(g, b, n)`.

*Proof sketch.* For a closed compact topological surface use Gallier–Xu Appendix `E`: finite coordinate disks; straighten the finite boundary graph using plane Jordan–Schönflies; subdivide polygonal faces to obtain a finite triangulation. Boundary surfaces require collars and a compatible extension (5.7). View the triangulation as a finite polygon cell complex. Elementary operations P1 (subdivide an edge) and P2 (split a face along a new edge), and their inverses, preserve the realized surface. GX13 Lemma 6.1 eliminates adjacent `aa⁻¹`, reduces inner vertices and merges faces. Isolate one boundary contour at a time. Pair consistently oriented sides into commutators (handles); inconsistent pairs form crosscaps. In the oriented case only the handle form remains. Compute `χ=2−2g−b` for the resulting handle polygon. Boundary count and `χ` determine `g`, so the normal form is unique up to homeomorphism. Punctures are the removed marked points, not additional boundary charts. (FM12, §1.1, Theorem 1.1, p. 18; GX13, Lemma 6.1 and Theorem 6.1, pp. 92–98; Appendix `E`, Theorem `E`.3, pp. 161–163.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.3; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 4.

**Checks.**

- The torus has genus 1 and `H₁ ≅ ℤ²`.
- A compact Riemann surface is a closed orientable surface, and its genus is half the rank of `H₁`.

### 5.5 Duality and the intersection form on a compact oriented surface

Compared with AlgebraicTopology Stage 6’s smooth-manifold formulation, this target gives the same duality for topological surfaces via a triangulation, and the explicit integral intersection form used by lifted twists.

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a compact oriented topological surface with boundary `∂S`, with fundamental class `[S] ∈ H₂(S, ∂S; ℤ)`. Cap product with `[S]` gives isomorphisms `H^k(S; ℤ) ≅ H_{2−k}(S, ∂S; ℤ)` and `H^k(S, ∂S; ℤ) ≅ H_{2−k}(S; ℤ)`. The intersection form `î : H₁(S; ℤ) × H₁(S; ℤ) → ℤ, î(x, y) = ⟨D_rel^{−1}(x) ∪ D_abs^{−1}(j y), [S]⟩`, where `D_rel : H¹(S,∂S)→H₁(S), D_abs : H¹(S)→H₁(S,∂S)` and `j : H₁(S)→H₁(S,∂S)`, is bilinear and alternating, its radical is the image of `H₁(∂S)`, it is perfect when `∂S = ∅, H₁(S; ℤ) ≅ Hom(H₁(S, ∂S; ℤ), ℤ)`, and it is invariant under orientation-preserving homeomorphisms. For transverse oriented simple closed curves it is the signed count of intersection points.

*Proof sketch.* Lefschetz duality for compact `R`-oriented manifolds with boundary (cap product with the relative fundamental class) and the universal coefficient theorem, using that `H₀` and `H₁` of a surface are free. Alternation is graded commutativity of cup product in degree one. Naturality of cap products gives invariance under orientation-preserving homeomorphisms. The geometric count follows from the local computation at a transverse intersection. (Hat02, Theorem 3.43, p. 254; Theorem 3.30, p. 241; Proposition 3.38, p. 250.) *Needs:* 5.1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6.

### 5.6 Homology, type and intersection form of an oriented surface

**Lemma.** For an oriented surface `Σ ∖ P` of type `(g,b,n), χ = 2−2g−b−n`. If `b+n=0, H₁(−;ℤ) ≃ ℤ^(2g)`; otherwise `H₁ ≃ ℤ^(2g+b+n−1)`. The intersection form has a standard unimodular symplectic rank-`2g` summand and a radical of rank `max(b+n−1,0)`; in particular it is perfect when `b+n≤1`.

- `Surface.genus`: The genus `g`.
- `Surface.numBoundary`: The number `b` of boundary circles.
- `Surface.eulerChar_eq`: `χ(S) = 2 − 2g − b − n`, computed from singular homology.
- `Surface.intersectionForm`: The algebraic intersection form `î` on `H₁(S; ℤ)`, alternating.
- `Surface.intersectionForm_perfect`: `î` is perfect when `b + n ≤ 1`.

*Proof sketch.* Apply classification to the capped compact surface to define genus and boundary count; choose its finite polygon/CW model. The cellular chain computation gives the handle basis and the boundary/peripheral generators, with their single sum relation when there are boundary components or punctures. Apply Poincaré–Lefschetz duality. Handles intersect in standard pairs; boundary and peripheral loops form the radical. Removing punctures is modeled by replacing each with a small boundary circle. (FM12, §1.1, Theorem 1.1, p. 18.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.4; 5.5; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 4; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6.

**Checks.**

- Annulus: `H₁=ℤ`, zero intersection form, `χ=0`.
- Once-punctured torus: `H₁=ℤ²` with a perfect alternating form, `χ=−1`.
- Thrice-punctured sphere: `H₁=ℤ²`, zero form, `χ=−1`.

### 5.7 Annular neighbourhoods and cutting

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a compact oriented topological surface. `(i)` Every simple closed curve `α` in the interior of `S` has a closed regular neighbourhood `N` with an orientation-preserving homeomorphism `S¹ × [−1, 1] → N` carrying `S¹ × {0}` onto `α`; any two such are isotopic relative to `α`. (ii) Cutting `S` along `α` yields a compact surface `S_α` with two new boundary circles and a quotient map `S_α → S` identifying them; homeomorphisms of `S` preserving `α` lift to `S_α`. Conversely a homeomorphism of `S_α` descends precisely when it preserves the pairs identified by the gluing map (it may exchange the two new boundary circles).

*Proof sketch.* Triangulate `S` (Radó) and move `α` by a small isotopy into the 1-skeleton of a subdivision (Schönflies in the charts); the closed star of `α` in the second barycentric subdivision is a regular neighbourhood, an annulus because `S` is orientable (a Möbius band would be nonorientable). Uniqueness of regular neighbourhoods up to isotopy gives the isotopy statement. Cutting removes the interior of `N` and reattaches two boundary copies of `α`. (FM12, §1.3, p. 38; §3.1.1, pp. 67–68; GX13, Appendix `E`, p. 163.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.3; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1.

### 5.8 Oriented simple closed curves

**Definition.** An oriented simple closed curve in `S` is an embedding `S¹→int(S)`, up to ambient isotopy and orientation-preserving reparameterization. Its class in integral `H₁` is induced by the standard orientation of `S¹`. Separating means that the complement of its image is disconnected. Essential/nonperipheral is a separate predicate: a disk-bounding embedded circle is still a simple closed curve.

- `SimpleClosedCurve`: The oriented embedding/isotopy quotient, without an essentialness condition.
- `SimpleClosedCurve.homologyClass`: The induced oriented integral `H₁` class.
- `SimpleClosedCurve.IsSeparating`: The complement of the image is disconnected.
- `SimpleClosedCurve.reverse`: Reverse the parameter orientation; the homology class changes sign and separation is unchanged.
- `SimpleClosedCurve.map`: Map by an orientation-preserving homeomorphism, compatibly with `H₁` and separation.
- `SimpleClosedCurve.cut`: Use the topological collar to cut along the image, with its boundary and gluing data.

*Construction.* Use topological embeddings of the oriented circle. Form the isotopy quotient using the annular neighbourhoods of 5.7, and show its image, separation and homology class are well-defined. (LV20, §8.3, p. 42.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.6; 5.7.

**Checks.**

- A horizontal circle on the oriented torus is nonseparating and represents a primitive `H₁` class.
- The boundary of a small disk is a separating simple closed curve with homology class zero; it is inessential, not excluded from the definition.
- A figure-eight immersion is not an embedding and does not define a simple closed curve.
- Orientation reversal and ambient isotopy have the stated effects; self-intersection excludes an immersion.

### 5.9 The mapping class group

**Definition.** For a surface `S, Mod(S)` is the group of isotopy classes of orientation-preserving homeomorphisms of `S` that restrict to the identity on `∂S`, isotopies being required to fix `∂S` pointwise; homeomorphisms may permute the punctures. For `x` in the interior, `S*` denotes `S` with the marked point `x` (equivalently `S ∖ {x}` with the new puncture fixed), and `Mod(S*)` the corresponding group of classes fixing `x`. `Mod(S)` acts on `H₁(S; ℤ)` preserving `î` (the symplectic representation `Ψ`), on the set of isotopy classes of simple closed curves, and, through `Out(π₁(S))`, on the set of isomorphism classes of finite coverings of `S`.

- `MappingClassGroup`: `Mod(S) = Homeo⁺(S, ∂S)`/isotopy rel `∂S`.
- `MappingClassGroup.marked`: `Mod(S*)` for a marked interior point `x`.
- `MappingClassGroup.homologyRep`: `Ψ : Mod(S) → Aut(H₁(S; ℤ), î)`.
- `MappingClassGroup.actCurves`: The action on isotopy classes of simple closed curves.
- `MappingClassGroup.actCovers`: The action on isomorphism classes of finite coverings of `S` via `Out(π₁ S)`.
- `MappingClassGroup.forget`: `Forget : Mod(S*) → Mod(S)`, filling in the marked point; surjective.
- `MappingClassGroup.examples`: `Mod(D²) = 1` (Alexander lemma) and `Mod`(annulus) `= ℤ`.

*Construction.* Homeomorphisms isotopic relative to `∂S` induce the same map on `H₁`, on isotopy classes of curves and on conjugacy classes of homomorphisms `π₁(S) → G`, so the actions factor through `Mod(S)`. The action on coverings: `f` sends the class of a covering `p : Z → S` to the class of `f ∘ p : Z → S`; in terms of monodromy this is precomposition with the outer automorphism `(f⁻¹)_*` of `π₁(S)`. (FM12, §2.1, printed p. 46 (PDF p. 56); §1.3.2, printed p. 40 (PDF p. 50).) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.4; 5.6; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.CoveringSpace.monodromyEquivalence`; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- The boundary-fixed mapping class group of a disk is trivial.
- The boundary-fixed mapping class group of an annulus is `ℤ`, with its Dehn twist corresponding to 1.
- The mapping class group of the three-punctured sphere allowing permutations is `Sym(3)`; its pure subgroup is trivial. These two groups must not be identified.
- The pure mapping class group of the thrice-punctured sphere is trivial (Farb–Margalit §3.6).
- `Mod` of an annulus is `ℤ`, generated by the twist map.

### 5.10 Change of coordinates for simple closed curves

**Lemma.** Let `S` be a surface. `(a)` Two nonseparating simple closed curves in `S` are related by an orientation-preserving homeomorphism of `S` fixing `∂S` and the punctures. `(b)` Two ordered pairs `(a, b)`, (a', `b'`) of simple closed curves meeting transversally in exactly one point, with the same algebraic intersection sign if orientations on the curves are prescribed, are related by such a homeomorphism. `(c)` If `S` is closed of genus `g`, there is a geometric symplectic basis: simple closed curves `a_1, b_1, …, a_g, b_g` with `a_i ∩ b_i` one transverse point and all other pairs disjoint, whose classes form a symplectic basis of `H₁(S; ℤ)`; any two such systems are related by a homeomorphism. `(d)` Every nonseparating simple closed curve a admits a simple closed curve `b` meeting it transversally once.

*Proof sketch.* Cut `S` along the given curves; the cut surfaces have the same type (Euler characteristic, number of boundary components, punctures and connectivity are determined by the combinatorial data), so by 5.4 they are homeomorphic by a homeomorphism respecting the boundary circles coming from the curves. Glue back: choose the homeomorphism compatibly with the gluing maps on the boundary circles (a homeomorphism of a circle is isotopic to a rotation or a reflection, and orientations are prescribed). `(c)` and `(d)` follow from `(a), (b)` and the standard picture on the connected sum of tori. (FM12, §1.3.2, printed p. 40 (PDF p. 50).) *Needs:* 5.4; 5.7; 5.8; 5.9; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1.

**Checks.**

- On the torus, the curves `(1, 0)` and `(1, k)` are related by `T^k` in the other coordinate.
- A separating and a nonseparating curve are not related.

### 5.11 Dehn twists

**Definition.** For a simple closed curve `α` in a surface `S`, a regular neighbourhood `N ≅ S¹ × [0, 1]` (orientation-preserving) and the twist map `T(θ, t) = (θ + 2πt, t)` of the annulus, the Dehn twist `T_α` is `φ ∘ T ∘ φ⁻¹` on `N` and the identity outside `N`. Its class `T_a ∈ Mod(S)` depends only on the isotopy class a of `α`; `T_{f(a)} = f T_a f⁻¹` for `f ∈ Mod(S)`; twists about disjoint curves commute; `T_a = 1` when a bounds a disk or a once-punctured disk.

- `dehnTwist`: `T_a ∈ Mod(S)` for an isotopy class a of simple closed curves.
- `dehnTwist_conj`: `f T_a f⁻¹ = T_{f(a)}`.
- `dehnTwist_commute`: `T_a T_b = T_b T_a` when a and `b` are disjoint.
- `dehnTwist_eq_one`: `T_a = 1` if a bounds a disk or a once-punctured disk.
- `dehnTwist_support`: `T_α` is supported in any prescribed regular neighbourhood of `α`.
- `multitwist`: `∏ T_{e_i}^{m_i}` for pairwise disjoint curves `e_i`.
- `dehnTwist_torus`: The twists about the two standard curves of the torus.

*Construction.* Well-definedness from the uniqueness of regular neighbourhoods up to isotopy. Conjugation formula: `f T_α f⁻¹` is the twist in the neighbourhood `f(N)` of `f(α)`. Disjoint curves have disjoint neighbourhoods, so the twists commute; a twist in the neighbourhood of a curve bounding a (once-punctured) disk is isotopic to the identity by the Alexander lemma. (FM12, §3.1.1, p. 68.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.6; 5.7; 5.8; 5.9.

**Checks.**

- A twist about a curve bounding a disk or a once-punctured disk is identity in `Mod(S)`.
- A twist about the core of the annulus is not identity when both boundary circles are fixed pointwise.
- For `f` orientation-preserving, `T_{f(a)}=f T_a f⁻¹`, whereas reversing the surface orientation changes the twist direction.
- On the torus with the standard basis, `T_b` acts on `H₁` by the matrix `[[1, 0], [±1, 1]]`.
- The boundary twist of a once-punctured disk is trivial in `Mod`.

### 5.12 Action of Dehn twists and multitwists on homology

**Lemma.** Let `S` be a surface and `b` an oriented simple closed curve. For every `x ∈ H₁(S; ℤ)` and `k ∈ ℤ, Ψ(T_b^k)(x) = x + k·î(x, b)·[b]`. Consequently, for pairwise disjoint oriented simple closed curves `e_1, …, e_r` and integers `m_i`, the multitwist `∏ T_{e_i}^{m_i}` acts by `x ↦ x + Σ_i m_i î(x, e_i)[e_i]`; in particular it is unipotent, and it is the identity on the `î`-orthogonal of span`{[e_i]}`.

*Proof sketch.* The twist is supported in an annulus `N` around `b`. Represent `x` by a cycle transverse to `b`; each transverse crossing of `b` is replaced by an arc that runs once around `N`, which changes the cycle by `±[b]` with the sign of the crossing. Summing over crossings gives `x + î(x, b)[b]` for `k = 1`; iterate, using `î(b, b) = 0`. For disjoint curves the twists commute and `î(e_i, e_j) = 0`, so the formulas compose additively. (FM12, §6.3, Proposition 6.3, p. 176.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.5; 5.6; 5.8; 5.10; 5.11; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 2; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6.

**Checks.**

- On `S_g` with a geometric symplectic basis, `T_{b_1}` sends `a_1` to `a_1 ± b_1` and fixes the other basis vectors (Farb–Margalit Proposition 6.3).
- A separating curve has class in the radical of the intersection form, so its twist acts trivially on `H₁`. Its class is zero on a closed surface; it need not vanish on a surface with several boundary components (the annulus core is a counterexample).

### 5.13 Primitive homology classes are represented by simple closed curves

**Lemma.** Let `g ≥ 1`. A nonzero class of `H₁(S_g; ℤ)` is represented by an oriented simple closed curve if and only if it is primitive; a simple closed curve is nonseparating if and only if its class is nonzero, and then its class is primitive. The same holds on a closed surface with one puncture or with one boundary circle (where `H₁` is identified with that of the closed surface), and every class of `H₁` is a sum of classes of simple closed curves.

*Proof sketch.* Farb–Margalit's topological Euclidean algorithm: write the class in a geometric symplectic basis and reduce the coordinates by twists, which preserve simplicity. A nonseparating curve has a dual curve meeting it once (change of coordinates), so its class pairs to 1 with an integral class and is primitive; a separating curve bounds a subsurface and is null-homologous. One puncture or one boundary circle: the inclusion into the capped surface induces an isomorphism on `H₁` and simple closed curves can be isotoped off the cap. (FM12, §6.2, Proposition 6.2, p. 173.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.6; 5.8; 5.10; 5.12.

**Checks.**

- `(2, 0) ∈ H₁(T²)` is not represented by a simple closed curve; `(2, 3)` is.
- On `S_2` the class `a_1 + a_2` is represented by a simple closed curve.

### 5.14 The homotopy exact sequence of a fibre bundle

**Theorem.** Let `p : E → B` be a map with the homotopy lifting property for disks (for instance a fibre bundle, which has it for all CW pairs), `b₀ ∈ B, F = p^{−1}(b₀)` and `x₀ ∈ F`. Then `p_* : π_n(E, F, x₀) → π_n(B, b₀)` is an isomorphism of groups for `n ≥ 2` and a bijection of pointed sets for `n = 1`, and if `B` is path-connected there is a long exact sequence `⋯ → π_n(F) → π_n(E) → π_n(B) → π_{n−1}(F) → ⋯ → π₀(F) → π₀(E) →` *, whose terms at `π₀` and the boundary from `π₁(B)` are pointed sets, natural in maps of fibrations; in low degrees the boundary map `π₁(B) → π₀(F)` is the action of loops on the fibre.

*Proof sketch.* Lift relative disks along `p` to identify relative homotopy groups of `(E, F)` with those of `B`, then splice into the long exact sequence of the pair `(E, F)` (AlgebraicTopology Stage 8). Local triviality of a bundle gives lifting for CW pairs by induction over cells. (Hat02, Theorem 4.41, p. 376; Proposition 4.48, p. 379.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 8; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5.

### 5.15 Oriented surfaces other than the sphere are aspherical

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a connected oriented surface of finite type (compact, possibly with boundary, minus finitely many interior points) that is not a sphere. Then the universal cover of `S` is contractible; in particular `π_n(S) = 0` for `n ≥ 2, S` is a `K(π₁(S), 1)` and `π₁(S)` is torsion-free.

*Proof sketch.* A closed orientable surface other than `S²` has infinite fundamental group, and its universal cover is a simply connected surface without boundary other than `S²`, hence `ℝ²`, which is contractible. A surface with nonempty boundary or punctures deformation retracts onto a finite graph, whose universal cover is a tree. A finite-dimensional `K(G, 1)` has torsion-free fundamental group. (Hat02, Example `1B`.2, p. 88; Example `1B`.14, p. 93.) *Needs:* 5.4; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- The disk and the plane have contractible universal covers despite positive Euler characteristic.
- `S²` is excluded: `π₂(S²)=ℤ`. Orientation excludes `ℝP²`, whose universal cover is `S²` and whose fundamental group has torsion.

### 5.16 Surface groups of negative Euler characteristic have trivial centre

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a connected surface of finite type with `χ(S) < 0`. Then the centralizer of every nontrivial element of `π₁(S)` is infinite cyclic; in particular `π₁(S)` has trivial centre.

*Proof sketch.* For a closed `S, χ(S)<0` gives a compact hyperbolic metric, so `π₁(S)` acts freely and discretely on `ℍ²`; commuting nontrivial hyperbolic or parabolic isometries share their fixed points at infinity, so the centralizer is a discrete subgroup of a one-parameter group, hence cyclic. For boundary or punctures, `S` retracts onto a finite graph of rank at least two; a nontrivial element acts by translation on an axis in its universal-cover tree, and its centralizer is the cyclic subgroup of translations of that axis. In either case the group is noncyclic, so its centre is trivial. This does not require a complete finite-area metric on a surface with boundary. (FM12, §1.1, Theorem 1.2, p. 21, and the discussion of centralizers, p. 23.) *Needs:* 5.4; 5.15.

### 5.17 The evaluation fibration of the homeomorphism group

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a surface and `x` a point of its interior, and give the group `Homeo⁺(S, ∂S)` of orientation-preserving homeomorphisms fixing `∂S` pointwise the compact-open topology. The evaluation map `E : Homeo⁺(S, ∂S) → int(S), φ ↦ φ(x)`, is a locally trivial fibre bundle with fibre `Homeo⁺(S, ∂S ∪ {x})`. Equivalently, every motion of the marked point along a path in `int(S)` extends to an isotopy of `S` fixed near `∂S`, depending continuously on the path.

*Proof sketch.* For a small disk `U` around a point `y` of `int(S)` choose, continuously in `u ∈ U`, homeomorphisms `φ_u` supported in a slightly larger disk with `φ_u(y) = u` (push the disk radially). Then `(u, ψ) ↦ φ_u ∘ ψ` is a homeomorphism from `U × E^{−1}(y)` onto `E^{−1}(U)`; translate by a homeomorphism taking `x` to `y`. Lifting paths along `E` gives the isotopy extension. (FM12, §4.2.3, proof of Theorem 4.6, pp. 104–105.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.7.

### 5.18 Contractibility of the identity component (Hamstrom)

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a surface of finite type that is not homeomorphic to `S², ℝ²`, the closed disk `D²`, the torus `T²`, the closed annulus, the once-punctured disk or the once-punctured plane. Then the identity component `Homeo₀(S)` of the group of homeomorphisms of `S` fixing `∂S` pointwise is contractible; in particular it is simply connected.

*Proof sketch.* Apply Hamstrom’s theorem in the compact-open topology; its contractibility gives `π₁(Homeo₀)=1`. For the Birman sequence only the `χ(S)<0` cases are used. Teichmüller-space contractibility alone does not prove this assertion about the homeomorphism group. (FM12, §1.4.3, Theorem 1.14, p. 45.) *Needs:* 5.1.

### 5.19 The point-pushing homomorphism

**Construction.** Let `S` be a surface and `x` an interior point. For a loop `α` in `S` based at `x`, extend the motion of `x` along `α` to an isotopy `φ_t` of `S` fixed near `∂S` (isotopy extension for points) and let `Push(α) ∈ Mod(S*)` be the class of `φ_1`, regarded as a homeomorphism of `S` fixing `x`. `Push(α)` depends only on the homotopy class of `α`, and `Push : π₁(S, x) → Mod(S*)` is a homomorphism (with the composition convention fixed by the path-concatenation convention of Mathlib's fundamental group). Its image lies in the kernel of `Forget : Mod(S*) → Mod(S)`, and `Push(α)` acts on `π₁(S, x)` by conjugation by `α`.

Conventions: Mathlib’s `FundamentalGroup.mul_def` makes `α * β` traverse `β` first. `Push(α)` is the endpoint mapping class of an isotopy moving the marked point along `α`; it acts on based loops by `γ ↦ α * γ * α^{−1}`, which traverses `α^{−1}`, then `γ`, then `α`.

- `pointPush`: `Push : π₁(S, x) →* Mod(S*)`.
- `pointPush_apply`: `Push(α)` is the class of the end map of any isotopy extending the motion of `x` along `α`.
- `forget_pointPush`: `Forget (Push α) = 1`.
- `pointPush_action`: With Mathlib multiplication `p*q=q.trans p`, endpoint point-pushing acts on based loops by `γ ↦ α*γ*α⁻¹`; equivalently the path `α⁻¹` followed by `γ` followed by `α`.
- `pointPush_homology`: `Push(α)` acts trivially on `H₁(S)` and on `H₁(S ∖ {x})` when `S` is closed.
- `pointPush_torus`: On the torus `Push` is trivial.

*Construction.* Isotopy extension for a point in the interior of a surface gives `φ_t`. Independence of the choices: the evaluation map Homeo`(S, ∂S) → S ∖ ∂S, f ↦ f(x)`, is a locally trivial fibre bundle with fibre Homeo`(S*, ∂S)`; the connecting map of its homotopy sequence is `Push`, which is therefore well defined on `π₁(S, x)` and a homomorphism (Farb–Margalit §4.2). `φ_1` is isotopic to the identity in `S` by construction, so `Forget(Push(α)) = 1`; tracking the base point along the isotopy shows that `φ_1` acts on `π₁(S, x)` by conjugation by `α`. (FM12, §4.2, Theorem 4.6, p. 102.) *Needs:* 5.9; 5.11; 5.17; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 8; Mathlib `FundamentalGroup.mul_def`.

**Checks.**

- The constant loop pushes to identity.
- Every loop on the torus pushes to identity in its once-marked mapping class group, using the translation isotopy.
- On a closed genus-two surface, pushing a nontrivial simple loop gives a nontrivial mapping class in the forgetful kernel, although its action on `H₁` is identity.
- `S = S_{0,3}`, with `α` oriented so that its left push-off a bounds the old once-punctured disk: `T_a=1` and `Push(α)=T_b⁻¹`, where `b` encloses that puncture and `x`. Reversing `α` gives `T_b`.
- `S` a torus: `Push` is trivial (`π₁` is abelian and the fibration has a section), so the injectivity statement of the Birman sequence needs `χ(S) < 0`.

### 5.20 The Birman exact sequence and pushes of simple loops

**Theorem.** Let `S` be a surface with `χ(S) < 0` and `x` an interior point. Then `1 → π₁(S, x) → Mod(S*) → Mod(S) → 1` (`Push`, then `Forget`) is exact. For a simple loop `α` at `x, Push([α]) = T_a T_b⁻¹`, where `a` and `b` are the simple closed curves in `S ∖ {x}` obtained by pushing `α` off itself to the left and to the right; a and `b` are nonseparating in `S ∖ {x}` if and only if `α` is nonseparating in `S`.

*Proof sketch.* Exactness at `Mod(S*)`: a class in the kernel of `Forget` is isotopic to the identity in `S`, and the track of `x` is a loop `α` whose push is that class (up to inversion); `Forget` is surjective because a homeomorphism can be isotoped to fix `x`. Injectivity: `Push(α)` acts on `π₁(S, x)` by conjugation by `α`, and `π₁(S)` has trivial centre when `χ(S) < 0`. The formula for simple loops: the isotopy pushing `x` once around `α` is supported in the annulus between a and `b`, where it equals `T_a T_b⁻¹`; nonseparation is read off from the complement of the annulus. (FM12, §4.2, Theorem 4.6, p. 102; §4.2, Fact 4.7, p. 103; §4.2, p. 104.) *Needs:* 5.9; 5.11; 5.14; 5.16; 5.17; 5.18; 5.19; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 8; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- `S = S_{0,3}` and `α` a loop around one puncture: a bounds a once-punctured disk, so `Push(α) = T_b⁻¹` with `b` enclosing that puncture and `x`.
- For `S` closed of genus `≥ 2` and `α` nonseparating, `Push(α)` is a nontrivial element acting trivially on `H₁(S)`.

### 5.21 Capping boundary circles and forgetting points are surjective on mapping class groups

**Lemma.** Let `S` be a surface and `S'` the surface obtained by capping a boundary circle `β` with a once-marked disk. Then `1 → ⟨T_β⟩ → Mod(S) → Mod(S', p) → 1` is exact (`Mod(S', p)` fixing the marked point `p`). Consequently, capping all boundary circles with disks induces a surjection `Mod(S) → Mod(Ŝ)` onto the mapping class group of the capped surface, compatible with the maps on `H₁`.

*Proof sketch.* The capping sequence is Farb–Margalit Proposition 3.19. Forgetting the marked points is surjective (Birman exact sequence, right-hand map), and a composite of surjections is surjective. The inclusion `S ⊆ Ŝ` is equivariant, which gives the compatibility on `H₁`. (FM12, §3.6.2, Proposition 3.19, p. 89.) *Needs:* 5.9; 5.14; 5.20.

**Checks.**

- Capping the torus with one boundary circle gives `Mod(T°) → Mod(T²) = SL₂(ℤ)`, surjective.

### 5.22 Surjectivity of the symplectic representation

**Theorem.** For `g ≥ 1` the symplectic representation `Ψ : Mod(S_g) → Sp(2g, ℤ)` is surjective.

*Proof sketch.* By FM12 Proposition 6.3 the twists about curves in a geometric symplectic basis and about the curves `a_i + a_{i+1}` map to symplectic transvections. These transvections generate `Sp(2g, ℤ)` (Farb–Margalit's first proof, via a generating set of `Sp(2g, ℤ)`; the second proof uses transitivity on primitive vectors, FM12 Proposition 6.2). (FM12, §6.3, Theorem 6.4, p. 177.) *Needs:* 5.10; 5.12; 5.13.

**Checks.**

- `g = 1: Mod(T²) ≅ SL₂(ℤ)`, generated by the images of the two standard twists.

### 5.23 The configuration fibration of a surface and its fundamental groups

**Lemma.** Let `S` be a connected oriented surface without boundary and `F(S) := {(x, y) ∈ S × S : x ≠ y}` with `p(x, y) = y`. Then `p` is a locally trivial fibre bundle with fibre `S ∖ {y}`, and for `y₀ ≠ x₀` the sequence `π₁(S ∖ {y₀}, x₀) → π₁(F(S), (x₀, y₀)) → π₁(S, y₀) → 1` (the first map `x ↦ (x, y₀)`) is exact; its image `Γ̄` is normal, and conjugation by `π₁(F(S))` preserves the conjugacy class `c` of a small loop around `y₀` in `S ∖ {y₀}`. For `S = Y` closed of genus `g ≥ 1` the first map is injective. For `S` of genus `g ≥ 1` closed, `π₁(S ∖ {y₀}, x₀)` is free on classes `x_1, x_1', …, x_g, x_g'` such that a small loop around `y₀` is conjugate to `[x_1, x_1'] ⋯ [x_g, x_g']`, and `H₁(S ∖ {y₀}) → H₁(S)` is an isomorphism.

*Proof sketch.* Local triviality: for `y` in a disk `D` around `y₀` choose homeomorphisms `h_y` of `S` supported in a slightly larger disk, continuous in `y`, with `h_y(y₀) = y`; then `(x, y) ↦ (h_y⁻¹ x, y)` trivializes `p` over `D`. A locally trivial bundle over a paracompact base has the homotopy lifting property, which gives exactness of the long exact sequence in low degrees: surjectivity on `π₁` because the fibre is path connected, exactness in the middle by lifting null-homotopies. The monodromy of the bundle along a loop is a homeomorphism of the fibre that is the identity near the end at `y₀` up to isotopy, so it preserves the peripheral class `c`. Injectivity for closed `S` of genus `≥ 1: π₂(S) = 0`, since the universal cover of `S` is a plane (or by the free-group structure of `π₁` of the punctured surface). Presentation: by the classification, `S` is a `4g`-gon with edge word `∏ [a_i, b_i]`; removing an interior point of the 2-cell, the punctured surface deformation retracts onto the wedge of `2g` circles, whose fundamental group is free (van Kampen), and the boundary of the 2-cell reads `∏[x_i, x_i']`. (LV20, §7.3, proof of Lemma 7.4, p. 38; §5, p. 26.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.4; 5.6; 5.14; 5.15; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 8; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- `S = ℂ ∖ {0}: F(S) → S` has fibre `ℂ ∖ {0, y}`, with `π₁` free of rank 2.
- `g = 1: π₁(T² ∖ {pt})` is free on two generators `x, x'` and the puncture loop is `[x, x']`.

### 5.24 Lifting homeomorphisms to covers

This specializes the supplied lifting criterion to invertible maps and fixes the uniqueness and deck-transformation ambiguity needed for mapping class actions.

**Theorem.** Let `p : Z → Y` be a connected finite covering of a connected, locally path-connected, semilocally simply connected space, `y₀ ∈ Y`, with monodromy `ρ : π₁(Y, y₀) → Sym(p^{−1}(y₀))`. A homeomorphism `f` of `Y` with `f(y₀) = y₀` lifts to a homeomorphism of `Z` covering `f` if and only if `ρ ∘ f_*` is conjugate to `ρ` in `Sym(p^{−1}(y₀))` (equivalently `f_*` preserves the conjugacy class of the subgroup `p_*π₁(Z, z₀)`). Two lifts differ by a deck transformation, and if the centralizer of the image of `ρ` in `Sym(p^{−1}(y₀))` is trivial the lift is unique. The same holds for a homeomorphism of `Y` moving `y₀` along a path, after transport along the path, and for compactified branched covers of surfaces away from the branch points.

*Proof sketch.* Apply the lifting criterion to `f ∘ p : Z → Y`, which lifts through `p` exactly when `(f ∘ p)_*π₁(Z) ⊆ p_*π₁(Z)` up to the choice of base point in the fibre, i.e. when `f_*` preserves the conjugacy class; apply it to `f^{−1}` for the inverse. Lifts agreeing at one point agree everywhere, so the lifts form a torsor under the deck group, which is the centralizer of the monodromy image. (Hat02, Proposition 1.33, p. 61; Proposition 1.34, p. 62.) *Needs:* TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.CoveringSpace.monodromyEquivalence`.

### 5.25 Lifting powers of Dehn twists to finite coverings

**Lemma.** Let `S` be a surface, `Z° → S` a connected finite covering of degree `n` with monodromy `Cov : π₁(S, s₀) → Sym(F)` (`F` the fibre), and `e` a simple closed curve in `S`. Let `n_e` be the order of `Cov(e)` and `(d_1, …, d_k)` its cycle type. Then the preimage of `e` is a disjoint union of simple closed curves `e_1, …, e_k`, with `e_i` of degree `d_i` over `e`; `T_e^{n_e}` fixes the isomorphism class of the covering, and the homeomorphism `∏_i T_{e_i}^{n_e/d_i}` (twists supported in the preimages of a regular neighbourhood of `e`) lifts `T_e^{n_e}`. If the centralizer of the image of `Cov` in `Sym(F)` is trivial, this is the only lift. The same holds for the compactified (branched) coverings when `S` is a punctured closed surface and the twists are supported away from the punctures.

*Proof sketch.* The preimage of an annulus `N` around `e` is a disjoint union of annuli `N_i`, with `N_i → N` the connected `d_i`-fold covering of annuli, whose core curve `e_i` maps with degree `d_i`. On `N_i`, the twist `T^{n_e/d_i}` of the core covers `T^{n_e}` on `N`: in the coordinates `(θ, t) ↦ (d_i θ, t)`, the map `(θ, t) ↦ (θ + 2π(n_e/d_i)t, t)` covers `(θ, t) ↦ (θ + 2πn_e t, t)`, and both are the identity near the boundary circles, so the lift extends by the identity. Lifts of a homeomorphism differ by deck transformations, i.e. by elements of the centralizer of the monodromy image, which is trivial by assumption. Branched case: the twists are supported away from the branch points, so the lift extends over the filled-in points. (LV20, §8.3, p. 42.) *Needs:* 5.8; 5.9; 5.11; 5.24; Mathlib `IsCoveringMap.monodromyPerm`; Mathlib `IsCoveringMap.liftHomotopy`; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- The double cover of the four-times-punctured sphere by a four-times-punctured torus, with `e` enclosing two punctures: `Cov(e) = 1, k = 2`, and `T_e` lifts to `T_{e_1}T_{e_2}`.
- An `Aff(q)`-cover and a curve with `Cov(e)` a `q`-cycle: `k = 1`, and `T_e^q` lifts to `T_{e_1}`.

### 5.26 Monodromy of families of branched covers over configuration spaces

**Lemma.** Let `Σ` be a closed oriented surface, `B ⊂ Σ` finite, `S = Σ ∖ B` with `χ(S) ≤ −1` (so `χ(S ∖ {y}) < 0`), and `F(S) → S` the configuration fibration. Let `𝒵° → F(S)` be a finite covering and, for `y ∈ S`, let `Z_y` be the compact surface obtained by filling in the punctures of the covering `Z°_y → Σ ∖ (B ∪ {y})` (the branched covering of `Σ`). Then: `(a) ⋃_y Z_y` is a locally trivial fibre bundle `𝒵̄ → S` with fibre `Z_{y₀}`; `(b)` for a loop `γ` at `y₀`, the monodromy of `𝒵̄` along `γ` is represented by a homeomorphism `h_γ` of `Z_{y₀}` that lifts a homeomorphism `φ_γ` of `Σ` fixing `B ∪ {y₀}` whose class in `Mod(Σ ∖ (B ∪ {y₀}))` is `Push(γ)` (for `S* = S` with marked point `y₀`); `(c)` consequently the monodromy action of `π₁(S, y₀)` on `H₁(Z_{y₀}; ℤ)` is given by lifts of point-pushing maps; if the lift of `Push(γ)` is unique (trivial centralizer of the monodromy of `Z°_{y₀}`), the action is the lifted action, and in general it differs from a chosen lift by a deck transformation.

*Proof sketch.* Isotopy extension: the loop `γ` extends to an isotopy `φ_t` of `Σ` fixing `B` with `φ_t(y₀) = γ(t)`, which trivializes `F(S)` over `γ` by `(x, t) ↦ (φ_t(x), γ(t))`. Lift the trivialization to `𝒵°` by the homotopy lifting property of coverings, starting from the identity at `t = 0`; the end map `h_γ°` is a homeomorphism of `Z°_{y₀}` lifting `φ_1`. A homeomorphism of punctured-disk coverings that lifts a homeomorphism of the base extending over the puncture extends over the filled-in points; this gives `h_γ` on `Z_{y₀}` and the local triviality `(a)`. `φ_1` represents `Push(γ)` by definition of the push map; the monodromy of the homology local system of `𝒵̄` is induced by `h_γ`. Two lifts of `φ_1` differ by a deck transformation of `Z°_{y₀}`. (LV20, §8.2.3, p. 41; FM12, §4.2, Theorem 4.6, p. 102.) *Needs:* 5.20; 5.23; 5.24; 5.25; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.LocalCoefficientSystem.monodromyRepresentation`; Mathlib `IsCoveringMap.liftHomotopy`; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 2; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- The Legendre family: `Σ = ℙ¹(ℂ), B = {0, 1, ∞}, Z_λ` the double cover branched at `{0, 1, λ, ∞}`.
- The Kodaira–Parshin family: `B = ∅, Σ = Y(ℂ)` of genus `≥ 2, Z_y` the `Aff(q)`-covers branched at `y`.

### Examples

- The boundary-fixed mapping class group of a disk is trivial and that of an annulus is `ℤ`, generated
  by the Dehn twist about its core.
- The mapping class group of the thrice-punctured sphere (punctures may be permuted) is `Sym(3)`; its pure
  subgroup is trivial.
- On a torus with `î(a, b) = 1`, the twist about `a` sends `[b]` to `[b] + î(b, a)[a] = [b] − [a]` (the sign
  convention of the Conventions section), and the two standard twists generate `SL₂(ℤ) = Sp₂(ℤ)`.
- Every loop on the torus pushes to the identity; on a closed surface of genus two, pushing a simple
  nonseparating loop gives a nontrivial mapping class acting trivially on homology.

### Dependencies

TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 4; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 2; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 8; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.6: The `S`-unit theorem

After adjoining eighth roots of unity and inverting `2`, a solution `t` of the `S`-unit equation that is not
a square generates a cyclic Kummer extension of fixed degree `m ≥ 8`, unramified outside `S`; there are
only finitely many such fields. For each, a place inert in it makes the fibre of the cyclic Legendre
family above `t` a single local field of degree `m ≥ 8` over `K_v`. The cyclic Legendre family has full
monodromy (its loop monodromies are powers of transvections, and factor-wise unipotents force the whole
product of `SL₂`'`s`), the Legendre curves with reducible representation are finite in number by the
Hodge-weight argument, and the finiteness criterion applies on every residue disk.

### 6.1 Reductions for the `S`-unit equation

**Finite-generation bridge.** For every number field `K` and finite set `S_f` of height-one
primes of `O_K`, prove `(Set.unit S_f K).FG` (`sUnits_fg`). Use Mathlib's `S`-unit subgroup and its
valuation membership equation. The valuations at the primes of `S_f` give a homomorphism into
`ℤ^(S_f)` whose kernel is the image of `(O_K)ˣ`; that kernel is finitely generated by Dirichlet's
unit theorem, the image is a subgroup of a finitely generated free abelian group, and hence the
whole `S`-unit group is finitely generated. This supplies the hypothesis for identifying the end
statement with DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence §2.4'`s` unit equation; the geometric proof
below is independent of its subspace-theorem proof. Sources: Mathlib
`Mathlib.NumberTheory.NumberField.Units.DirichletTheorem`, the `Monoid.FG (O_K)ˣ` instance
and its proof; `Mathlib.RingTheory.DedekindDomain.SInteger`, `Set.unit_valuation_eq_one` and
`Set.unitEquivUnitsInteger`; the valuation exact-sequence argument just given.

**Checks of the bridge.** With `S_f` empty this is finite generation of the ordinary unit group.
For `K = ℚ` and `S_f = {2}`, the group is `{±2^n : n ∈ ℤ}`. For `S_f = {2,3}`, the valuations
of `2` and `3` give the two independent integer coordinates, while the sign is in the kernel.

**Lemma.** Let `K` be a number field, `S` a finite set of places containing the archimedean ones and `U(K, S) := {t ∈ 𝒪_S^× : 1 − t ∈ 𝒪_S^×}`. `(a)` If `K'/K` is finite and `S' ⊇` the places of `K'` above `S`, then `U(K, S) ⊆ U(K', S')`; so finiteness of `U(K', S')` implies finiteness of `U(K, S)`. In particular one may assume `μ_8 ⊆ K` and that `S` contains the places above 2. `(b)` Assume `μ_8 ⊆ K`, let `m` be the largest power of 2 dividing #`μ(K)` (so `m ≥ 8`), and `U₁ := {t ∈ U(K, S) : t ∉ K^{×2}}`. Then `U(K, S) ⊆ ⋃_{0 ≤ j ≤ log₂ m} {s^{2^j} : s ∈ U₁}`; hence `U(K, S)` is finite if `U₁` is.

*Proof sketch.* `(a) 𝒪_{K,S} ⊆ 𝒪_{K',S'}` and units stay units. `(b)` If `s ∈ K^×` and `s^k ∈ U(K, S)` for some `k ≥ 1`, then `s ∈ U(K, S): s` is integral over `𝒪_S` and a unit, and `1 − s^k = ∏_{η ∈ μ_k(K̄)} (1 − ηs)` is a product of `S`-integers of `K(μ_k)` that is a unit, so `1 − s` is an `S`-unit (its inverse is integral over `𝒪_S` and lies in `K`). Let `t ∈ U`. If `t` is not an `m`-th power, choose `j < log₂ m` maximal with `t = s^{2^j}` for some `s ∈ K`; then `s` is not a square, so `s ∈ U₁` by the previous step. If `t = s^m`, then among `s` and `ζs` (`ζ` a primitive `m`-th root of unity) one is not a square: otherwise `ζ` would be a square, i.e. `K` would contain a primitive `2m`-th root of unity, contradicting the maximality of `m`. Both are `m`-th roots of `t`, so the non-square one lies in `U₁`. (LV20, §4.1, p. 20.) *Needs:* Mathlib `Set.integer`; Mathlib `Set.unit`.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ, S = {2, ∞}: U = {−1, 2, 1/2}`.
- `K = ℚ(ζ₈): μ(K) = μ₈`, so `m = 8`.

### 6.2 The Kummer fields of non-square `S`-unit solutions

**Lemma.** Assume `μ₈ ⊆ K`, let `m ≥ 8` be the largest power of two dividing `|μ(K)|`, and let `S` contain the places above 2. Let `U₁` be the set of non-squares of 6.1. For `t ∈ U₁: (a)` the class of `t` in `K^×/K^{×m}` has order exactly `m`; `(b) X^m − t` is irreducible over `K` and `L_t := K[X]/(X^m − t)` is a cyclic extension of `K` of degree `m`, unramified at every finite place outside `S`; `(c)` the fields `L_t (t ∈ U₁)` fall into finitely many isomorphism classes, so `U₁ = U_{1,L_1} ∪ ⋯ ∪ U_{1,L_r}` with `U_{1,L} := {t ∈ U₁ : L_t ≅ L}`.

*Proof sketch.* `(a)` If `t^k = a^m` with `k` a proper divisor of `m`, then `t ∈ a^{m/k}μ_k ⊆ K^{×2}·μ_k`; `μ_k` consists of squares since `k | m/2` and `μ_m ⊆ K`, contradicting `t ∉ K^{×2}`. `(b)` Kummer theory with `μ_m ⊆ K`: for a root `α` of `X^m − t, σ ↦ σ(α)/α` embeds `Gal(K(α)/K)` into `μ_m` with image `μ_d, d = [K(α) : K]`; then `α^d ∈ K`, so `t^d ∈ K^{×m}`, and conversely `t^k ∈ K^{×m}` forces `α^k ∈ K` and `d | k`. So `d` equals the order of `t`, which is `m` by `(a)`; `X^m − t` is irreducible, `K(α)` is its splitting field and `Gal(K(α)/K) ≅ μ_m` is cyclic. Unramified outside `S`: at a finite place `w ∉ S, t` and `m` are `w`-units, so `𝒪_w[X]/(X^m − t)` is standard étale (the derivative `mX^{m−1}` is a unit modulo `X^m − t`) and `K_w[X]/(X^m − t)` is a product of unramified extensions of `K_w`. `(c)` Hermite–Minkowski: there are finitely many extensions of `K` of degree `m` unramified outside `S` (ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1). (LV20, §4.1, pp. 20–21; §4.1, p. 21.) *Needs:* 6.1; ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1 (Hermite–Minkowski); Mathlib `isCyclic_of_isSplittingField_X_pow_sub_C`; Mathlib `autEquivZmod`; Mathlib `autEquivRootsOfUnity`; Mathlib `StandardEtalePair`; Mathlib `Polynomial.separable_X_pow_sub_C_unit`; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ(ζ₈), S = {places above 2, ∞}`: every `L_t` is a cyclic octic extension of `ℚ(ζ₈)` unramified outside 2.
- `t = ζ₈` lies in `U₁` for `K = ℚ(ζ₈)` (`1 − ζ₈` generates the prime above 2, and `ζ₈` is not a square in `K`), and `L_t = ℚ(ζ₆₄)`, cyclic of degree 8 over `K`.

### 6.3 An auxiliary place inert in a cyclic extension

**Lemma.** Let `L/K` be a cyclic extension of number fields, `S` a finite set of places of `K` and `T` a finite set of places. There is a finite place `v ∉ S ∪ T`, unramified in `L`, whose Frobenius generates `Gal(L/K)`, whose residue characteristic `p` is odd, unramified in `K` and not below any place of `S`. For such `v, L ⊗_K K_v` is a field, unramified of degree `[L : K]` over `K_v`, and `K_v/ℚ_p` is unramified.

*Proof sketch.* Apply Chebotarev (Tau Ceti Chebotarev Layer 10) to the conjugacy class of a generator `σ`, excluding the finitely many places in `S ∪ T`, above 2, above primes ramified in `K` and above primes lying below `S`. The decomposition group of a place above `v` is generated by `Frob_v = σ`, hence is all of `Gal(L/K)`; so there is one place above `v`, with residue degree `[L : K]` and ramification index 1, and `L ⊗_K K_v` is that completion. (LV20, §4.1, p. 21.) *Needs:* Tau Ceti `NumberField.artinSymbol`; Tau Ceti `NumberField.exists_isArithFrobAt`; Tau Ceti `NumberField.Chebotarev.frobeniusPrimeSet`; TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev Layer 10; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ(i)` and `L = ℚ(ζ₈)`, a quadratic extension of `K`: every prime `p ≡ 5 mod 8` splits in `K`, and the places of `K` above it are inert in `L` because 2 is not a square modulo `p`.
- For `m = 8` and `K = ℚ(ζ₈), v` has residue degree 8 in `L_t`.

### 6.4 The Legendre family and its cyclic variant (LV20 §4.2)

The universal smooth family is ModularCurves Layer `4C`. The light coefficient model
`legendreCurve t : WeierstrassCurve R` here is defined over every commutative ring, including
singular parameters and characteristic two, so that the discriminant and specialization tests
make sense before any ellipticity assumption. It does not introduce another moduli family.

**Construction.** Let `𝒪` be a ring with `2 ∈ 𝒪^×` and `𝒴 := Spec 𝒪[t, 1/(t(1 − t))] = ℙ¹_𝒪 ∖ {0, 1, ∞}`. The Weierstrass curve `E : y² = x(x − 1)(x − t)` over `𝒴` has discriminant `16t²(t − 1)²`, a unit, so it is an elliptic curve over `𝒴` (an abelian scheme of relative dimension 1 with its principal polarization), with `j = 2⁸(t² − t + 1)³/(t²(t − 1)²)`. For `m` a power of 2, put `𝒴' := Spec 𝒪[u, 1/(u(1 − u^m))]` and `π : 𝒴' → 𝒴, t ↦ u^m`; `π` is finite étale of degree `m`, since `𝒪[t, 1/(t(1−t))][u]/(u^m − t) ≅ 𝒪[u, 1/(u(1 − u^m))]` is standard étale. Let `X → 𝒴'` be the Legendre curve with parameter `u, y² = x(x − 1)(x − u)` (the pullback of `E` along the open immersion `𝒴' ⊆ 𝒴, u ↦ t`, which is defined since `u − 1` divides `u^m − 1`). Then `X → 𝒴' → 𝒴` is an abelian-by-finite family of relative dimension 1; for `𝒪 = 𝒪_S` with `S ⊇` places above 2 it is a good model of its generic fibre, and for `t₀ ∈ 𝒴(K)` the fibre algebra is `E_{t₀} = K[u]/(u^m − t₀)`, with `X_{t₀}` geometrically the disjoint union of the curves `E_u, u^m = t₀`.

The Legendre curve is the base change of the universal curve of the Legendre problem of TauCetiRoadmap ModularCurves, Layer `4C`, along `ℤ[1/2][λ, 1/(λ(λ − 1))] → 𝒪[t, 1/(t(1 − t))]`, `λ ↦ t`; what is new here is the cyclic variant and its structure as an abelian-by-finite family.

- `legendreCurve`: for every commutative ring `R` and `t : R`, the coefficient model `y² = x(x − 1)(x − t)` as a `WeierstrassCurve R`; its specialization over `O[t, 1/(t(1−t))]` is the supplied universal smooth family.
- `legendreCurve.isElliptic`: Its discriminant `16t²(t − 1)²` is a unit when 2 is.
- `legendreCurve.j_eq`: `j = 2⁸(t² − t + 1)³/(t²(t − 1)²)`.
- `legendreVariant`: The abelian-by-finite family `X → 𝒴' → 𝒴` with `π(u) = u^m`.
- `legendreVariant.isGoodModel`: It is a good model over `𝒪_S` when `S` contains the places above 2.
- `legendreVariant.fibreAlgebra`: `E_{t₀} = K[u]/(u^m − t₀)`.
- `legendreVariant.analytic`: Over `ℂ`, the total space of `X` over `𝒴'(ℂ) = ℂ ∖ ({0} ∪ μ_m)` is the restriction of the Legendre family over `ℂ ∖ {0, 1}`.
- `legendreCurve.j_one_seven_two_eight`: `j(−1) = j(2) = j(1/2) = 1728`.
- `legendreCurve.Δ_eq`: `Δ = 16t²(t − 1)²`.

*Construction.* Compute the discriminant of the Weierstrass equation; a Weierstrass curve with unit discriminant over a ring is elliptic (Mathlib) and is an abelian scheme with its canonical principal polarization. `π` is standard étale: `u^m − t` is monic in `u` with derivative mu`^{m−1}`, a unit because `m` and `u = t/u^{m−1}` are. The good-model conditions of Layer LV.2 hold for abelian schemes over the smooth `𝒪_S`-scheme `𝒴` (AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli A4). The fibre of `π` over `t₀` is `Spec K[u]/(u^m − t₀)`, and the fibre of `X` over it is the Legendre curve with parameter `u`. (LV20, §4.2, p. 21.) *Needs:* 2.1; 2.2; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A0.2, A1.6; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A2.5; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A4.3; Mathlib `WeierstrassCurve`; Mathlib `WeierstrassCurve.IsElliptic`; Mathlib `WeierstrassCurve.j`; Mathlib `StandardEtalePair`.

**Checks.**

- The Legendre coefficients have `Δ=16t²(t−1)²` and `j=256(t²−t+1)³/(t²(t−1)²)`.
- For `m=2, t₀=4` over `ℚ`, the finite étale fibre algebra is `ℚ×ℚ` and the two curves are `E₂` and `E_{−2}`.
- At `t=0` or `t=1, Δ=0`, so the Legendre equation does not define an elliptic fibre.
- `t = −1, 2, 1/2` (the `S`-units of `ℚ` with `S = {2, ∞}`) all give `j = 1728`, the curve `y² = x³ − x` up to twist.
- `m = 2, t₀ = 4: E_{t₀} = K[u]/(u² − 4) ≅ K × K` and `X_{t₀} = E_2 ⊔ E_{−2}`.

### 6.5 Monodromy of the Legendre family

**Theorem.** Let `B = ℂ ∖ {0, 1}, λ₀ ∈ (0, 1)`, and let `γ₀` (resp. `γ₁`) be the standard positively oriented loop, accessed along the real interval from `λ₀`, encircling 0 (resp. 1) once and no other point of `{0, 1}`. There are classes `a, b ∈ H₁(E_{λ₀}(ℂ); ℤ)` with `î(a, b) = ±1` such that the monodromy of the Legendre family along `γ₀` (resp. `γ₁`) acts on `H₁(E_{λ₀}(ℂ); ℤ)` as `ε₀T_a^{2s₀}` (resp. `ε₁T_b^{2s₁}`) with `ε_i,s_i ∈ {±1}`, where `T_c(x) = x + î(x, c)c`. Consequently: `(a)` the monodromy of `γ₀²` (resp. `γ₁²`) is the nontrivial unipotent `T_a^{4s₀}` (resp. `T_b^{4s₁}`); `(b)` the algebraic monodromy group of the Legendre family is `SL(H¹_B(E_{λ₀}, ℂ)) = Sp`, i.e. the family has full monodromy; `(c)` the Zariski closure of the monodromy of any subgroup of `π₁(B, λ₀)` containing powers `γ₀^k` and `γ₁^k (k ≥ 1)` is also `SL₂`.

*Proof sketch.* Topological model: `E_λ(ℂ)` is the double cover of `ℙ¹(ℂ)` branched at `{0, 1, λ, ∞}`, and the family is the configuration family of Layer LV.5 with `Σ = ℙ¹(ℂ), B = {0, 1, ∞}, S = ℂ ∖ {0, 1} (χ = −1)` and `𝒵° = {y² = x(x − 1)(x − λ), y ≠ 0}`; the analytic local system `R¹f_*ℤ` is its homology local system (Ehresmann; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli A5). By Layer LV.5, the monodromy along `γ` is `±` the action of the lift of `Push(γ)`, the sign coming from the deck involution, which acts on `H₁` of the elliptic curve by `−1`. `Push(γ₀) = T_{c₀}^{±1}` in `Mod(ℂ ∖ {0, 1, λ₀})`, where `c₀` bounds a disk `D₀` containing exactly 0 and `λ₀` (Birman: the other push-off bounds a once-punctured disk). The monodromy of the double cover around `c₀` is trivial (two branch points), so `c₀` lifts to two curves `c', c''` and `T_{c₀}` lifts to `T_{c'}T_{c''}`. The preimage of `D₀` is an annulus with boundary `c' ⊔ c''` (Euler characteristic `2·1 − 2 = 0`), exchanged by the deck involution, so `[c''] = −[c'] =: −a` and `T_{c'}T_{c''}` acts by `x ↦ x + 2î(x, a)a` (Layer LV.5, Dehn twists on homology). The complement of the annulus is also an annulus, so `c'` is nonseparating and `a` is primitive. Retain the point-push exponent `s₀`: the loop transport is `ε₀T_a^{2s₀}`; the scalar deck sign `ε₀` does not absorb an inverse twist. Likewise `γ₁` gives `ε₁T_b^{2s₁}` with `b` from a disk `D₁` containing exactly `λ₀` and 1. Taking `D₀, D₁` to be thin neighbourhoods of the segments `[0, λ₀]` and `[λ₀, 1]`, the core circles of the two annuli are the preimages of the segments, which meet in exactly one point, transversally (in the local coordinate `w² = x − λ₀` the segments lift to the two axes); so `î(a, b) = ±1`. `(b), (c)`: the Zariski closure contains the closures of `{T_a^{4ks₀n}}` and `{T_b^{4ks₁n}}`, i.e. the root groups `U_a, U_b` (Layer LV.0), which generate `Sp(H₁ ⊗ ℂ) = SL₂` because `î(a, b) ≠ 0` (LV20 Lemma 2.13 in Layer LV.0). (LV20, §4.4, proof of Lemma 4.3, p. 24; FM12, §4.2, Fact 4.7, p. 103.) *Needs:* 0.19; 0.20; 3.11; 5.12; 5.13; 5.20; 5.25; 5.26; 6.4; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2.

**Checks.**

- Algebraic convention check: in a basis `(a,b)` with `î(a,b)=1`, the positive operators `T_a²=[[1,−2],[0,1]], T_b²=[[1,0],[2,1]]` generate the standard free index-two subgroup of `Γ(2)`. Inverting either generator leaves the generated subgroup and its Zariski closure unchanged.
- For the positive operators, `T_a²T_b²=[[−3,−2],[2,1]]` has trace `−2`. This checks the transvection convention; identifying those operators with the specific positive puncture-loop transports requires comparing the orientation of each puncture loop with the annulus model of the twist (5.11, 5.19).

### 6.6 A closed subgroup of a product of `SL₂'s` with surjective projections and factorwise unipotents is everything

**Lemma.** Let `k` be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, `V_1, …, V_r` two-dimensional `k`-vector spaces and `H ⊆ ∏_j SL(V_j)` a Zariski-closed subgroup such that `(i)` each projection `pr_j(H)` is `SL(V_j)`, and (ii) for each `j, H` contains an element whose `j`-th component is a nontrivial unipotent and whose other components are 1. Then `H = ∏_j SL(V_j)`.

*Proof sketch.* `N_j := H ∩ (1 × ⋯ × SL(V_j) × ⋯ × 1)` is a closed subgroup of `SL(V_j)` normalized by `pr_j(H) = SL(V_j)`, hence a closed normal subgroup of `SL(V_j)`. By (ii) it contains a nontrivial unipotent, which is not `±1`; closed normal subgroups of `SL₂` are `SL₂` or contained in `{±1}` (ReductiveGroups Layer 6), so `N_j = SL(V_j)`. Hence `H ⊇ ∏_j N_j = ∏_j SL(V_j)`. (LV20, §4.4, proof of Lemma 4.3, p. 24.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.17; Tau Ceti `LinearMap.GeneralLinearGroup.IsUnipotent`; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6.

**Checks.**

- The diagonal `SL₂ ⊆ SL₂ × SL₂` satisfies `(i)` but not (ii).
- `r = 1: (i)` alone already gives `H = SL₂`.

### 6.7 Big monodromy for the cyclic variant of the Legendre family (LV20 Lemma 4.3)

**Theorem.** For every `m ≥ 1`, the family over `ℂ ∖ {0, 1}` whose fibre over `t` is `⊔_{z^m = t} E_z` (the analytification of `X → Y' → Y` of the Legendre variant 6.4) has full monodromy: for `t₀ ∈ ℂ ∖ {0, 1}`, the Zariski closure of the monodromy on `⊕_{z^m = t₀} H¹_B(E_z, ℚ)` contains `∏_z SL(H¹_B(E_z, ℂ))`.

*Proof sketch.* Full monodromy does not depend on the base point, so take `t₀ ∈ (0, 1)` and `u₀ = t₀^{1/m} ∈ (0, 1)`, with roots `u_j = ζ^j u₀`. `Y' → Y (u ↦ u^m)` is a connected cyclic covering, so `π₁(Y', u₀)` is a normal subgroup of `π₁(Y, t₀)` of index `m` whose elements lift to closed loops at every `u_j` and act factorwise, by Legendre monodromy along the lifts `(Y' ⊆ ℂ ∖ {0, 1})`. Let `H` be the Zariski closure of the image of `π₁(Y', u₀)`. The loop `δ₀` running `m` times around the circle `|t| = t₀` lifts at `u₀` to the circle `|u| = u₀`, a simple loop around 0 avoiding 1; a small loop `δ₁` around `t = 1` (joined to `t₀` along the real segment) lifts at `u₀` to a simple loop around 1, and at `u_j (j ≠ 0)` to a loop around `ζ^j` joined along the ray arg `u = 2πj/m`, which is null-homotopic in `ℂ ∖ {0, 1}`. Both `δ₀` and `δ₁` lie in `π₁(Y', u₀)`. By the Legendre monodromy theorem `(c), pr_{u₀}(H) = SL₂`; and `μ(δ₁²)` is the nontrivial unipotent `T_b⁴` in the `u₀`-factor and 1 elsewhere. The loop once around `t = 0` permutes the roots cyclically and normalizes `H` (`π₁(Y')` is normal); conjugating by its powers transports both properties to every factor. The factorwise-unipotent criterion gives `H = ∏ SL(H¹_B(E_{u_j}, ℂ)) ⊆ Γ`. (LV20, §4.3, Lemma 4.3, p. 21; §4.4, proof of Lemma 4.3, p. 24.) *Needs:* 3.11; 6.4; 6.5; 6.6; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- `m = 2, t₀ = 1/4`: two factors `u = ±1/2`, and the loop around `t = 1` acts as `(±T_b², 1)`.
- `m = 1` is the Legendre monodromy theorem.

### 6.8 Generic simplicity of the Legendre curves (LV20 Lemma 4.4)

**Theorem.** Let `L` be a number field and `p > 2` a prime unramified in `L`. There are only finitely many `z ∈ L` such that `z` and `1 − z` are units at every place of `L` above `p` and `H¹_et(E_{z, L̄}, ℚ_p)` (equivalently `V_p(E_z)`) is reducible as a `G_L`-representation.

*Proof sketch.* The admissible `z` fall into finitely many classes modulo the places above `p` (their residues avoid 0 and 1); fix a class with representative `z₀ ∈ L`. For `z` in the class, `E_z` has good reduction at every `w | p` (`16z²(z − 1)²` is a `w`-unit and `p > 2`). Suppose `W ⊂ H¹(E_z)` is a `G_L`-stable line. `W` is pure of weight 1, finitely ramified and crystalline at the places above p. LV20 Lemma 2.10 (1.15) with `K = ℚ` and `v = p` (friendly, as `ℚ` has no CM subfield and `p` is unramified) gives `Σ_{w|p} [L_w : ℚ_p]·a_w(W) = [L : ℚ]/2` with `a_w(W) ∈ {0, 1}`; since `Σ_{w|p} [L_w : ℚ_p] = [L : ℚ]`, some `w` has `a_w(W) = 1`. At that `w`, with `D_z := D_cris(H¹(E_z)|G_{L_w}) = (H¹_dR(E_z/L_w), φ_z, F¹)` (4.1), the line `W^dR := D_cris(W)` satisfies `W^dR ⊆ F¹` (the filtration is induced), so `W^dR = F¹D_z`. Weak admissibility of `D_cris` gives `t_N = t_H` for `W^dR (= 1)` and for `D_z (= 1)`. So `φ_z` has slope 1 on `W^dR` and slope 0 on `D_z/W^dR`, the `L_w`-linear `φ_z^{[L_w:ℚ_p]}` has eigenvalues of different valuations, and `W^dR` is the unique `φ_z`-stable line of slope `1, ℓ(D_z)`. The Gauss–Manin transport `T_w(z) : (D_{z₀}, φ_{z₀}) ≅ (D_z, φ_z)` (2.11) carries `ℓ(D_{z₀})` to `ℓ(D_z)` when either exists; hence the period map satisfies `Φ_w(z) = T_w(z)^{-1}(F¹D_z) = ℓ₀ := ℓ(D_{z₀})`, a fixed point of `ℙ(D_{z₀})` (if `ℓ(D_{z₀})` does not exist, no `z` in the class is reducible at `w`). The Legendre family over `L` has full monodromy (Legendre monodromy theorem) and `ℂ ∖ {0, 1}` is connected, so by 3.19 the set `{z ∈ Ω_w(z₀) : Φ_w(z) = ℓ₀}` is finite (a point has dimension `0 < 1`). Take the union over the finitely many `w | p` and classes. (LV20, §4.3, Lemma 4.4, p. 22; §4.4, proof of Lemma 4.4, p. 24.) *Needs:* 1.5; 1.15; 1.16; 2.9; 3.10; 3.12; 3.19; 4.1; 6.4; 6.5; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.1; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5.

**Checks.**

- `L = ℚ, p = 3` and `z ≡ 2 mod 3: E_z` reduces to `y² = x³ − x` over `𝔽₃`, which is supersingular `(a₃ = 0)`; both slopes are 1/2, so no `z` in this class has reducible `V₃(E_z)`.
- Lawrence and Venkatesh remark that much stronger results are known (Serre's open image theorem); the point is the combination of purity and period maps that Layer LV.7 reuses.

### 6.9 Finiteness in a residue disk (LV20 Lemma 4.2)

**Theorem.** Assume `μ_8 ⊆ K` and that `S` contains the places above 2; let `m` be the 2-part of #`μ(K), L` a cyclic extension of `K` of degree `m, v` a finite place as in 6.3 (`v ∉ S, Frob_v` generates `Gal(L/K), p =` char `k_v` odd and unramified in `K`, no place of `S` above `p`), and `t₀ ∈ 𝒪_S`. Then `{t ∈ U_{1,L} : t ≡ t₀ mod v}` is finite.

*Proof sketch.* We may assume `t₀ ∈ U_{1,L}`. Use the Legendre-variant family over `𝒪_S` with `y₀ = t₀ ∈ 𝒴(𝒪_S): E₀ = K[u]/(u^m − t₀) ≅ L`, and since `v` is inert in `L, E₀ ⊗ K_v = L₀` is a field of degree `m` over `K_v`; there is a single pair `(y₀', w₀)` over `(t₀, v)` and, for `t` in the disk, a single pair `(t', w)` over `(t, v)`, with `K(t') ≅ L`. The fibre over `t'` is the Legendre curve `E_z` over `K(t'), z` a root of `u^m = t`. For an isomorphism `α : K(t') ≅ L, α(z)` and `1 − α(z)` are units above `p` (`t` is an `S`-unit and `1 − z` divides `1 − t`), and `p` is unramified in `L` (`L/K` is unramified outside `S`). By LV20 Lemma 4.4 (6.8), outside a finite set of `t` (those with `α(z)` in the finite exceptional set, for one of the `m` choices of `α`), `ρ_{t'}` is simple. Faltings's finiteness lemma over `L` (dimension 2, weight 1, unramified outside the places above `S` and `p`, integral Frobenius polynomials) leaves finitely many isomorphism classes for the semisimple `ρ_{t'}`; hence the pairs `(K(t'), ρ_{t'})`, and the local pairs `(K(t')_w, ρ_{t',w})`, lie in finitely many classes. Apply the finiteness criterion 4.4: the family has full monodromy (LV20 Lemma 4.3, 6.7), `ℂ ∖ {0, 1}` is connected, `[L₀ : K_v] = m ≥ 8` and `c ≤ (dim_{L₀} H¹_dR)² = 4 < m = dim Z₀`.
