(LV20, §4.1, Lemma 4.2, p. 21; §4.3, proof of Lemma 4.2, p. 22; §4.3, proof of Lemma 4.2, p. 23.) *Needs:* 0.6; 1.2; 1.16; 4.4; 6.2; 6.3; 6.4; 6.7; 6.8; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ(ζ₈), m = 8: c ≤ 4 < 8 = dim H_v`.
- Without the variant `(m = 1)` the criterion fails, as for the Legendre family over `ℚ_p` (LV20 §1.3).

### 6.10 Lawrence–Venkatesh proof of the supplied `S`-unit theorem (LV20 Theorem 4.1)

**Proof target.** Give the Lawrence–Venkatesh proof of the number-field `S`-unit specialization of
DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence §2.4'`s`
`DiophantineApproximation.finite_two_term_unit_equation`, with `a = b = 1` and
`Γ = Set.unit S_f K`, where `S_f` is the finite-prime part of `S`. The hypotheses are a number field `K` and a finite set `S` of places
containing the archimedean ones. Write `U(K,S) := {t ∈ O_Sˣ : 1 − t ∈ O_Sˣ}`; this is the
solution set identified with the supplied two-term unit equation. Its finite generation is the
finite-generation target `sUnits_fg` of §6.1. This is a proof of that supplied declaration, with no second local finiteness declaration.

*Proof sketch.* By 6.1, assume `μ_8 ⊆ K` and that `S` contains the places above 2; it suffices to show that `U₁` is finite. `U₁` is the finite union of the `U_{1,L}` over the Kummer fields `L` (Hermite–Minkowski). For each `L` choose `v` by 6.3. `𝒴(𝒪_S)` meets finitely many residue classes modulo `v` (`k_v` is finite); in each class that meets `U_{1,L}` choose `t₀` in it and apply 6.9 (LV20 Lemma 4.2). (LV20, §4, Theorem 4.1, p. 20; §4, p. 20.) *Needs:* 6.1; 6.2; 6.3; 6.9.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ, S = {2, ∞}: U = {−1, 2, 1/2}`.
- `K = ℚ, S = {2, 3, ∞}: U` contains `−1, 2, 1/2, 3, −2, 4, −3, 9, −8`, and their images under `t ↦ 1 − t` and `t ↦ 1/t`.

### Examples

- `j(−1) = j(2) = j(1/2) = 1728` for the Legendre curve, and `Δ = 16t²(t − 1)²` vanishes at `t = 0, 1`.
- For `m = 2` and `t₀ = 4` over `ℚ`, the fibre algebra of the cyclic variant is `ℚ[u]/(u² − 4) ≅ ℚ × ℚ`, and its
  curves are `E₂` and `E_{−2}`.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.1, LV.2, LV.3, LV.4, LV.5; ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev Layer 10; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A0.2, A1.6; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A2.5; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A4.3; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.1; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.7: Rational points on the base of an abelian-by-finite family

For a general abelian-by-finite family over a curve the local factors need not be large. The proportion
`size_v` of geometric points of the fibre lying in short Frobenius orbits measures this, and the layer
proves that the rational points with `size_v < 1/(d+1)` are finite. Such a point always has a local
factor of degree at least eight. If its representation there is not simple, a minimal subrepresentation
and the average Hodge weight produce a Frobenius-stable subspace meeting the Hodge filtration in at least
half its dimension; general position for at least five Lagrangians makes such filtrations a proper
closed subset of the period variety, so these bad points are finite. The simple fibres are handled by
the finiteness criterion together with Faltings's finiteness lemma.

### 7.1 The proportion of small Frobenius orbits (LV20 Definition 5.2)

**Definition.** Let `K` be a number field, `v` a finite place and `E` a nonempty finite set with a continuous action of `G_K` that is unramified at `v` (the inertia groups at the places of `K̄` above `v` act trivially). For an arithmetic Frobenius `Frob_v` at a place of `K̄` above `v, size_v(E) :=` #`{e ∈ E : the ⟨Frob_v⟩-orbit of e has fewer than 8 elements}` / #`E`. It does not depend on the choice of place above `v` or of Frobenius. For a finite étale `K`-scheme `Z, size_v(Z) := size_v(Z(K̄))`. The total finite-permutation function in `Suggested.lean` assigns value zero on the empty set, using rational division by zero; all proportions in the arithmetic applications have nonempty fibres. If `f : E → E'` is a `G_K`-equivariant map all of whose fibres have the same cardinality, then `size_v(E) ≤ size_v(E')` (LV20 (5.3)).

- `sizeV`: `size_v(E) ∈ ℚ` for a nonempty finite `G_K`-set unramified at `v`.
- `sizeV_indep`: Independence of the place above `v` and of the Frobenius.
- `sizeV_le_of_fibres`: `size_v(E) ≤ size_v(E')` for an equivariant map with fibres of constant cardinality.
- `sizeV_mem_Icc`: `0 ≤ size_v(E) ≤ 1`.
- `sizeV_scheme`: `size_v` of a finite étale `K`-scheme through its `K̄`-points.
- `sizeV_eq_places`: For `Z = Spec E` with `E` finite étale and unramified at `v: size_v(Z) = Σ_{[E_w : K_v] < 8} [E_w : K_v] / [E : K]`, the sum over the factors `E_w` of `E ⊗_K K_v`.
- `sizeV_cycle`: A single `k`-cycle: size 1 if `k < 8`, else 0.

*Construction.* Two choices of Frobenius are conjugate by some `g ∈ G_K` modulo inertia; `g` maps the small-orbit elements for one choice bijectively onto those for the other. (5.3): all fibres having the same cardinality `c > 0, f` is surjective and #`E = c·`#`E'`. The orbit of `e` maps onto the orbit of `f(e)`, so small-orbit elements of `E` lie over small-orbit elements of `E'`, and `#small(E) ≤ c·#small(E')`. (LV20, §5, Definition 5.2, p. 25; §5, (5.3), p. 25.) *Needs:* Mathlib `IsArithFrobAt`; Tau Ceti `NumberField.exists_isArithFrobAt`; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- A single 7-cycle has `size_v=1`; a single 8-cycle has `size_v=0` (`sizeV_seven_cycle`, `sizeV_eight_cycle`).
- A disjoint union of one fixed point and one 8-cycle has `size_v=1/9`, not 1/2 (`sizeV_fixedPoint_eight_cycle`).
- An 8-cycle mapping to a singleton is equivariant with constant fibre size and gives `0≤1` in the monotonicity inequality.
- `E = 𝔽_{q^k}`-points of a set permuted by Frobenius as a single `k`-cycle: `size_v(E) = 1` if `k < 8` and 0 otherwise.
- For `E = Υ ⊆ (ℤ/N)^{2g}` and `E → Υ` with fibres of constant size (Layer LV.11), `size_v(E) ≤ size_v(Υ)`.

### 7.2 Frobenius orbits on geometric points are the places above `v`

**Lemma.** Let `E` be a finite étale `K`-algebra unramified at the finite place `v`, and fix a place `v̄` of `K̄` above `v`. The `⟨Frob_v⟩`-orbits on `Hom_K(E, K̄)` are in bijection with the factors `E_w` of `E ⊗_K K_v` (the pairs `(i, w)` with `E = ∏ E_i` and `w` a place of `E_i` above `v`), the orbit of `(i, w)` having `[E_w : K_v]` elements; in particular `Σ_w [E_w : K_v] = [E : K]`.

*Proof sketch.* Completion at `v̄` identifies the decomposition group `D_{v̄}` with `G_{K_v}` and `Hom_K(E_i, K̄)` with `Hom_{K_v}(E_i ⊗ K_v, K̄_v) = ⊔_{w|v} Hom_{K_v}(E_{i,w}, K̄_v)` as `D_{v̄}`-sets. `G_{K_v}` acts transitively on `Hom_{K_v}(E_{i,w}, K̄_v)`, a set with `[E_{i,w} : K_v]` elements; since `E_{i,w}/K_v` is unramified, the action factors through the unramified quotient, topologically generated by `Frob_v`, so the `⟨Frob_v⟩`-orbits are the `G_{K_v}`-orbits. (LV20, §6, proof of Lemma 6.1, p. 32; §6, (6.1), p. 30.) *Needs:* 0.1; Mathlib `IsArithFrobAt`; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- `E = K(√a)` with `v` inert: one orbit of size 2.
- `E = K × K`: two fixed points.

### 7.3 Finite surjective morphisms preserve dimension

**Lemma.** Let `f : X → Y` be a finite surjective morphism of schemes of finite type over a field. Then `dim X = dim Y`, and `f` maps every closed subset `Z ⊆ X` onto a closed subset of the same dimension. In particular, if `M/k` is a finite splitting extension, the base-change projection `X_M → X` is finite surjective and preserves dimensions of closed subsets and their images. A projection to only some factors of a product has no such dimension guarantee.

*Proof sketch.* Finite morphisms are closed and have finite fibres; by going up for integral extensions, chains of irreducible closed subsets lift and descend, so dimension is preserved on every closed subset and its image. (Stacks, Tag `0ECG` (Lemma 29.45.9).) *Needs:* SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0.

### 7.4 General position for tuples of Lagrangians (LV20 Lemma 6.4)

**Lemma.** Let `(V, ω)` be a symplectic space of dimension `2d ≥ 2` over a field `k` of characteristic zero and `r ≥ 5`. Let `E_r ⊆ LGr(V, ω)^r` be the set of tuples `(F_1, …, F_r)` of Lagrangians (over `k̄`) for which some subspace `0 ≠ W ≠ V` satisfies `dim(F_j ∩ W) ≥ dim(W)/2` for all `j`. Then `E_r` is Zariski closed and `E_r ≠ LGr(V, ω)^r`; in particular `dim E_r < r·d(d+1)/2`.

*Proof sketch.* Closedness: in `Gr(V) × LGr(V, ω)^r` (`Gr(V)` the disjoint union of the Grassmannians `Gr(V, w), 0 < w < 2d`) the conditions `dim(F_j ∩ W) ≥ ⌈w/2⌉` are rank conditions on the tautological bundles, hence closed; `E_r` is the image of this closed set under the projection, which is proper because `Gr(V)` is projective. In a symplectic basis `(e_i, e_i')` with `⟨e_i, e_i'⟩ = 1` take `F_1 = span(e_i), F_2 = span(e_i'), F_3 = span(e_i + e_i'), F_4 = span(e_i + 2i·e_i')`; these are Lagrangian and pairwise transverse (`2i ≠ 1` in characteristic zero). If `W` works for `F_1, …, F_4`, then `W = (W ∩ F_1) ⊕ (W ∩ F_2)` with both summands of dimension `dim W/2`, and likewise for `F_3, F_4`; the projections along `F_1 ⊕ F_2` give isomorphisms `W ∩ F_3 ≅ W ∩ F_1` and `W ∩ F_3 ≅ W ∩ F_2`, so `Φ_{12;3} : F_1 → F_2 (e_i ↦ e_i')` maps `W ∩ F_1` onto `W ∩ F_2`, and similarly `Φ_{12;4} (e_i ↦ 2i·e_i')`. Hence `W ∩ F_1` is stable under `Φ_{12;4}⁻¹Φ_{12;3}`, which is diagonal with the distinct eigenvalues `(2i)⁻¹`; so `W ∩ F_1` is spanned by a subset of the `e_i, W ∩ F_2 = Φ_{12;3}(W ∩ F_1)`, and there are finitely many candidates `W_1, …, W_M`. For each proper nonzero `W_s` the set of Lagrangians `F` with `dim(F ∩ W_s) ≥ dim(W_s)/2` is a proper closed subset of `LGr(V, ω)`: otherwise every pair of transverse Lagrangians `L, L'` would split `W_s` as `(W_s ∩ L) ⊕ (W_s ∩ L')` with equal dimensions, and letting `L'` run over the graphs of symmetric maps into `L` (which can send a given nonzero vector anywhere in `L`) forces `L ⊆ W_s` and symmetrically `L' ⊆ W_s`, so `W_s = V`. `LGr(V, ω)(k)` is Zariski dense (it contains an affine space), so some `F_5 ∈ LGr(V, ω)(k)` avoids these finitely many proper closed subsets; `(F_1, …, F_5, F_6, …, F_r) ∉ E_r` for any `F_6, …, F_r`. (LV20, §6, Lemma 6.4, p. 33; §6, proof of Lemma 6.4, p. 33; §6, proof of Lemma 6.4, p. 34.) *Needs:* 3.1; 3.2; 3.3; 3.4; 3.5; 3.6; 3.7; 7.3; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §§1.3, 1.7; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0.

**Checks.**

- `d = 1` (lines in a plane): `W` is a line and the condition says `W = F_j` for all `j`, so `E_r` is the small diagonal, of dimension `1 < r`.
- The explicit `F_1, …, F_4` of Lawrence and Venkatesh for `d = 2: span(e₁, e₂), span(e₁', e₂'), span(e₁ + e₁', e₂ + e₂'), span(e₁ + 2e₁', e₂ + 4e₂')`.

### 7.5 Lagrangians meeting a Frobenius-stable subspace in half its dimension (LV20 Lemma 6.3)

**Lemma.** Let `K_v` be a field of characteristic zero, `L_w/K_v` a cyclic extension of degree `r ≥ 5` with generator `σ_w, (V, ω)` a symplectic `L_w`-space of dimension `2d`, and `φ : V → V` a bijective `σ_w`-semilinear map that is a similitude: `ω(φx, φy) = c·σ_w(ω(x, y))` for some `c ∈ K_v^×`. Let `H_w := LGr_{L_w}(V, ω)`, a `K_v`-variety of dimension `r·d(d+1)/2`. There is a Zariski-closed `B_w ⊂ H_w` with `dim B_w < dim H_w` such that every Lagrangian `L_w`-subspace `F ∈ H_w(K_v)` for which some `φ`-stable `L_w`-subspace `0 ≠ W ≠ V` satisfies `dim_{L_w}(F ∩ W) ≥ dim_{L_w}(W)/2` lies in `B_w(K_v)`.

*Proof sketch.* Over the finite splitting field `M=L_w, V ⊗_{K_v} M = ⊕_{i=1}^r V_i` along the embeddings `τ_i = τ_1 ∘ σ_w^{−(i−1)}` of `L_w`; the `M`-linear map `φ ⊗ 1` maps `V_i` onto `V_{i+1}`, so `ψ_i := (φ ⊗ 1)^{i−1} : V_1 ≅ V_i` are similitudes. By 3.9, `F ↦ (ψ_i⁻¹(F_i))_i` identifies `H_w ⊗ M` with `LGr(V_1, ω_1)^r`. `A φ`-stable `W` gives `W ⊗ M = ⊕ W_i` with `ψ_i⁻¹(W_i) = W_1` for all `i` (the cyclic identification `V_r → V_1` is not used), and `dim(F_i ∩ W_i) = dim_{L_w}(F ∩ W)`; so the image of `F` lies in `E_r` of LV20 Lemma 6.4. Let `B_w` be the image of `E_r` under the finite surjection `H_w ⊗ M → H_w`; it is closed by 7.3 and has dimension `dim E_r < r·d(d+1)/2`. (LV20, §6, Lemma 6.3, p. 33; §6, proof of Lemma 6.3, p. 33.) *Needs:* 0.1; 2.11; 3.9; 7.3; 7.4; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0.

**Checks.**

- For `d=1` and `φ^r` with distinct eigenvalues, at most two `φ`-stable `L_w`-lines can be bad filtrations. The particular descended bad locus constructed in the proof can have positive dimension; it need not itself be finite.
- The hypothesis applies to `φ₀^{f_v}` for the crystalline Frobenius `φ₀` on a factor `V₀` of Layer LV.4, with `c = p^{f_v}`.

### 7.6 Bad points produce a Frobenius-stable subspace with large Hodge filtration (Sublemma in LV20 §6)

**Lemma.** In setting `Q`, let `y ∈ Y(K)` with `size_v(π^{-1}(y)) < 1/(d + 1)` be bad: for every `(y', w)` over `(y, v)` with `[K(y')_w : K_v] ≥ 8, ρ_{y'}` is not simple. Then some `(y', w)` over `(y, v)` with `[K(y')_w : K_v] ≥ 8` has a `φ`-stable `K(y')_w`-subspace `0 ≠ W^dR ≠ H¹_dR(X_{y'}/K(y')_w)` with `dim F¹W^dR ≥ dim(W^dR)/2`, where `F¹W^dR = W^dR ∩ F¹`. Assume `d ≥ 1` throughout this criterion.

*Proof sketch.* For each closed point `y'` over `y` choose a nonzero subrepresentation `W_{y'} ⊆ ρ_{y'}` of minimal dimension, and for `w | v` let `W^dR_{y',w} := D_cris(W_{y'}|G_{K(y')_w}) ⊆ H¹_dR(X_{y'}/K(y')_w)` (Layer LV.4), `φ`-stable with the induced filtration; its `K(y')_w`-dimension is `dim W_{y'}`. (6.8): if `[K(y')_w : K_v] ≥ 8` then `ρ_{y'}` is not simple; it carries a nondegenerate alternating pairing preserved up to the cyclotomic character, so `dim W_{y'} ≤ d` (Layer LV.0). All places of `K(y')` above `p` are good (no place of `S` lies above `p`), so `W_{y'}` is crystalline there, pure of weight 1 and finitely ramified; LV20 Lemma 2.10 (1.15) at the friendly place `v` gives (6.9): `Σ_{w|v} [K(y')_w : K_v]·a_{y',w} = [K(y') : K]/2` with `a_{y',w} = dim F¹W^dR_{y',w}/dim W^dR_{y',w}`. Suppose `a_{y',w} < 1/2`, hence `a_{y',w} ≤ 1/2 − 1/(2d)`, for all pairs of local degree `≥ 8`. Summing (6.9) over `y'` and using `a ≤ 1` for the other pairs gives `(1/2)·Σ_{deg<8} deg ≥ (1/(2d))·Σ_{deg≥8} deg` (LV20 (6.11)). By the orbit–place dictionary the local degrees are the Frobenius orbit sizes on `π^{-1}(y)(K̄)`, so `d·#small ≥ #large`, i.e. `size_v(π^{-1}(y)) ≥ 1/(d + 1)`, a contradiction. (LV20, §6, proof of Lemma 6.1, p. 31; §6, proof of Lemma 6.1, p. 32.) *Needs:* 0.13; 1.5; 1.15; 1.16; 4.1; 7.1; 7.2; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.1.

**Checks.**

- For `π=id, size_v=1`, so the size hypothesis fails even when `d=1`. The `S`-unit generic-simplicity result uses a separate argument and is not a special case of this sublemma.
- If all orbits have size `≥ 8 (size_v = 0)`, the counting forces `a_{y',w} ≥ 1/2` for some pair outright.

### 7.7 Generic simplicity along the family (LV20 Lemma 6.1)

**Lemma.** In setting `Q`, let `y₀ ∈ Y(K)` with `size_v(π^{-1}(y₀)) < 1/(d + 1)` and `Ω_v = Ω_v(y₀)`. There is a finite set `F` of points `y ∈ Ω_v ∩ Y(K)` with `size_v(π^{-1}(y)) < 1/(d + 1)` such that every other such `y` has a pair `(y', w)` over `(y, v)` with `[K(y')_w : K_v] ≥ 8` and `ρ_{y'}` simple. Assume `d ≥ 1` throughout this criterion.

*Proof sketch.* Let `y` be bad. The sublemma gives `(y', w)` of local degree `≥ 8` and `W^dR`; let `(y₀', w₀)` be the corresponding pair over `(y₀, v)`, which has the same local degree (the transport identifies `K(y')_w` with `L₀ = K(y₀')_{w₀}`). Transport `W^dR` by `T_v(y)⁻¹` to a `φ₀`-stable subspace `W₀` of the factor `V₀` (4.2); then `dim(pr₀Φ_v(y) ∩ W₀) = dim F¹W^dR ≥ dim(W₀)/2`. As `φ₀` is `σ_{L₀}`-semilinear and a similitude, `φ₀^{f_v}` satisfies the hypotheses of 7.5 (LV20 Lemma 6.3) over `K_v`, and `W₀` is `φ₀^{f_v}`-stable; so `pr₀Φ_v(y) ∈ B_{(y₀',w₀)}`. Under full monodromy the closure of `pr₀Φ_v(Ω_v)` is `H₀`, of dimension `> dim B_{(y₀',w₀)}`; by 3.19 (finite preimages, part `(c)`) only finitely many `y ∈ Ω_v` have `pr₀Φ_v(y) ∈ B_{(y₀',w₀)}`. Take `F` to be the union over the finitely many pairs `(y₀', w₀)` of local degree `≥ 8` (Lawrence and Venkatesh fix one pair; the union is what 7.6 requires). (LV20, §6, Lemma 6.1, p. 30; §6, proof of Lemma 6.1, p. 32.) *Needs:* 2.11; 3.18; 3.19; 4.2; 7.5; 7.6; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5.

**Checks.**

- For `π=id`, the size hypothesis is never satisfied; this criterion gives no assertion about the Legendre family itself.
- If no pair over `(y₀, v)` has local degree `≥ 8` then `size_v(π^{-1}(y₀)) = 1`, so `y₀` is excluded by hypothesis.

### 7.8 Galois representations really vary in the family (LV20 Lemma 6.2)

**Lemma.** In setting `Q`, let `y₀ ∈ Y(K)` and `Ω_v = Ω_v(y₀)`. Fix a finite extension `K'_v/K_v` with `[K'_v : K_v] ≥ 8` and a representation `ρ₀` of `G_{K'_v}`. Only finitely many `y ∈ Ω_v ∩ Y(K)` admit a pair `(y', w)` over `(y, v)` with `(K(y')_w, ρ_{y',w}) ≅ (K'_v, ρ₀)`. Assume `d ≥ 1` throughout this criterion.

*Proof sketch.* For each of the finitely many pairs `(y₀', w₀)` over `(y₀, v)` with local degree `[K'_v : K_v]`, apply the finiteness criterion 4.4 to the set of `y` whose corresponding pair realizes the class `(K'_v, ρ₀)` (a single class of pairs). Its hypothesis holds: under full monodromy `dim Z₀ = [K'_v : K_v]·d(d+1)/2 ≥ 4d(d+1) > 4d² ≥ c`. (LV20, §6, Lemma 6.2, p. 31.) *Needs:* 0.6; 3.18; 4.4; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5.

**Checks.**

- `d = 1` and `[K'_v : K_v] = 8: c ≤ 4 < 8 = dim H₀`.
- The conditions `(i)`, (ii) of LV20 Lemma 6.1 are not needed for this finiteness.

### 7.9 Rational points on the base of an abelian-by-finite family (LV20 Proposition 5.3)

**Theorem.** In setting `Q` (so `d ≥ 1`), the set `Y(K)* := {y ∈ Y(K) : size_v(π^{-1}(y)) < 1/(d + 1)}` is finite.

*Proof sketch.* `Y(K) = 𝒴(𝒪)` meets finitely many residue disks at `v`; for each disk meeting `Y(K)*` choose `y₀ ∈ Y(K)*` in it. It suffices to show `Ω_v(y₀) ∩ Y(K)*` is finite. By 7.7 (LV20 Lemma 6.1), outside a finite set every `y ∈ Ω_v ∩ Y(K)*` has a pair `(y', w)` with `[K(y')_w : K_v] ≥ 8` and `ρ_{y'}` simple. `K(y')` has degree `≤ deg π` over `K` and is unramified outside `S` (`𝒴' → 𝒴` is finite étale over `𝒪_S`), so it lies in finitely many isomorphism classes (Hermite–Minkowski). For each such field, the simple representations `ρ_{y'}` (dimension `2d`, unramified outside the places above `S` and `p`, pure of weight 1 with integral Frobenius polynomials) lie in finitely many classes (Faltings's lemma, Layer LV.1). So the pairs `(K(y'), ρ_{y'})`, and the local pairs `(K(y')_w, ρ_{y',w})` with `w | v`, lie in finitely many classes. 7.8 (LV20 Lemma 6.2) applied to each of these local classes of degree `≥ 8` leaves finitely many `y`. (LV20, §5, Proposition 5.3, p. 26; §6, p. 31.) *Needs:* 1.2; 1.16; 2.2; 2.5; 7.1; 7.7; 7.8; ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1 (Hermite–Minkowski); NeronModelsAndSemistableAbelianVarieties §5.1.

**Checks.**

- Layer LV.11 applies the proposition to the Kodaira–Parshin family, for which `size_v(π^{-1}(y)) < 1/(d_q + 1)` for all `y ∈ Y(K)`, so `Y(K)* = Y(K)`.
- For `π = id, size_v(π^{-1}(y)) = 1` for all `y` and the proposition is empty, which is why Lawrence and Venkatesh pass to abelian-by-finite families.

### Examples

- A single `7`-cycle has `size_v = 1` and a single `8`-cycle has `size_v = 0`; one fixed point together with
  one `8`-cycle has `size_v = 1/9`. The proportion counts geometric points, so averaging two local factors
  without their degrees is wrong.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.1, LV.2, LV.3, LV.4; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §§1.3, 1.7; PadicHodgeTheory §§3.2–3.4; WeightsInEtaleCohomology Layer R34.1; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; ArithmeticGaloisDuality §R02.3.1; NeronModelsAndSemistableAbelianVarieties §5.1; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.8: Hurwitz spaces and the Kodaira–Parshin family

For a curve `Y` of genus at least two and a centre-free finite group `G`, the `G`-covers of `Y` branched
at one moving point form a finite étale family `Y' → Y` whose fibre over `y` is the set of conjugacy
classes of surjections from `π₁(Y ∖ {y})` to `G` that are nontrivial on the peripheral class. The complex
construction extends the action of the fibre group to the configuration group, builds the cover of the
configuration space and compactifies it; Riemann existence algebraizes it and the arithmetic outer
action descends it to `K`. For `G = Aff(q)` the reduced Prym — the identity component of the kernel of
`#G·(1 − e_H + e_G)` on the relative Picard scheme — is a polarized abelian scheme over `Y'`, and this is the
Kodaira–Parshin family, of relative dimension `(2g − 1)(q − 1)/2`. Its fibre over a rational point maps
`G_K`-equivariantly onto the generating tuples in `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q − 1))` with fibres of constant size.

### 8.1 The complex points of a curve form a closed surface of the same genus

**Lemma.** Let `Y` be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g` over a subfield `K ⊆ ℂ`. Then `Y(ℂ)` is a connected closed orientable surface of genus `g`; for `y ∈ Y(ℂ), π₁(Y(ℂ) ∖ {y})` is free of rank `2g` with the peripheral class as in Layer LV.5, and `H₁(Y(ℂ); ℤ) ≅ ℤ^{2g}`.

*Proof sketch.* `Y(ℂ)` is a compact Riemann surface, hence a closed orientable surface, and it is connected (ComplexComparisonPartII C4). By the algebraic de Rham–Betti comparison, `dim_ℂ H¹(Y(ℂ), ℂ) = dim H¹_dR(Y/K) = dim H⁰(Y, Ω¹) + dim H¹(Y, 𝒪) = 2g` (Hodge exact sequence, Serre duality and the definition of the genus). The classification of surfaces gives the topological type; the statements on `π₁` are those of the configuration fibration 5.23. (LV20, §5, p. 26.) *Needs:* 5.4; 5.23; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2; ComplexComparisonPartII §C5.2 (relative Hodge bundles); TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `B`; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicCurves Layer `12 (12E)`.

**Checks.**

- An elliptic curve: `Y(ℂ)` is a torus.
- A smooth plane quartic: a closed surface of genus 3.

### 8.2 Surjections nontrivial on a peripheral class and their symmetries (LV20 Lemma 7.4, group part)

**Definition.** Let `Γ` be a group (or a profinite group, with continuous homomorphisms), `G` a finite group with trivial centre and `c` a set of elements of `Γ` closed under conjugation. `S(Γ, c, G)` is the set of surjective homomorphisms `φ : Γ → G` with `φ(γ) ≠ 1` for `γ ∈ c`. It carries commuting actions of `Γ, γ·φ := φ ∘ Ad(γ)⁻¹`, and of `G, φ·h := Ad(h⁻¹) ∘ φ`; `G` acts freely, and `S(Γ, c, G)/G` is the set of `G`-conjugacy classes of such surjections, on which `Γ` acts trivially. The stabilizer of `φ` in `Γ × G^op` is `{(γ, h) : h⁻¹ = φ(γ)}`, and `φ` is determined by its stabilizer (`singlyRamifiedSurjections_conj_free`).

- `singlyRamifiedSurjections`: `S(Γ, c, G)` as a set of (continuous) surjective homomorphisms nontrivial on `c`.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.actLeft`: The `Γ`-action `γ·φ = φ ∘ Ad(γ)⁻¹`.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.actRight`: The `G`-action `φ·h = Ad(h⁻¹) ∘ φ`, free when `Z(G) = 1`.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.quotient`: `S(Γ, c, G)/G`, the conjugacy classes, with trivial `Γ`-action.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.stabilizer_eq`: `Stab_{Γ × G^op}(φ) = {(γ, φ(γ)⁻¹)}`.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.comap`: For a surjection `Γ → Γ̄` through which the `Γ`-action factors, `S(Γ̄, c̄, G) = S(Γ, c, G)`.
- `singlyRamifiedSurjections.aff3`: The `Aff(3)` count 810/135.

*Construction.* `γ·φ = Ad(φ(γ))⁻¹ ∘ φ`, so the `Γ`-action preserves `G`-orbits and commutes with the `G`-action. `φ·h = φ` means `Ad(h⁻¹)` is trivial on the image `G`, so `h` is central, hence `h = 1`. `(γ, h)` fixes `φ` iff `Ad(φ(γ)h)⁻¹ ∘ φ = φ`, iff `φ(γ)h` is central (`φ` is surjective), i.e. `h = φ(γ)⁻¹`; so `φ` is recovered from its stabilizer as the map sending `γ` to the inverse of its partner `h`. (LV20, §7.3, proof of Lemma 7.4, p. 38.) *Needs:* 0.9; 0.10.

**Checks.**

- For `Γ` free of rank `4, c` its product-of-commutators peripheral class and `G=Aff(3), |S|=810` and `|S/G|=135`.
- For `c` containing the identity, `S` is empty.
- If the target has nontrivial centre, its conjugation action on surjections is not free; that case is excluded from the free-action conclusion.
- `G = S₃ = Aff(3), Γ` free on `x₁, x₁', x₂, x₂'` and `c` the conjugacy class of `[x₁, x₁'][x₂, x₂']`: #`S(Γ, c, G) = 810` and #`S/G = 135` (LV20 Lemma 2.11 in Layer LV.0).
- If `G` had a nontrivial centre `Z`, the `G`-action would not be free (`Z` acts trivially).

### 8.3 Unique extension of the action to an overgroup

**Lemma.** In the setting of singly ramified surjections 8.2, let `Γ̃ ⊇ Γ̄` be a group containing a normal subgroup `Γ̄` through which the `Γ`-action on `S = S(Γ, c, G)` factors (via a surjection `Γ → Γ̄` with image `c̄` of `c`), and suppose conjugation by `Γ̃` preserves `c̄`. Then every `φ ∈ S` factors through `Γ̄`, and `γ̃·φ := φ ∘ Ad(γ̃)⁻¹|_{Γ̄}` defines an action of `Γ̃` on `S` commuting with `G` and extending the `Γ̄`-action. It is the only such action: any action of `Γ̃` on `S` commuting with `G` and extending the `Γ̄`-action is given by this formula.

*Proof sketch.* If `n ∈ ker(Γ → Γ̄)`, then `n` acts trivially: `Ad(φ(n))⁻¹ ∘ φ = φ`, so `φ(n)` is central, hence trivial; so `φ` factors through `Γ̄`. The formula is an action commuting with `G`, and it preserves surjectivity and nontriviality on `c̄` because `Ad(γ̃)` preserves `Γ̄` and `c̄`. Uniqueness: for an action `⋆` commuting with `G` and extending the `Γ̄`-action, the stabilizer of `γ̃ ⋆ φ` in `Γ̄ × G^op` is the conjugate by `γ̃` of the stabilizer of `φ` (since `Γ̄` is normal and the actions commute); a surjection is determined by its stabilizer, and `φ ∘ Ad(γ̃)⁻¹` has that stabilizer. (LV20, §7.3, proof of Lemma 7.4, p. 38.) *Needs:* 8.2.

**Checks.**

- `Γ̃ = Γ̄ ⋊ ℤ` with `ℤ` acting by an automorphism preserving `c̄`: the extended action is precomposition with the inverse automorphism.
- Profinite version: for `Γ̄` the geometric and `Γ̃` the arithmetic étale fundamental group of a punctured curve over `K`, with `c̄` the inertia generators at the puncture (a set stable under the cyclotomic twist `σ(ι) = ι^{χ(σ)}`).

### 8.4 Normalization, finite covers and analytification

**Lemma.** Let `X` be a normal connected scheme of finite type over `ℂ` and `U ⊆ X` a dense open subscheme. `(i)` A scheme of finite type over `ℂ` is normal (resp. regular, reduced, of dimension `n`) if and only if its analytification is. (ii) Restriction to `U` is fully faithful on normal finite covers of `X`, and the functor `X′ ↦ X′^an` from finite étale covers of `U` to finite étale analytic covers of `U^an` is an equivalence (Riemann existence). (iii) If `X′ → X` is the normalization of `X` in a finite étale cover `U′ → U`, then `X′^an → X^an` is the normalization of `X^an` in `U′^an`, the unique normal analytic space finite over `X^an` extending `U′^an`; in particular `X′^an` is smooth wherever the analytic local model is smooth.

*Proof sketch.* Normality and regularity are detected on completed local rings, which agree for `X` and `X^an`; the extension of finite covers across a closed analytic subset of codimension at least one is Grauert–Remmert’s theorem, unique for normal spaces; algebraic and analytic normalizations both satisfy that uniqueness. (SGA1, Exposé XII, Proposition 2.1, p. 242; Théorème 5.1, p. 251; Proposition 5.3, p. 254; Théorème 5.4, p. 255.) *Needs:* ComplexComparisonPartII §C0.4; ComplexComparisonPartII Layer C2; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.3, IG.3.1; Mathlib `AlgebraicGeometry.Scheme.Hom.normalization`.

### 8.5 The complex Hurwitz space of `G`-covers branched at one point (LV20 §7.3)

**Construction.** `Y` is a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over a number field `K ⊂ ℂ`, and `G` is a finite group with trivial centre. Fix `y₀ ≠ y₁` in `Y(ℂ), Γ := π₁(Y(ℂ) ∖ {y₀}, y₁), c` the conjugacy class of a small loop around `y₀`, and `Γ̃ := π₁(F(Y(ℂ)), (y₁, y₀))` for the configuration space `F(Y(ℂ)) = Y(ℂ)² ∖ Δ`. The `Γ̃ × G`-set `S(Γ, c, G)` (8.3, with `Γ ⊴ Γ̃` by Layer LV.5) defines a covering `Z° → F(Y(ℂ))` with free `G`-action, whose quotient `Z°/G` is the pullback along `(y, y') ↦ y'` of a finite covering `e : Y' → Y(ℂ)` with fibres `S(y) := S(π₁(Y(ℂ) ∖ {y}), c_y, G)/G`. So `Z° → Y' × Y(ℂ) ∖ Γ_e` (`Γ_e` the graph of `e`) is a `G`-torsor. By Riemann existence `Y'` is a smooth projective curve and `Z°` is algebraic; `Z :=` the normalization of `Y' × Y` in `Z°` is a smooth projective surface with a `G`-action and a finite morphism `f : Z → Y' × Y`, and the composite `Z → Y'` is a smooth proper relative curve whose fibre `Z_{y'} → Y` over `y'` is the connected `G`-covering classified by `y' ∈ S(e(y'))`, branched exactly at `e(y')`; near `f⁻¹(Γ_e), f` is `(z, w) ↦ (z, wⁿ)` in suitable local coordinates. The finite étale parameter scheme `Y′` may be disconnected (or empty for some `G`); it is not required to be a geometrically connected curve. Its individual relative curve fibres are connected.

- `hurwitzComplex`: The complex curve `Y'` with `e : Y' → Y` and the `G`-covering `Z → Y' × Y`.
- `hurwitzComplex.fibre`: `e⁻¹(y) ≃ S(y)` naturally in `y`.
- `hurwitzComplex.torsor`: `Z → Y' × Y` is a `G`-torsor off the graph `Γ_e`.
- `hurwitzComplex.localForm`: Near `Γ_e, Z → Y' × Y` is `(z, w) ↦ (z, wⁿ)`.
- `hurwitzComplex.relativeCurve`: `Z → Y'` is smooth and proper, with fibre `Z_{y'}` the `G`-cover branched at `e(y')`.
- `hurwitzComplex.configuration`: `Z°` is the covering of the configuration space attached to `S(Γ, c, G)`; its monodromy is described by 5.26.
- `hurwitzComplex.aff3`: Degree 135 for `Aff(3)` and `g = 2`.

*Construction.* The `Γ̃`-action on `S(Γ, c, G)` exists by 8.3 (`Γ̄ = Γ` is normal in `Γ̃` and `Γ̃` preserves `c`, Layer LV.5); covering-space theory gives `Z°` with fibre `S` at `(y₁, y₀)` and `G` acting freely. `Γ` acts trivially on `S/G`, so the `Γ̃`-action on `S/G` factors through `π₁(Y(ℂ), y₀)` (Layer LV.5 exact sequence), and `Z°/G` is the pullback of the covering `e` with fibre `S/G`. Riemann existence (SGA 1 XII 5.1): finite étale coverings of the analytifications of `Y` and of `Y' × Y ∖ Γ_e` are algebraic; `Y'` is proper because it is finite over `Y`. Local structure: in coordinates `(z, u)` on `Y' × Y` near `Γ_e` with `Γ_e = {u = ẽ(z)}` (`e` is étale), put `w := u − ẽ(z)`; over the punctured polydisk the `G`-torsor is a disjoint union of copies of `(z, w') ↦ (z, w'ⁿ)`, where `n` is the order of `φ(c)`, and its normalization over the polydisk is the same map on the full polydisk, smooth over `z`. The normalization of `Y' × Y` in `Z°` analytifies to the normalization of the analytic space (ComplexComparisonPartII C0/C4), which is the space just described; so `Z → Y'` is smooth and proper with the stated fibres. (LV20, §7.3, p. 36; §7.3, p. 37; SGA1, Exposé XII, Théorème 5.1, p. 251.) *Needs:* 3.10; 5.23; 8.1; 8.3; 8.4; ComplexComparisonPartII §C0.4; ComplexComparisonPartII Layer C2; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.3, IG.3.1; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.CoveringSpace.monodromyEquivalence`; Mathlib `AlgebraicGeometry.Scheme.Hom.normalization`; Mathlib `CommAlgCat.FiniteEtale`; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- For `g=2` and `G=Aff(3), Y′→Y` has degree 135.
- For `G=1` (centre-free), `S` is empty and `Y′` is empty; no branched cover is manufactured.
- Off the graph of `Y′→Y`, the constructed map is a `G`-torsor; at the graph its local model is `(z,w)↦(z,w^n)` with `n>1` for the nontrivial peripheral image.
- `G = Aff(3) = S₃, g = 2: e : Y' → Y` has degree 135.
- For `G` abelian the construction does not apply (nontrivial centre), and indeed abelian covers cannot be branched at a single point (the peripheral class is a product of commutators).

### 8.6 Descent of the Hurwitz covering to `K` (LV20 Lemma 7.4)

**Lemma.** `Y` is a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over a number field `K ⊂ ℂ`, and `G` is a finite group with trivial centre. Let `Z° → (Y² ∖ Δ)_ℂ` be the `G`-covering of the complex Hurwitz construction 8.5. (1) It extends uniquely, with its `G`-action, to a finite étale `G`-covering `Z°_K → (Y² ∖ Δ)_K`. (2) For `y₀ ∈ Y(K)`, the set `S(y₀)`, defined with the geometric étale fundamental group of `Y_{K̄} ∖ {y₀}` and its `G_K`-action through the outer action, is `G_K`-equivariantly identified with the fibre over `y₀` of the covering `Y'_K` of (3). `(3) Z°_K/G` extends uniquely to a finite étale covering of `Y_K × Y_K` of the form `Y'_K × Y_K → Y_K × Y_K`, for a finite étale covering `Y'_K → Y_K` with `(Y'_K)_ℂ = Y'`.

*Proof sketch.* Étale fundamental groups: `Γ̄ :=` image of `π₁^et(Y_{K̄} ∖ {y₀}, y₁)` in `Γ̃^geom := π₁^et((Y² ∖ Δ)_{K̄}, (y₁, y₀))`, which is the kernel of `Γ̃^geom → π₁^et(Y_{K̄}, y₀)`: the étale sequence is the profinite completion of the topological one (Riemann existence and invariance of `π₁` under `K̄ ⊆ ℂ`) and profinite completion is right exact. Only this middle exactness is used; injectivity on the left is not needed. The `Γ̃^geom`-action on the fibre `S` of `Z°` (a finite continuous set) restricts on `Γ̄` to the conjugation action, so by 8.3 every `φ` factors through `Γ̄` and the `Γ̃^geom`-action is given by the conjugation formula. `Γ̄` is normal in the arithmetic group `Γ̃ := π₁^et((Y² ∖ Δ)_K, (y₁, y₀))` (it is the intersection of `Γ̃^geom` with the kernel to `π₁^et(Y_K)`), and conjugation by `Γ̃` preserves the set of inertia generators at `y₀` up to the cyclotomic character, hence preserves 'nontrivial on `c'`. The conjugation formula extends the action to `Γ̃ × G`, uniquely; by the Galois-category description of finite étale coverings (InverseGalois IG.0/IG.1) this is (1). `(3): Γ` acts trivially on `S/G`, so the `Γ̃`-action on `S/G` factors through `π₁^et(Y_K, y₀)`, which gives `Y'_K`; uniqueness holds because finite étale coverings are determined by their `Γ̃`-sets. (2): restrict `Z°_K/G` to the `K`-subvariety `(Y ∖ {y₀}) × {y₀}`; it is the constant covering with fibre `Y'_{K,y₀}`, on which `π₁^et((Y ∖ {y₀})_K)` acts through `G_K`. The same restriction of `Z°_K` has fibre `S` with the conjugation action (8.3), whose quotient by `G` is `S(y₀)` with the outer `G_K`-action. No `K`-rational point `y₁` is needed. (LV20, §7.3, Lemma 7.4, p. 37; §7.3, proof of Lemma 7.4, p. 38; §7.3, proof of Lemma 7.4, p. 39.) *Needs:* 5.23; 8.3; 8.5; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups Layer IG.0; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.2–IG.1.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.0.A1–F.0.A2; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.3, IG.3.1; Mathlib `CategoryTheory.PreGaloisCategory.IsFundamentalGroup`; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.FiniteCoveringSpace.instProfiniteCompletionIsFundamentalGroup`.

**Checks.**

- If `Y(K) = {y₀}, (2)` still holds: the `G_K`-action on `S(y₀)` is defined through the outer action.
- For `G = Aff(q)` and `y₀ ∈ Y(K)`, the fibre map (5.5) of this layer is a `G_K`-equivariant map from `S(y₀)` to `H¹(Y_{K̄}, ℤ/(q − 1))`.

### 8.7 Hurwitz spaces of `G`-covers branched at one point (LV20 Proposition 7.1)

**Theorem.** `Y` is a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over a number field `K ⊂ ℂ`, and `G` is a finite group with trivial centre. There are a finite étale morphism `π : Y' → Y` of `K`-curves and a smooth proper relative curve `Z → Y'` with a morphism `Z → Y' × Y` of relative curves over `Y'` and a `G`-action on `Z` over `Y' × Y` such that: `(i)` for `y ∈ Y(K̄)` there is a bijection between `π⁻¹(y)` and the set of `G`-conjugacy classes of surjections `π₁^geom(Y ∖ {y}) → G` nontrivial on a loop around `y, G_K`-equivariant when `y ∈ Y(K)`; (ii) `Z → Y' × Y` is a `G`-torsor away from the graph of `π`, and for `y' ∈ Y'(K̄)` the fibre `Z_{y'} → Y` is the connected `G`-covering classified by `y'`, ramified exactly at `π(y')`. The finite étale parameter scheme `Y′` may be disconnected (or empty for some `G`); it is not required to be a geometrically connected curve. Its individual relative curve fibres are connected.

*Proof sketch.* Take `Y' := Y'_K` from 8.6 and `Z :=` the normalization of `Y' × Y` in `Z°_K`. Normalization commutes with the base change `K → ℂ` in characteristic zero (normality is preserved by separable field extensions), so `Z_ℂ` is the complex `Z` of the analytic construction; smoothness and properness of `Z → Y'` and the fibre description descend from `ℂ`. `(i)` is 8.6 (2) together with the analytic identification of fibres; (ii) holds over `ℂ` and hence over `K`. (LV20, §7.1, Proposition 7.1, p. 34; §7.1, Proposition 7.1, p. 35; §7.3, p. 37.) *Needs:* 8.4; 8.5; 8.6; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; Mathlib `AlgebraicGeometry.Scheme.Hom.normalization`.

**Checks.**

- `G = Aff(3), g = 2: π` has degree 135.
- The fibres of `Z → Y'` have genus `1 + |G|(g − 1) + (|G| − |G|/n)/2` by Riemann–Hurwitz, `n` the order of the monodromy at the branch point.

### 8.8 The idempotents `e, e′` and `e`″ of `ℚ[Aff(q)]`

**Lemma.** For a prime `q ≥ 3, G = Aff(q)` and `H = H_q` the stabilizer of 0, use the supplied averages `e_H := TauCeti.groupAlgebraSubgroupAverage ℚ H` and `e_G := TauCeti.groupAlgebraSubgroupAverage ℚ ⊤`, `e := e_H − e_G, e' := 1 − e` and `e'' :=` #`G·e' ∈ ℤ[G]`. Then `e`, `e'` are idempotents, `e_G e_H = e_H e_G = e_G`, and all four are fixed by the anti-involution `g ↦ g⁻¹`. For every `ℚ[G]`-module `M: e·M = M^H ∩ ker(e_G)`, which is a complement of `M^G` in `M^H`, and `ker(e'' : M → M) = e·M` (`averagingIdempotent_top_mul`, `isIdempotentElem_averagingIdempotent_sub`).

*Proof sketch.* The supplied averaging theorem applies to `H` and `G`, and `e_G e_H = e_H e_G = e_G` because `G ⊇ H`. `(e_H − e_G)² = e_H − 2e_G + e_G = e_H − e_G`; `e'` is the complementary idempotent. `e'' =` #`G −` (#`G`/#`H`)`Σ_{h∈H} h + Σ_{g∈G} g` has integer coefficients. `e·M ⊆ M^H ∩ ker e_G`, and conversely `x` in the intersection satisfies `e·x = x`; `ker e'' = ker e' = e·M`. (LV20, §7.2, p. 36.) *Needs:* 0.7.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3: G = S₃, H = ⟨(1 2)⟩`; on the permutation module `ℚ³, e` projects onto the 1-dimensional complement of the constants in `(ℚ³)^H`.
- On the regular representation, `dim e·ℚ[G] =` #`(G/H) − 1 = q − 1`.

### 8.9 The connected kernel of an endomorphism of an abelian scheme

**Lemma.** Let `B` be a smooth connected scheme of finite type over a field of characteristic zero, `A → B` an abelian scheme and `ε` an endomorphism of `A` satisfying `ε²=[n]ε` for some positive integer `n`. Suppose the fibres `ker(ε_b)` have constant dimension. Then the reduced identity component `(ker ε)°` is an abelian subscheme of `A` over `B`, smooth over `B`, whose fibre at every point `b` is `(ker ε_b)°`, and its formation commutes with base change along `B′ → B`. A polarization of `A` restricts to a polarization of `(ker ε)°`, which need not be principal.

*Proof sketch.* Over every geometric point the kernel is a smooth proper group scheme in characteristic zero, and its identity component is an abelian variety (Mil-AV, Chapter `I`, proof of Proposition 10.1, p. 42). Apply the section-component criterion `spaceSectionComponentCriterion` of AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §7.9 to the proper finite-presentation group scheme `ker ε`: its geometrically reduced fibres, locally constant identity-component dimension and reduced base give an open, smooth relative identity component, compatible with arbitrary base change; the proper-component clause of that criterion, using the proper geometric component fibres and separated ambient kernel, makes it proper and closed in `A`. Thus it is an abelian subscheme. This argument uses that supplier’s universal-openness and flatness results; it does not infer flatness from a purported constant Hilbert polynomial of a generic-fibre closure. Restrict the ample polarization. The relation `ε²=[n]ε` identifies the particular scaled-idempotent kernels needed for Pryms. (LV20, §7.2, (7.3) and Definition 7.3, p. 36; Mil-AV, Chapter `I`, proof of Proposition 10.1, p. 42.) *Needs:* AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §7.9 (`spaceSectionComponentCriterion`, including smoothness over a reduced base and properness); AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A0.1, A1.1; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A2.5.

**Checks.**

- `ε=0` has connected kernel `A`; `ε=[n]` has finite étale kernel and zero-dimensional identity component.
- On `E×E, ε=[n]` on the second factor and zero on the first has connected kernel `E×{0}` and satisfies `ε²=[n]ε`.
- The restriction of a product polarization of degrees 1 and `m` to the second factor has degree `m²`, so the restricted polarization is not asserted to be principal.
- A nonreduced base is not covered by the reduced-base smoothness criterion; subsequent base change preserves the already constructed abelian scheme.


### 8.10 The reduced relative Prym of a family of `Aff(q)`-covers

**Construction.** Let `q ≥ 3` be prime, `B` a smooth scheme of finite type over a field of characteristic zero and `Z → B` a smooth proper relative curve with geometrically connected fibres and an action of `G = Aff(q)` over `B`. Let `P := Pic⁰_{Z/B}`, an abelian scheme with its canonical principal polarization, on which `G` acts (by pushforward; the idempotents are invariant under `g ↦ g⁻¹`, so the choice does not matter), and `e'' ∈ End(P)`. If the dimension of `ker(e''_b)` is constant in `b ∈ B`, the reduced Prym is `X := (ker e'')°`, the relative identity component, an abelian subscheme of `P` with the restricted polarization; its fibre at `b` is `(ker e''_b)°`.

- `reducedPrym`: `X := (ker e'')° ⊆ Pic⁰_{Z/B}` for a relative curve with `Aff(q)`-action and constant kernel dimension.
- `reducedPrym.isAbelianScheme`: `X` is an abelian scheme over `B`.
- `reducedPrym.polarization`: The restriction of the principal polarization of `Pic⁰`.
- `reducedPrym.fibre`: `X_b = (ker e''_b)°` for every point `b`.
- `reducedPrym.baseChange`: Formation of `X` commutes with base change on `B`.
- `reducedPrym.dim`: The relative dimension of `X` over a base whose complex fibres are singly ramified `Aff(q)`-covers of a genus-`g` curve is `(2g − 1)(q − 1)/2`.
- `reducedPrym.example`: `q = 3, g = 2`: relative dimension 3.

*Construction.* Relative Picard scheme of a smooth proper curve with geometrically connected fibres: `Pic⁰_{Z/B}` is an abelian scheme with principal polarization (JacobianChallenge Layer `D`, AlgebraicModuli A0-extension, AbelianSchemes A2). Functoriality gives the `G`-action and `e'' ∈ ℤ[G] → End(P)`. Writing `n=|G|` and `e''=n(1−e)`, the idempotence `e²=e` gives `(e'')²=[n]e''`. Apply 8.9 on each connected component of the smooth base; the polarization restricts and formation commutes with base change. (LV20, §7.2, p. 36; §7.2, p. 35.) *Needs:* 8.8; 8.9; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §7.9; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A0.1, A1.1; JacobianChallengePartII §§1.2–1.4; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `D`; JacobianChallengePartII §§1.2–1.4.

**Checks.**

- For singly branched `Aff(3)`-covers of a genus-two curve, the reduced Prym has dimension 3.
- If `G` acts trivially on `Pic⁰, e=e_H−e_G=0`, so `e′′=|G|` and the connected kernel is the zero abelian scheme.
- After base change to a geometric point `b`, the reduced Prym is `(ker e′′_b)°`, with the polarization restricted from `Pic⁰`; the polarization is not automatically principal.
- If `Z_b → Z_b/G` has genus-0 quotient the Prym has dimension given by Riemann–Hurwitz for `Z_b/H`.
- For the Kodaira–Parshin family the fibre dimension is constant, equal to `(2g − 1)(q − 1)/2` (8.12).

### 8.11 Homology of the Jacobian of a compact Riemann surface

**Lemma.** Let `C` be a smooth projective connected curve of genus `g` over `ℂ` and `J = Pic⁰(C)`. After choosing a base point of `C`, the Abel–Jacobi map identifies `J(ℂ) = H⁰(C, Ω¹)^∨ / H₁(C(ℂ), ℤ)`, so `H₁(J(ℂ), ℤ) = H₁(C(ℂ), ℤ)`, compatibly with pushforward along a finite morphism of curves and with automorphisms; the Riemann form of the canonical principal polarization of `J` is the intersection form on `H₁(C(ℂ), ℤ)` (with the sign convention fixed in the Conventions section). The induced map on `H₁` is independent of the base point, because changing it translates `J` and translations act trivially on `H₁`. The same holds fibrewise for a family of curves over a complex base.

*Proof sketch.* Integration of holomorphic forms over cycles gives the period lattice `H₁(C, ℤ) ⊆ H⁰(Ω¹)^∨` (Riemann bilinear relations show it is a lattice and that the intersection form is a positive Riemann form); the Abel–Jacobi map from `Pic⁰` to the torus is an isomorphism (Abel and Jacobi inversion); the polarization of `J` is the theta divisor, whose Riemann form is the intersection pairing. (Mil-AV, Chapter III, §2, Theorem 2.5 and the preceding discussion, p. 93; §18, Theorem 18.5, p. 73.) *Needs:* AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `D`.

### 8.12 Homology of the reduced Prym is the primitive homology of the degree-`q` cover

**Lemma.** Let `Z → Y` be a connected `Aff(q)`-Galois covering of compact Riemann surfaces branched over exactly one point `y` with inertia generated by `a q`-cycle (a nontrivial translation), `g(Y) = g, C' := Z/H_q` and `π' : C' → Y` the associated degree-`q` covering. Then `H₁((ker e'')°(ℂ), ℚ) = e·H₁(Z, ℚ)` inside `H₁(Pic⁰(Z)(ℂ), ℚ) = H₁(Z, ℚ)`, and the pushforward along `p : Z → C'` restricts to an isomorphism `e·H₁(Z, ℚ) ≅ H₁^Pr(C', Y; ℚ) := ker(π'_* : H₁(C', ℚ) → H₁(Y, ℚ))` that multiplies intersection forms by `q − 1`; the Riemann form of the restricted polarization is the restriction of the intersection form up to sign. In particular `dim_ℚ H₁^Pr(C', Y) = (2g − 1)(q − 1)` and `dim (ker e'')° = (g − 1/2)(q − 1)`. These identifications are natural for homeomorphisms of `Z` commuting with `G` and covering a homeomorphism of `Y`.

*Proof sketch.* Complex uniformization: `H₁(Pic⁰(Z)(ℂ), ℤ) = H₁(Z, ℤ)` with the intersection form as Riemann form (AbelianSchemes A5, JacobianChallenge), and for an endomorphism `ε` of a complex abelian variety, `H₁((ker ε)°, ℚ) = ker`(`ε` on `H₁(·, ℚ)`); `ker e'' = e·H₁` by 8.8. `p : Z → C'` is an unramified `H_q`-Galois covering: the ramification index `q` of `Z → Y` at points over `y` equals that of `C' → Y`, whose unique point over `y` has index `q`. Transfer (TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5) gives `p_* : H₁(Z, ℚ)^{H} ≅ H₁(C', ℚ)` with `p^*p_* = Σ_{h∈H} h`, and `⟨p_*x, p_*x'⟩ = (q − 1)⟨x, x'⟩` for `H`-invariant `x, x'`. For the `G`-covering `Z → Y` (unramified off `y`, and `H₁(Y ∖ {y}) = H₁(Y)`), `Σ_{g∈G} g = π^*π_*` on `H₁(Z, ℚ)`, so `ker e_G = ker π_*`; under `p_*` this becomes `ker π'_*`, since `π_* = π'_* p_*`. Riemann–Hurwitz: `2g(C') − 2 = q(2g − 2) + (q − 1)`, so `2g(C') − 2g = (2g − 1)(q − 1)`. Naturality: all maps are induced by the coverings and commute with lifted homeomorphisms. (LV20, §8.2.3, p. 41; §7.2, p. 35.) *Needs:* 8.1; 8.8; 8.10; 8.11; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `D`; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2: C'` has genus 5 and `dim H₁^Pr(C', Y) = 6 = 2·3`.
- `H₁^Pr` is the orthogonal complement of `π'^*H₁(Y)` in `H₁(C')` (Layer LV.9).

### 8.13 The Kodaira–Parshin family (LV20 Definitions 7.2–7.3)

**Definition.** Let `Y` be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over a number field `K` and `q ≥ 3` a prime. The Kodaira–Parshin curve family `Z_q → Y'_q → Y` is the Hurwitz space of LV20 Proposition 7.1 for `G = Aff(q)`, and the Kodaira–Parshin family is `X_q → Y'_q → Y` with `X_q` the reduced Prym of `Z_q → Y'_q`. It is an abelian-by-finite family of relative dimension `d_q = (g − 1/2)(q − 1)`, it admits a good model over `𝒪_S` for some finite `S` (with `𝒴` proper), and for `y ∈ Y(K)` the fibre `π⁻¹(y)` is `G_K`-equivariantly the set of `Aff(q)`-conjugacy classes of surjections `π₁^geom(Y_{K̄} ∖ {y}) → Aff(q)` nontrivial on a loop around `y` (LV20 property (iii)).

- `kodairaParshinCurves`: `Z_q → Y'_q → Y`.
- `kodairaParshin`: `X_q → Y'_q → Y` as an abelian-by-finite family.
- `kodairaParshin.relDim`: `d_q = (g − 1/2)(q − 1)`, i.e. `2d_q = (2g − 1)(q − 1)`.
- `kodairaParshin.exists_goodModel`: A good model over some `𝒪_S` with `𝒴` proper.
- `kodairaParshin.fibreEquiv`: `π⁻¹(y)(K̄) ≃` conjugacy classes of surjections `π₁^geom(Y ∖ y) → Aff(q)` nontrivial at `y, G_K`-equivariantly for `y ∈ Y(K)`.
- `kodairaParshin.fibreHomology`: `H₁(X_{q,y'}(ℂ), ℚ) ≅ H₁^Pr(Z_{q,y'} ×^{Aff(q)} 𝔽_q, Y(ℂ); ℚ)`.
- `kodairaParshin.example`: `q = 3, g = 2: d = 3`, fibre of size 135.

*Construction.* `Aff(q)` has trivial centre (Layer LV.0), so the Hurwitz space exists. The fibre dimension of `ker e''` is constant by 8.12 (computed at complex points, which lie over every point of `Y'_q`), so the reduced Prym is an abelian scheme of relative dimension `d_q` with polarization. Good model: Layer LV.2 (existence after enlarging `S`), with `𝒴` proper because `Y` is. Property (iii) is 8.7 (LV20 Proposition 7.1 `(i)`); `Aff(q)`-conjugacy and `Sym(𝔽_q)`-conjugacy classes agree (Layer LV.0). (LV20, §7.1, Definition 7.2, p. 35; §7.2, Definition 7.3, p. 36; §5, p. 26.) *Needs:* 0.9; 2.1; 2.3; 8.7; 8.10; 8.12.

**Checks.**

- For `g=2` and `q=3`, relative dimension is 3 and the parameter fibre has 135 points.
- For `g=2` and `q=107`, relative dimension is 159.
- The associated degree-`q` curve and the `Aff(q)`-Galois curve have different degrees, `q` and `q(q−1)`; the reduced Prym uses the degree-`q` quotient, not the full Prym of the Galois curve.
- `q = 3, g = 2: d_3 = 3` and #`π⁻¹(y) = 135`.
- `q = 107, g = 2: d_q = 159`.

### 8.14 The map from Kodaira–Parshin fibres to `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q − 1))` (LV20 (5.5))

**Lemma.** Let `y ∈ Y(K), N = q − 1`, and fix a generator of `𝔽_q^×` to identify `λ : Aff(q) → 𝔽_q^×` with a surjection onto `ℤ/N`. Composition with `λ` defines a `G_K`-equivariant map `ψ : π⁻¹(y)(K̄) → M := H¹_et(Y_{K̄}, ℤ/N) = Hom(π₁^geom(Y_{K̄}), ℤ/N)`. In coordinates given by a standard generating system of the free group `π₁(Y(ℂ) ∖ {y}) (M ≅ (ℤ/N)^{2g})`, the image `Υ` of `ψ` is the set of `2g`-tuples whose entries generate `ℤ/N`, and every fibre of `ψ` over `Υ` has `q^{2g−2}` elements. In particular #`π⁻¹(y) = q^{2g−2}·J_{2g}(N)`.

*Proof sketch.* For a surjection `φ` nontrivial at `y, φ(c)` lies in `[Aff(q), Aff(q)] = 𝔽_q^+` because the peripheral class is a product of commutators; so `λ ∘ φ` kills the peripheral class and factors through `π₁^geom(Y_{K̄})` (the kernel of `π₁(Y ∖ y) → π₁(Y)` is normally generated by `c`, also after profinite completion). `λ ∘ φ` depends only on the conjugacy class of `φ` since `ℤ/N` is abelian. Equivariance: `G_K` acts on both sides through its outer action on the geometric fundamental groups, and trivially on `ℤ/N`. Homomorphisms to finite groups from the profinite completion of a free group are homomorphisms from the free group; in the standard generators, surjections nontrivial at `y` are the generating tuples `g ∈ Aff(q)^{2g}` with `∏[g_i, g_i'] ≠ 0`, and LV20 Lemma 2.11 (Layer LV.0) shows that the image of `Λ` is the set of generating tuples of `𝔽_q^×` and each fibre has `q^{2g−1}(q − 1)` tuples. `Aff(q)` acts freely by conjugation (trivial centre) and preserves the fibres of `Λ`, so each fibre of `ψ` has `q^{2g−1}(q − 1)/(q(q − 1)) = q^{2g−2}` elements. (LV20, §5, p. 26; §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 27.) *Needs:* 0.9; 0.10; 0.11; 8.1; 8.13; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.2–IG.1.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.0.A1–F.0.A2; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.3, IG.3.1; EtaleDualityAndPerverseSheaves §§0.5–0.7.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2: M = (ℤ/2)⁴, Υ` has 15 elements, the fibres have 9, and #`π⁻¹(y) = 135`.
- The count `J_{2g}(N) ≥ N^{2g}/2` is Layer LV.0'`s` generating-tuple count.

### Examples

- For `g = 2` and `q = 3` (`Aff(3) = Sym(3)`) there are `810` peripheral-nontrivial surjections from the free
  group of rank four onto `Aff(3)` and `810/6 = 135` conjugacy classes; the degree-three covers have genus
  `5`, primitive homology of dimension `6` and reduced Prym of dimension `3`.
- For `g = 2` and `q = 107`, the Kodaira–Parshin family has relative dimension `159`.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.2, LV.3, LV.5; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2; ComplexComparisonPartII §C5.2; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `B`; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicCurves Layer 12; ComplexComparisonPartII §C0.4; ComplexComparisonPartII Layer C2; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.3, IG.3.1; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups Layer IG.0; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.2–IG.1.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.0.A1–F.0.A2; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A0.1, A1.1; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §A2.5; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §7.9; JacobianChallengePartII §§1.2–1.4; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `D`; JacobianChallengePartII §§1.2–1.4; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; TauCetiRoadmap JacobianChallenge Layer `D`; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5; EtaleDualityAndPerverseSheaves §§0.5–0.7; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.9: `Aff(q)`-covers of surfaces

This layer studies a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover `Z → Y` topologically. Its primitive rational
homology, the kernel of pushforward, is the orthogonal complement of the transfer image and carries a
symplectic form. Because `Aff(q)` has trivial centralizer in `Sym(𝔽_q)`, every mapping class of
`Y ∖ {y}` that preserves the cover lifts uniquely to `Z`, which gives the monodromy representation on
primitive homology; intersecting the stabilizers of the finitely many covers gives a common
finite-index subgroup. For a simple closed curve the rank of the lifted twist minus one detects the cycle
type of the monodromy around it, and for a liftable curve the lifted twist is a single symplectic
transvection. Finally, every cover is put in a normal form concentrated on a torus with one boundary
circle, where explicit curves are available.

### 9.1 `Aff(q)`-covers and singly ramified `Aff(q)`-covers (LV20 §8.2)

**Definition.** For a surface `Y` (with boundary or punctures allowed), an `Aff(q)`-cover is a connected surface `Z` with a degree-`q` covering map `π : Z → Y` whose monodromy on a fibre, for some labelling of the fibre by `𝔽_q`, has image `Aff(q)`. After a choice of base point it determines a homomorphism `Cov : π₁(Y, y₀) → Aff(q)`, well defined up to `Aff(q)`-conjugation (the normalizer of `Aff(q)` in `Sym(𝔽_q)` is `Aff(q)`); two `Aff(q)`-covers are isomorphic (homeomorphic over `Y`) if and only if their classes `Cov` agree. The cycle type of `Cov(η)` is well defined for `η ∈ π₁(Y, y₀)` and for free loops. For a closed `Y` of genus `g` and `y ∈ Y`, a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover is an `Aff(q)`-cover of `Y ∖ {y}` with nontrivial monodromy around `y` (a `q`-cycle); its compactification `Z` has genus gq `− (q − 1)/2` and a single point over `y`. Up to isomorphism there are finitely many, `Z_1, …, Z_N`, and `Mod(Y ∖ {y})` acts on this finite set.

- `AffineCover`: An `Aff(q)`-cover of a surface with its monodromy class `Cov`.
- `AffineCover.cov`: `Cov : π₁(Y, y₀) → Aff(q)` up to conjugacy.
- `AffineCover.iso_iff`: `Z₁ ≅ Z₂` over `Y ↔ Cov₁` and `Cov₂` are `Aff(q)`-conjugate.
- `AffineCover.cycleType`: The cycle type of `Cov(η)`, a partition of `q`, for loops `η`.
- `SinglyRamified`: Singly ramified `Aff(q)`-covers of `(Y, y)` and their compactifications.
- `SinglyRamified.genus`: `g(Z) =` gq `− (q − 1)/2`.
- `SinglyRamified.finite`: Finitely many isomorphism classes `Z_1, …, Z_N`.
- `SinglyRamified.modAction`: The action of `Mod(Y ∖ {y})` on `{Z_1, …, Z_N}`.
- `SinglyRamified.example`: `q = 3, g = 2: N = 135`, genus 5.

*Construction.* Covering-space classification (Layer LV.5): isomorphism classes of connected degree-`q` coverings correspond to conjugacy classes of transitive actions `π₁ → Sym(𝔽_q)`; if the image is `Aff(q)`, conjugacy under `Sym(𝔽_q)` is conjugacy under the normalizer `Aff(q)` (Layer LV.0). The monodromy around `y` lies in `[Aff(q), Aff(q)] = 𝔽_q^+` (the peripheral class is a product of commutators), so if nontrivial it is a `q`-cycle; the compactification has one point over `y` with ramification index `q`, and Riemann–Hurwitz gives `2g(Z) − 2 = q(2g − 2) + (q − 1)`. `π₁(Y ∖ {y})` is finitely generated, so there are finitely many homomorphisms to `Aff(q)`; mapping classes act through `Out(π₁(Y ∖ {y}))`. (LV20, §8.2, p. 39.) *Needs:* 0.8; 0.9; 5.9; 5.23; 5.24; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2.

**Checks.**

- For `q=3` and `g=2` there are 135 singly ramified isomorphism classes, each compactified source of genus 5.
- A connected cyclic degree-3 cover with monodromy `C₃` is not an `Aff(3)`-cover, despite being transitive.
- A trivial disconnected degree-`q` covering is not an `Aff(q)`-cover.
- `q = 3, g = 2: N = 135` and each `Z_i` has genus 5.
- The trivial cover (`q` disjoint copies) is not an `Aff(q)`-cover.

### 9.2 Primitive homology of a covering

**Definition.** For a positive-degree finite (possibly branched) cover `π:Z→Y` of closed oriented surfaces, allowing disconnected `Z`, primitive rational homology is `H₁^Pr(Z,Y):=ker(π_*:H₁(Z;ℚ)→H₁(Y;ℚ))`. It is the kernel of a linear map; transfer and perfectness are theorems about it (9.3), not part of the definition.

- `primitiveHomology`: `H₁^Pr(Z, Y) = ker(π_* : H₁(Z; ℚ) → H₁(Y; ℚ))`.
- `transfer`: `π^* : H₁(Y; ℚ) → H₁(Z; ℚ)` with `π_*π^* = q`.
- `isCompl_primitiveHomology`: `H₁(Z) = π^*H₁(Y) ⊕ H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`, with projection `x ↦ x − q⁻¹π^*π_*x`.
- `primitiveHomology_eq_orthogonal`: `H₁^Pr(Z, Y) = (π^*H₁(Y))^⊥`.
- `primitiveHomology.symplectic`: The restricted intersection form is nondegenerate and alternating.
- `primitiveHomology.equivariant`: A lift of a homeomorphism of `Y` preserves `π^*H₁(Y)` and `H₁^Pr`.
- `primitiveHomology.finrank`: `dim H₁^Pr = dim H₁(Z;ℚ) − 2g(Y) = 2Σ_C g(C) − 2g(Y)`, where `C` ranges over the connected components of `Z`. For connected `Z` this is `2g(Z)−2g(Y)`.
- `primitiveHomology.trivialCover`: The sum-zero subspace for a trivial cover.

*Construction.* Take the kernel (Mathlib `LinearMap.ker`) of the singular-homology pushforward. The branched transfer, the decomposition and the symplectic comparison are 9.3. (LV20, §8.2, p. 39; §8.2, pp. 39–40.) *Needs:* 9.1.

**Checks.**

- For the identity covering, primitive homology is zero and the primitive projection is zero.
- For the trivial disconnected double cover, primitive homology consists of pairs `(x,−x)`, with intersection form twice the form on the base.
- For a singly ramified `Aff(3)`-cover of a genus-two surface, primitive homology has dimension 6, and the projection `x↦x−(1/3)π*π_*x` kills the transfer image.
- A trivial (disconnected) cover of degree `q: H₁^Pr = {(x_i) : Σ x_i = 0}`.
- For a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover of a genus-`g` surface, `dim H₁^Pr = 2g(Z) − 2g = (2g − 1)(q − 1)`.

### 9.3 Transfer complement and symplectic primitive homology

**Lemma.** Let `π : Z → Y` be a finite covering of positive degree `q` between closed oriented surfaces (allowing disconnected source), orientation-preserving away from the branch points, possibly branched over finitely many points. With rational coefficients, `π_* : H₁(Z) → H₁(Y)` and the transfer `π^* : H₁(Y) → H₁(Z)` satisfy `π_*π^* = q, ⟨π^*a, π^*b⟩ = q⟨a, b⟩` and `⟨π^*a, x⟩ = ⟨a, π_*x⟩` for the intersection forms. Hence `H₁(Z) = π^*H₁(Y) ⊕ H₁^Pr(Z, Y)` with `H₁^Pr(Z, Y) := ker π_* = (π^*H₁(Y))^⊥`, the projection onto `H₁^Pr` is `x ↦ x − q⁻¹π^*π_*x`, and the intersection form restricts to a symplectic form on `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`. Homeomorphisms of `Z` covering homeomorphisms of `Y` preserve the decomposition (`isCompl_primitiveHomology`, `primitiveHomology_orthogonal_transfer`).

*Proof sketch.* Define `π^*` on the unbranched part (TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5) and use that removing finitely many points from a closed surface, or their preimages, does not change `H₁` modulo the classes of small loops, which are zero in the closed surface. `π_*π^* = q` is the degree; the projection formula `⟨π^*a, x⟩ = ⟨a, π_*x⟩` holds for transverse cycles by counting preimages of intersection points; `⟨π^*a, π^*b⟩ = q⟨a, b⟩` follows. Hence `π^*` is injective, `π^*H₁(Y) ∩ ker π_* = 0`, and the decomposition and orthogonality follow by dimension count; nondegeneracy on `ker π_*` follows from nondegeneracy on `H₁(Z)` and on `π^*H₁(Y)`. (LV20, §8.2, p. 39; §8.2, pp. 39–40.) *Needs:* 5.1; 5.6; 9.1; 9.2; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6.

**Checks.**

- A trivial (disconnected) cover of degree `q: H₁^Pr = {(x_i) : Σ x_i = 0}`.
- For a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover of a genus-`g` surface, `dim H₁^Pr = 2g(Z) − 2g = (2g − 1)(q − 1)`.

### 9.4 Lifted mapping classes and the monodromy maps `Mon` (LV20 (8.2)–(8.5))

**Construction.** In setting `C`, for a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover `Z`, let `Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z` be the stabilizer of its isomorphism class. Each `f ∈ Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z` has a unique lift `f̃ ∈ Mod(Z)` (the centralizer of `Aff(q)` in `Sym(𝔽_q)` is trivial), giving a homomorphism `Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z → Mod(Z)` and `Mon_Z : Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z → Sp(H₁^Pr(Z, Y))`. Let `Mod(Y ∖ {y})' := ⋂_i Mod(Y ∖ {y})_{Z_i}`, a normal subgroup of finite index (the kernel of the action on `{Z_1, …, Z_N}`); `Mon := (Mon_{Z_i})_i : Mod(Y ∖ {y})' → ∏_i Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))` (LV20 (8.3)); `π₁(Y, y)' := Push⁻¹(Mod(Y ∖ {y})')`, a normal subgroup of finite index in `π₁(Y, y)`, and `Mon ∘ Push` on it (LV20 (8.5)). For `f ∈ Push(π₁(Y, y)) ∩ Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z`, the lift acts trivially on `π^*H₁(Y)`.

- `liftMappingClass`: `Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z →* Mod(Z), f ↦ f̃`.
- `liftMappingClass_unique`: `f̃` is the only mapping class of `Z` covering `f`.
- `monodromyMap`: `Mon_Z : Mod(Y ∖ {y})_Z →* Sp(H₁^Pr(Z, Y))`.
- `stabilizerAll`: `Mod(Y ∖ {y})' = ⋂ Mod(Y ∖ {y})_{Z_i}`, normal of finite index.
- `pushSubgroup`: `π₁(Y, y)' = Push⁻¹(Mod(Y ∖ {y})')`, normal of finite index.
- `monodromyMap.prod`: `Mon : Mod(Y ∖ {y})' → ∏_i Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))`.
- `monodromyMap_push_transfer`: Lifts of point-pushes act trivially on `π^*H₁(Y)`.
- `monodromyMap_twist`: `Mon_Z(T_e^{n_e})` is the multitwist `∏ T_{e_i}^{n_e/d_i}` on homology.
- `monodromyMap.example`: `q = 3, g = 2: 135` factors.

*Construction.* Existence of a lift: `f` preserves the class of `Cov`, so the pullback covering is isomorphic to `Z`; composing gives a homeomorphism of `Z°` covering `f`, which extends over the point above `y` (Layer LV.5). Uniqueness: two lifts differ by a deck transformation, i.e. by an element of the centralizer of the monodromy group `Aff(q)` in `Sym(𝔽_q)`, which is trivial (Layer LV.0); the same argument applies to isotopies, so lifting is well defined on mapping classes and multiplicative. `Mon_Z` preserves the intersection form and the decomposition of primitive homology 9.2, so it lands in `Sp(H₁^Pr)`. The action of `Mod(Y ∖ {y})` on the finite set `{Z_i}` has a normal kernel of finite index; its preimage under the homomorphism `Push` is normal of finite index. A point-push is isotopic to the identity on `Y`, so it acts trivially on `H₁(Y ∖ {y}) = H₁(Y)`; by equivariance of the transfer its lift acts trivially on `π^*H₁(Y)`. (LV20, §8.2.1, p. 40; §8.2.2, p. 40.) *Needs:* 0.9; 5.20; 5.24; 5.25; 5.26; 9.1; 9.2; 9.3; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.BilinForm.isometryGroup`.

**Checks.**

- Lifting the identity gives the identity mapping class and identity linear monodromy.
- For `q=3` and `g=2`, the product monodromy has 135 factors, each acting on a six-dimensional primitive space.
- The lift of a point-push acts trivially on the transfer summand `π*H₁(Y)`, while it can act nontrivially on primitive homology.
- `q = 3, g = 2: Mon` has 135 factors, each `Sp` of a 6-dimensional space.
- For `f = T_e^M` with `M` a multiple of `n_e` (Layer LV.5 lifting), `Mon_Z(f)` is the multitwist action on `H₁^Pr`.

### 9.5 Classes of the preimage circles (LV20 Lemma 8.2)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, let `Z` be a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover and `e ⊂ Y ∖ {y}` a nonseparating simple closed curve whose preimage consists of the circles `e_1, …, e_k` (of degrees `d_i`). Then `[e_1], …, [e_k]` are linearly independent in `H₁(Z; ℚ)`; their span meets `π^*H₁(Y)` in `ℚ·π^*[e] = ℚ·Σ_i[e_i]`, and its projection to `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)` has dimension `k − 1`. Consequently `Mon_Z(T_e^{n_e}) − 1` has rank exactly `k − 1` on `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`, and so does `Mon_Z(T_e^{M}) − 1` for every positive multiple `M` of `n_e`.

*Proof sketch.* By change of coordinates, `Y` has a CW structure with one 2-cell containing `y` in its interior and with `e` in the 1-skeleton; `Y ∖ {y}` deformation retracts onto the 1-skeleton (a wedge of `2g` circles), and `Z ∖ π⁻¹(y)` onto its preimage graph. Since `π⁻¹(y)` is one point, `H₁(Z ∖ π⁻¹(y)) = H₁(Z)`, so `H₁(Z)` is the cycle space of the preimage graph. The `e_i` are cycles with pairwise disjoint edge supports in that graph, so they are linearly independent. If `Σ c_i[e_i] = π^*z` then applying `π_*` gives `q z = (Σ c_i d_i)[e]`, so the intersection with `π^*H₁(Y)` is `ℚ·π^*[e]`, where `π^*[e] = Σ_i[e_i] ≠ 0`; the projection therefore has dimension `k − 1`. The lift of `T_e^{n_e}` is the multitwist `∏ T_{e_i}^{n_e/d_i}`; by Layer LV.5 it acts by `x ↦ x + Σ (n_e/d_i)⟨x, e_i⟩e_i`, whose image is the span of the `[e_i]` (the functionals `⟨·, e_i⟩` are independent). It acts on `π^*H₁(Y)` as `T_e^{n_e}`, with image `ℚ·π^*[e]`; so on `H₁^Pr` the image has dimension `k − 1`. `N := Mon_Z(T_e^{n_e}) − 1` satisfies `N² = 0` (the `e_i` are pairwise disjoint), so `Mon_Z(T_e^{m n_e}) − 1 = mN` has the same rank. (LV20, §8.3, Lemma 8.2, p. 42; §8.3, proof of Lemma 8.2, p. 42.) *Needs:* 5.8; 5.10; 5.12; 5.23; 5.25; 9.2; 9.3; 9.4; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 4.

**Checks.**

- Trivial monodromy along `e: k = q` and the rank is `q − 1`.
- Monodromy a `q`-cycle: `k = 1` and `T_e^q` acts trivially on `H₁^Pr`.

### 9.6 The rank of a lifted twist detects the cycle type (LV20 Lemma 8.3)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, let `e ⊂ Y ∖ {y}` be a nonseparating simple closed curve and `M` a positive multiple of `n_e`. Then the rank of `Mon_Z(T_e^M) − 1` on `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)` equals `k − 1`, where `k` is the number of cycles of `Cov(e)`, and `k` determines the cycle type of `Cov(e): k = q` for the identity, `k = 1` for a `q`-cycle, and `k = 1 + (q − 1)/r` for an element with nontrivial linear part of order `r`.

*Proof sketch.* The rank statement is LV20 Lemma 8.2 (9.5). The possible cycle types in `Aff(q)` are `(1^q), (q)` and `(1, r, …, r)` with `r | q − 1, r > 1` (Layer LV.0); the number of cycles `k` distinguishes them. (LV20, §8.3, Lemma 8.3, p. 42.) *Needs:* 0.8; 9.5.

**Checks.**

- `q = 5`: the identity, a 5-cycle, and elements with linear part of order 2 or 4 give `k = 5, 1, 3, 2`.
- Two covers with different cycle types along `e` give `Mon(T_e^M)` with fixed spaces of different codimensions.

### 9.7 Liftable curves and their transvections

**Definition.** In setting `C`, a simple closed curve `e ⊂ Y ∖ {y}` is liftable for `Z` if `λ(Cov(e))` generates `𝔽_q^×`.

The order of the arguments matters: Layer LV.0 writes `T_v^r(x) = x + r·ω(v, x)·v`, while the Dehn-twist formula uses `î(x, v)`, so the parameter is `−q` when the two forms are identified.

- `IsLiftable`: `λ(Cov(e))` generates `𝔽_q^×`.
- `IsLiftable.nonseparating`: Liftable curves are nonseparating.
- `IsLiftable.liftPlus`: The degree-one lift `e⁺`.
- `IsLiftable.primitiveClass`: `ẽ ∈ H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`, nonzero.
- `IsLiftable.mon_twist`: With `ω` equal to the topological intersection pairing, `Mon_Z(T_e^{q−1}) = T_{ẽ}^{−q}` in the Layer LV.0 convention `T_v^r(x)=x+rω(v,x)v`. Equivalently the actual action is `x↦x+qω(x,ẽ)ẽ`.
- `IsLiftable.inner_eq`: `Ã·B̃ = A⁺·B⁺ − q⁻¹ A·B`.
- `IsLiftable.example`: `Aff(3)` and a reflection monodromy.

*Construction.* Define liftability by the order of the linear part; conjugation changes neither that order nor the underlying unbased curve. The lift, nonzero primitive class, intersection identity and monodromy formula are established in 9.8. (LV20, §8.3, p. 42; §8.3, p. 43.) *Needs:* 0.8; 5.8; 9.1.

**Checks.**

- For `q=3`, reflection monodromy `x↦−x+b` is liftable, with one degree-one lift and one degree-two lift.
- A nonzero translation has linear part 1 and is not liftable for `q≥3`.
- For a liftable curve `e, T_e^(q−1)` acts on primitive homology by `x↦x+q·î(x,ẽ)ẽ`; the exponent on the base twist is `q−1`, not `q`.
- `q = 3`: a curve with `Cov(e) = (x ↦ −x + b)` is liftable, with `e⁺` over the fixed point.
- A curve with `Cov(e)` a translation is not liftable.

### 9.8 Liftable-curve monodromy and primitive intersection

**Theorem.** In setting `C`, let `e ⊂ Y ∖ {y}` be a simple closed curve that is liftable for `Z` (9.7: `λ(Cov(e))` generates `𝔽_q^×`). Then `e` is nonseparating, `Cov(e)` has cycle type `(1, q − 1)`, the preimage of `e` is `e⁺ ⊔ e⁻` with `e⁺` of degree 1 and `e⁻` of degree `q − 1`, and `ẽ :=` the projection of `[e⁺]` to `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)` is nonzero. `Mon_Z(T_e^{q−1})` is the symplectic transvection `x ↦ x + q⟨x, ẽ⟩ẽ` of `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`, and for liftable curves `A, B` one has `Ã·B̃ = A⁺·B⁺ − q⁻¹ A·B` (LV20 (8.6)).

*Proof sketch.* `λ ∘ Cov` factors through `H₁(Y)`, so a separating curve has trivial `λ`-part; hence liftable curves are nonseparating. A generator `a` of `𝔽_q^×` has order `q − 1`, so `Cov(e)` has cycle type `(1, q − 1)` and `n_e = q − 1`; the lift of `T_e^{q−1}` is `T_{e⁺}^{q−1}T_{e⁻}`. For `x ∈ H₁^Pr, ⟨x, π^*[e]⟩ = ⟨π_*x, e⟩ = 0` and `π^*[e] = [e⁺] + [e⁻]`, so the multitwist sends `x` to `x + ⟨x, e⁺⟩((q − 1)e⁺ − e⁻) = x + ⟨x, e⁺⟩(q e⁺ − π^*[e])`; since `ẽ = e⁺ − q⁻¹π^*[e]` and `⟨x, ẽ⟩ = ⟨x, e⁺⟩`, this is `x + q⟨x, ẽ⟩ẽ`. `ẽ ≠ 0` by LV20 Lemma 8.2 `(k − 1 = 1)`. (8.6): `qÃ = qA⁺ − π^*A`, so `q²(Ã·B̃) = q²(A⁺·B⁺) − 2q(A·B) + q(A·B)` by the transfer identities. (LV20, §8.3, p. 42; §8.3, p. 43.) *Needs:* 0.8; 0.14; 5.8; 9.2; 9.3; 9.4; 9.5; 9.7.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3`: a curve with `Cov(e) = (x ↦ −x + b)` is liftable, with `e⁺` over the fixed point.
- A curve with `Cov(e)` a translation is not liftable.

### 9.9 Mapping classes of a surface with two boundary circles realize `Sp(V, b)` (LV20 Lemma 8.4)

**Lemma.** Let `W` be a compact surface of genus `h ≥ 1` with two boundary circles, `V := H₁(W; ℤ)` (free of rank `2h + 1`) with the degenerate alternating form induced by `V → H₁(W, ∂W; ℤ)` and Poincaré–Lefschetz duality; its radical `V⁰` is spanned by the class `b` of a boundary circle. Let `Sp(V, b)` be the group of automorphisms of `V` preserving the form and fixing `b`. Then `Mod(W) → Sp(V, b)` is surjective. Consequently `Mod(W)` acts transitively on the set of classes `ℓ ∈ H₁(W, ∂W; ℤ) ≅ Hom(V, ℤ)` with `ℓ(b) = 1`, and every such class is represented by a simple proper arc from one boundary circle to the other; and for classes `v, u ∈ V` with `⟨v, u⟩ = 1` represented by simple closed curves and `k ∈ ℤ`, the class `v + k b` is represented by a simple closed curve.

*Proof sketch.* `1 → Hom(V/V⁰, V⁰) → Sp(V, b) → Sp(V/V⁰) → 1` is exact, the kernel acting by `f ↦ 1 + f`. Capping both boundary circles surjects `Mod(W)` onto the mapping class group of the closed genus-`h` surface (Layer LV.5), which surjects onto `Sp(V/V⁰)` (Farb–Margalit Theorem 6.4). For `v ∉ V⁰` represented by a simple closed curve, `v + b` is also represented by one (band the curve with a boundary circle, replacing `b` by `−b` if necessary); `T_{v+b}T_v⁻¹` acts by `x ↦ x + ⟨x, v⟩b`, and these elements generate `Hom(V/V⁰, V⁰)` because simple closed curves span `V/V⁰` and the form on `V/V⁰` is unimodular. Transitivity: the kernel acts on `{ℓ : ℓ(b) = 1}` by `ℓ ↦ ℓ − μ` for `μ ∈ Hom(V/V⁰, ℤ)`, transitively; any simple proper arc joining the two boundary circles has a class with `ℓ(b) = ±1`, and mapping classes carry it to every class in the orbit. Applying `(T_{u+b}T_u⁻¹)^k` to a simple closed curve representing `v` gives a simple closed curve representing `v + k⟨v, u⟩b = v +` kb. (LV20, §8.4, Lemma 8.4, p. 44; §8.4, proof of Lemma 8.4, p. 44; §8.4, p. 44.) *Needs:* 5.10; 5.12; 5.13; 5.21; 5.22; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6.

**Checks.**

- `h = 1` (a torus with two holes): `V ≅ ℤ³` with radical `ℤb`, and `Sp(V, b) ≅ SL₂(ℤ) ⋉ ℤ²`.
- The boundary twist `T_b` acts trivially on `V`, consistent with `b` spanning the radical.

### 9.10 Primitive integral lift of a cyclic quotient

**Lemma.** Let `r≥2` and `N≥1`. Every surjective homomorphism `ℤ^r → ℤ/N` has a lift `ℤ^r → ℤ` that is surjective (a primitive integral covector) (`exists_surjective_lift`).

*Proof sketch.* Choose integer coordinate lifts `a₁,…,aᵣ`. Their joint `gcd` with `N` is 1. If needed, add `N` to one of `a₂,…,aᵣ` to make `g=gcd(a₂,…,aᵣ)` nonzero. For each prime dividing `g` and `N, a₁` is already nonzero modulo that prime. For each prime dividing `g` but not `N`, choose `k` so `a₁+Nk` is nonzero modulo that prime; use the Chinese remainder theorem for the finitely many choices. Then `gcd(a₁+Nk,a₂,…,aᵣ)=1`, so Bézout gives a surjective integral covector reducing to the given cyclic quotient. Extend a primitive covector to a unimodular basis for its use in normal forms. (LV20, §8.4, p. 43.)

**Checks.**

- For `(2,2) mod 3`, replace the first coefficient by 5 to obtain `gcd(5,2)=1`.
- Rank one would be false: multiplication by 2 on `ℤ/5` has no surjective integral lift.

### 9.11 Normal form of a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover (LV20 Proposition 8.5)

**Theorem.** In setting `C`, let `Z` be a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover. Then `Y` is the union of `S°_{g−1}` (genus `g − 1`, one boundary circle) and `T°` (genus 1, one boundary circle) glued along their boundary circles, such that: `y` lies in the interior of `T°`; the cover is trivial over `S°_{g−1}`; its restriction to `T°` has trivial monodromy around `∂T°`; and for a standard free basis `β₁, β₂` of `π₁(T ∖ {y})` (`T` the capped torus, `β_i` crossing the cutting curve `α_i` once and not the other), `Cov(β₁)` projects to a generator of `𝔽_q^×` and `Cov(β₂)` is a nonzero translation. Consequently, after conjugation, `Cov(β₁) = (x ↦ cx)` with `c` a generator and `Cov(β₂) = (x ↦ x + 1)`, and `Cov` factors through the map `π₁(Y ∖ {y}) → π₁(T ∖ {y})` collapsing `S°_{g−1}`.

*Proof sketch.* The surjection `λ∘Cov : H₁(Y;ℤ) → ℤ/(q−1)` has rank `2g≥4`. Apply 9.10 (including its CRT argument), then integral Poincaré duality to obtain a primitive class `α₁`; 5.13 represents it by a simple curve avoiding `y`. Take parallel copies `α₁^±` on either side of `y`; they cobound an annulus containing `y`, so `Cov(α₁^+)Cov(α₁^−)⁻¹` is conjugate to the monodromy around `y`, and both lie in `𝔽_q^+` (their `λ`-part is `⟨α₁, α₁⟩ = 0`); so they are not both trivial. Cutting along `α₁^±` and discarding the annulus gives `Y¹` of genus `g − 1` with boundary circles `b₊, b₋`; `Cov` restricted to `π₁(Y¹)` has image in `𝔽_q^+` and factors through `H₁(Y¹; ℤ)`, with `Cov(b₊) = −Cov(b₋) ≠ 0`; conjugating by a scaling, `Cov(b₊) = 1`. The induced map `H₁(Y¹; ℤ) → 𝔽_q^+` lifts to `ℓ : H₁(Y¹; ℤ) → ℤ` with `ℓ(b₊) = 1` (`b₊` is primitive); by Poincaré–Lefschetz duality `ℓ = ⟨·, α₂⟩` with `α₂ ∈ H₁(Y¹, ∂Y¹; ℤ)`, which by LV20 Lemma 8.4 is represented by a simple proper arc joining `b₊` to `b₋`. Cutting `Y¹` along `α₂` gives `Y²` of genus `g − 1` with one boundary circle, over which the cover is trivial (`Cov` is `ℓ mod q`, which vanishes on curves disjoint from `α₂`); the complement of `Y²` in `Y` is a regular neighbourhood of `α₁^± ∪ α₂` together with the annulus, a torus `T°` with one boundary circle containing `y` (Euler characteristics: `(3 − 2g) + (−1) = 2 − 2g`). The monodromy around `∂T° = ∂Y²` is trivial; `β₁` crosses `α₁` once, so `λ(Cov(β₁)) = ±λ`(generator); `β₂` crosses `α₂` once and not `α₁`, so `Cov(β₂) = ±1 ∈ 𝔽_q^+`. Conjugating fixes the fixed point of `Cov(β₁)` at 0 and `Cov(β₂) = +1` (replace `β₂` by its inverse if necessary). `π₁(Y ∖ {y})` is the amalgam of `π₁(S°_{g−1})` and `π₁(T° ∖ {y})` over `∂` (van Kampen); `Cov` kills `π₁(S°_{g−1})` and `∂`, so it factors through `π₁(T° ∖ {y})/⟨⟨∂⟩⟩ = π₁(T ∖ {y})`, free on `β₁, β₂`. (LV20, §8.4, p. 43; §8.4, p. 45; §8.4, Proposition 8.5, p. 45.) *Needs:* 0.7; 5.1; 5.6; 5.10; 5.13; 5.23; 9.1; 9.9; 9.10; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1.

**Checks.**

- `g = 2, q = 3: T°` carries an `S₃`-cover of the once-punctured torus with `Cov(β₁) = (x ↦ −x), Cov(β₂) = (x ↦ x + 1)`.
- Two normal forms with `c₁ = c₂` give isomorphic covers.

### 9.12 Curves on the torus part of the normal form (LV20 Lemma 8.11)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, in the normal form, fix `p ∈ ∂T°`, a labelling of the fibre over `p` by `𝔽_q` with `Cov(β₁) = (x ↦ cx), Cov(β₂) = (x ↦ x + 1)`. There are simple closed curves `γ_j (0 ≤ j ≤ q)` on `T° ∖ {y}`, based at `p` and meeting `∂T°` only at `p`, such that: `(i) λ(Cov(γ_j)) = c` for all `j`; (ii) `Cov(γ_j)` fixes exactly `j` modulo `q` (for `j = q`: fixes 0); (iii) the classes of the degree-one lifts `γ_j⁺` span `H₁` of the restricted cover `T̃°` modulo the homology of its boundary; (iv) all `γ_j` have the same germs at `p`.

*Proof sketch.* Let `d` be a push-off of `β₂` into the interior of `T° ∖ {y}`, on the side away from `y`, crossing `β₁` once near the end of `β₁`. Then `T_d^k(β₁)` is a simple loop at `p` representing `β₁β₂^k` (with the twist direction chosen accordingly), for every `k ≥ 0`, and it has the germs of `β₁` at `p` because `T_d` is supported away from p. `Cov(β₁β₂^k) = (x ↦ c(x + k))` fixes ck/`(1 − c)`. The map `k ↦ k* :=` [ck/`(1 − c)`] `(k < q), q* := q`, is a bijection of `[0, q]`; put `γ_{k*} := T_d^k(β₁)`. This gives `(i)`, (ii), (iv). (iii): the covering of `T°` extends over the torus `T` with a single point over `y`, so it suffices that the lifts span `H₁` of the covering `T̃` of `T ∖ {y}` (compare `H₁(T̃°)/H₁(∂T̃°)` with `H₁(T̃)`). The group `π₁(T̃ ∖ {pt}, p̃)` is the stabilizer `H` of 0 in the free group `⟨β₁, β₂⟩`, and the lift of `γ_j` corresponds to `β₂^{−j*}β₁β₂^{j+j*} ∈ H`. In the abelianization, the `j=0` word is `β₁` and the `j=q` word is `β₂^(−q) β₁ β₂^(2q)`, whose difference from `β₁` is `β₂^q`. Schreier gives `H` generated by `β₂^q` and `β₂^`(`−`[cj])`β₁β₂^j` for `0≤j<q`. The bijection between pairs of exponents modulo `q` changes each word only by left or right powers of `β₂^q`, so these words are in the subgroup generated by `β₂^q` and the lifted words. Thus the lifted homology classes span `H_ab`. This does not assert that those homology classes themselves generate the nonabelian `H`. (LV20, §8.6, Lemma 8.11, p. 48; §8.6, proof of Lemma 8.11, p. 49; §8.6, proof of Lemma 8.11, p. 50.) *Needs:* 5.8; 5.11; 5.25; 9.11; Mathlib `Subgroup.closure_mul_image_eq`.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, c = −1: Cov(β₁β₂^k) = (x ↦ −x − k)` fixes `−k/2`.
- The `γ_j` have classes `(1, k)` in `H₁(T)`, and their lifts span a lattice of rank `2 + (q − 1)` in `H₁(T̃)`.

### Examples

- For the trivial disconnected double cover, primitive homology is `{(x, −x)}` and its intersection form is
  twice that of the base.
- A connected cyclic cover of degree three has monodromy `C₃` and is not an `Aff(3)`-cover.
