- For `q = 3`, a curve with reflection monodromy `x ↦ −x + b` is liftable: its preimage has one circle of
  degree one and one of degree two.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.5; TauCetiRoadmap Completed/UniversalCovers Stage 2; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 5; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 6; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 4; TauCetiRoadmap GeometricTopology Layer 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.10: The monodromy theorem for Kodaira–Parshin families

The monodromy theorem says that the point-pushing subgroup acts on the product of the primitive homologies
of all the singly ramified `Aff(q)`-covers with Zariski-dense image in the product of their symplectic
groups. On one factor, liftable curves with connected intersection graph give a spanning family of
transvections, so the image is dense. Distinct covers are distinguished by the cycle type along some
simple closed curve, so the unipotent fixed spaces have different dimensions and Goursat excludes graphs
between factors. The closure of the point-pushing image is normal in the product and noncentral in each
factor, hence is the product. Through the homology of the reduced Prym this becomes full monodromy of the
Kodaira–Parshin family.

### 10.1 Point-pushing acts non-centrally on each factor (LV20 Lemma 8.6)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, for every `i`, the image of `π₁(Y, y)'` under `Mon_{Z_i} ∘ Push` is not contained in the centre `{±1}` of `Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))`; more precisely it contains a nontrivial unipotent element.

*Proof sketch.* Put `Z = Z_i` in normal form (Layer LV.9) and choose a nonseparating simple closed curve `w` in `S°_{g−1} (g − 1 ≥ 1)` and a curve `w'` in `S°_{g−1}` meeting `w` once. Finger-move `w` along an arc to `y` to get a simple loop `γ` at `y`, isotopic to `w` in `Y`, whose left push-off `a` is isotopic to `w` in `Y ∖ {y}` and whose right push-off `b` bounds, together with `a`, an annulus containing `y` (Farb–Margalit Fact 4.7). Then `Push(γ) = T_a T_b⁻¹`. `Cov(a) = Cov(w) = 1` and `Cov(b)` is conjugate to `Cov(a)` times the monodromy around `y`, a `q`-cycle. So a lifts to the `q` copies `w_1, …, w_q` of `w` in the copies of `S°_{g−1}, b` lifts to one circle `b̃` of degree `q`, and `T_a^q T_b^{−q}` lifts to `∏_j T_{w_j}^q · T_{b̃}⁻¹` (Layer LV.5). The preimage of the annulus is a sphere with `q + 1` holes (Riemann–Hurwitz), so `Σ_j [w_j] = [b̃]` in `H₁(Z)`. Hence `u := Mon_Z(Push(γ)^q)` acts on `H₁(Z)` by `x ↦ x + q Σ_j ⟨x, w_j⟩w_j − ⟨x, b̃⟩b̃ = x + Σ_{j,l} (qδ_{jl} − 1)⟨x, w_j⟩w_l`. For `x = [w'_1]` (the copy of `w'` in the first sheet), `⟨x, w_j⟩ = ±δ_{1j}`, and `(u − 1)x = ±((q − 1)w_1 − Σ_{l≠1} w_l) ≠ 0` because the `w_l` are linearly independent (LV20 Lemma 8.2). So `u` is a nontrivial unipotent on `H₁(Z)`. `u` is the identity on `π^*H₁(Y)` (lifts of point-pushes) and preserves the decomposition, so `u` is a nontrivial unipotent on `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`. `π₁(Y, y)'` has finite index `k` in `π₁(Y, y)` and is normal, so `γ^{qk!} ∈ π₁(Y, y)'` and `Mon_Z(Push(γ^{qk!})) = u^{k!}`, a nontrivial unipotent (characteristic zero), which is not `±1`. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.6, p. 46; §8.5, proof of Lemma 8.6, p. 46; FM12, §4.2, Fact 4.7, p. 103.) *Needs:* 5.10; 5.12; 5.20; 5.25; 9.2; 9.3; 9.4; 9.5; 9.11; Tau Ceti `LinearMap.GeneralLinearGroup.IsUnipotent`.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2: u − 1` has rank 2 on the 6-dimensional primitive homology (the matrix `qI − J` on the three copies has rank `q − 1 = 2`).
- Lawrence and Venkatesh leave this lemma to the reader and sketch an argument through the theorem of the fixed part, Torelli and de Franchis; the proof above is topological.

### 10.2 Distinct covers are distinguished by the cycle type along a simple closed curve (LV20 Lemma 8.8)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, for non-isomorphic `Z₁, Z₂` among the `Z_i` there is a nonseparating simple closed curve `η ⊂ Y ∖ {y}` such that `Cov₁(η)` and `Cov₂(η)` have different cycle types.

*Proof sketch.* Case `1: λ₁ := λ ∘ Cov₁` and `λ₂ := λ ∘ Cov₂`, as maps `H₁(Y; ℤ) → 𝔽_q^×`, have different kernels. Choose a basis `e_1, …, e_{2g}` of `H₁(Y; ℤ)` with `ker λ₁ = ⟨(q − 1)e_1, e_2, …⟩`; some `e_j (j ≥ 2)` is not in `ker λ₂`. A simple closed curve `η` representing `e_j` (primitive) has `Cov₁(η) ∈ 𝔽_q^+` (cycle type `(1^q)` or `(q)`) and `λ₂(Cov₂(η)) ≠ 1` (cycle type `(1, r, …, r), r > 1`). Case 2: equal kernels. Cut `Y` along the same `α₁` for both covers (Layer LV.9 normal form), normalize `Cov_i(b₊) = 1`, and let `g_i : H₁(Y¹; ℤ) → 𝔽_q^+` be the induced maps. If `g₁ ≠ g₂`, pick a class `v_s` of a geometric symplectic basis of the capped part with `g₁(v_s) ≠ g₂(v_s)`, and `k ≡ −g₁(v_s) mod q`; by LV20 Lemma 8.4 the class `v_s + k b₊` is represented by a simple closed curve `η ⊂ Y¹`, nonseparating in `Y`, with `Cov₁(η) = 1` and `Cov₂(η)` a `q`-cycle. Case `3: g₁ = g₂`. Cut along the same `α₂`, so both covers are in normal form on the same decomposition `Y = S°_{g−1} ∪ T°`, with `Cov_i(β₁) = (x ↦ c_i x), Cov_i(β₂) = (x ↦ x + 1)` and `c₁ ≠ c₂` (otherwise `Cov₁ = Cov₂`, since both factor through the free group `⟨β₁, β₂⟩`). In `T° ∖ {y}` choose disjoint simple arcs `ℓ₁, ℓ₂` from `∂T°` to `∂T°`, parallel copies of `β₂` separated by `y`, whose closures in `T ∖ {y}` represent `β₂` and `β₁β₂β₁⁻¹`; in `S°_{g−1}` choose simple arcs `w, w'` joining their endpoints on `∂`, disjoint from each other except for one transverse crossing (possible since `g − 1 ≥ 1`). The closed curves `d := ℓ₁ ∪ w` and `x := ℓ₂ ∪ w'` are simple, nonseparating, and cross exactly once. For `m ≥ 1, η := T_d^m(x)` is simple; as a free loop it is `x·d^{±m}`, and its image under the collapse `π₁(Y ∖ {y}) → π₁(T ∖ {y})` is conjugate to `β₁β₂β₁⁻¹β₂^{±m}`. Hence `Cov_i(η)` is conjugate to translation by `c_i^{±1} ± m` (the exponent of `c_i` fixed by the composition convention). Choose `m ∈ [1, q − 1]` with `c₁^{±1} ± m ≡ 0: Cov₁(η) = 1` while `Cov₂(η)` is translation by `c₂^{±1} − c₁^{±1} ≠ 0`, a `q`-cycle. The class of `η` is `[x] ± m[d]`, whose `S_{g−1}`-component `[w'] ± m[w]` is primitive, so `η` is nonseparating. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.8, p. 47; §8.5, proof of Lemma 8.8, p. 47.) *Needs:* 0.8; 5.8; 5.10; 5.11; 5.13; 9.1; 9.9; 9.11; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3: Aff(3) = S₃` and cycle types `(1³), (3), (1, 2)` are all realized.
- Lawrence and Venkatesh draw the curve of case 3 for exponent 2 (their Figure 4); the proof above constructs it for every exponent as a twist of `x` about `d`.

### 10.3 Signed primitive lattice representatives affinely span

A vector `v : ℤ^r` is primitive when the `gcd` of all its coordinates is `1`
(`IsPrimitiveVector`). Its API identifies this with a cyclic quotient of `ℤ^r` having a
surjective integral lift, with membership in an integral basis for `r ≥ 1`, and with coprime
coordinates; changing sign and applying a unimodular matrix preserve primitivity. These use
Mathlib's `gcd` and the integral basis theory, together with §9.10. Source: LV20 §8.4, p. 43,
and §8.5, proof of Lemma 8.10, p. 48.

**Lemma.** Let `r≥2`. Assign an arbitrary sign `ε(v)∈{−1,1}` to every primitive vector `v` of `ℤ^r`. The differences of the signed vectors `ε(v)v` span `ℚ^r`; in particular choosing one representative of each pair `{v,−v}` suffices (choose `ε(−v)=−ε(v)`) (`IsPrimitiveVector`, `span_sub_signedPrimitive`).

*Proof sketch.* If a rational linear functional `ℓ` has constant value `c` on the chosen representatives, then `ℓ(v)∈{c,−c}` on every primitive vector. For `i≠j` every `eᵢ+n eⱼ` is primitive, for all integers `n`. The sequence `ℓ(eᵢ)+nℓ(eⱼ)` taking only two values forces `ℓ(eⱼ)=0`. Vary `j` to conclude `ℓ=0` and `c=0`. The annihilator of the span of all differences is zero; finite-dimensional duality proves it is the entire rational space. This is a rational spanning claim, not an integral index-one claim. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.10, p. 48; application in §8.6, pp. 50–51.)

**Checks.**

- `(1,0)` is primitive; `(2,4)` is not, since its coordinate `gcd` is `2`.
- In rank zero the empty-coordinate `gcd` is `0`, so the unique vector is not primitive.
- `(2,3)` is primitive although neither coordinate is a unit of `ℤ`; requiring a unit coordinate would be too strong.
- For rank two, arbitrary signs still give rational affine span `ℚ²`.
- Rank one is excluded: one signed representative gives no nonzero difference.

### 10.4 Primitive vectors avoiding two intersection constraints

**Lemma.** On `ℤ^r` with `r≥2`, let `ℓ₁,ℓ₂` be nonzero rational linear forms. For every bound `B` there is a primitive integral vector `w` with `|ℓ₁(w)|>B` and `|ℓ₂(w)|>B` (`exists_primitive_large`).

*Proof sketch.* Choose `u=(1,n,n²,…,n^(r−1))` outside the finitely many roots of the two nonzero evaluation polynomials. Then `u` is primitive and both forms are nonzero on `u`. Extend `u` to an integral basis and let `v` be another basis vector. Every `w=v+m u` is primitive, since its coordinates in this basis include 1. As `|m|` grows both absolute values tend to infinity. Signs assigned to primitive representatives leave these absolute values unchanged. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.10, p. 48; application in §8.6, pp. 50–51.)

**Checks.**

- For the standard symplectic lattice of rank two, use this with the pairing against any two nonzero primitive classes.
- A zero form would make the conclusion false.

### 10.5 A connected spanning system of liftable curves (LV20 Lemma 8.10)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, for a singly ramified `Aff(q)`-cover `Z` there are liftable curves `A_1, …, A_M` on `Y ∖ {y}` such that `(a)` the classes `Ã_s` span `H₁^Pr(Z, Y)`, and `(b)` the graph with an edge between `A_s` and `A_t` whenever `Ã_s·Ã_t ≠ 0` is connected.

*Proof sketch.* Put `Z` in normal form with base point `p ∈ ∂D ≅ ∂D'` and take the curves `γ_j` of LV20 Lemma 8.11; label the sheets of the trivial cover over `S°_{g−1}` by `𝔽_q` using the labelling at p. Let `W` be a set of simple loops at `p` in `S°_{g−1}`, one for each primitive class. For `w ∈ W`, one of `γ_j·w^{±1}` is represented by a simple closed curve `A(w, j)` (smooth the concatenation at `p`; the branches of `γ_j` and `w` lie on opposite sides of `∂`, and by (iv) the admissible sign depends only on `w`). Its monodromy has linear part `c`, so `A(w, j)` is liftable, with `[A(w, j)⁺] = [γ_j⁺] ± [w_j]` (`w_j` the lift of `w` to the `j`-th sheet). For each sheet `j`, differences of `[A(w,j)⁺]` as `w` varies are the differences of signed primitive representatives. Apply 10.3, rather than assuming arbitrary signed basis differences span. Combine this with 9.12 (LV20 Lemma 8.11) and Mayer–Vietoris to span `H₁(Z;ℚ)`, then project to primitive homology. The projected intersection equals a constant plus `±(δⱼₖ−1/q)` times the intersection of the sheet classes. The coefficient is nonzero. Apply 10.4 to the two nonzero integral linear forms given by pairing against `w₁` and `w₂`, making both products dominate their fixed constants. Either chosen sign works. Thus every two vertices have a common adjacent vertex. Finally choose finitely many vertices spanning the finite-dimensional primitive homology and finitely many paths in the connected graph joining them. Their union is the finite connected spanning system required by the statement. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.10, p. 48; §8.6, p. 50; §8.6, p. 51.) *Needs:* 5.13; 9.2; 9.3; 9.7; 9.8; 9.11; 9.12; 10.3; 10.4; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 3.

**Checks.**

- `g = 2, q = 3: W` can be taken to be the primitive classes of the genus-1 part, and finitely many `A(w, j)` already span the 6-dimensional primitive homology.
- The constant term `[γ_j⁺]·[γ_k⁺] − q⁻¹[γ_j]·[γ_k]` is bounded independently of `w₃`, which is what makes 'large enough' possible.

### 10.6 Zariski density on one factor (LV20 Lemma 8.9)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, for each `i`, the image of `Mon_{Z_i} : Mod(Y ∖ {y})_{Z_i} → Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))` is Zariski dense, and so is the image of every finite-index subgroup, in particular of `Mod(Y ∖ {y})'`.

*Proof sketch.* For the liftable curves `A_s` of LV20 Lemma 8.10 (10.5), `Mon(T_{A_s}^{q−1}) = T_{Ã_s}^{−q}` (Layer LV.9), so the Zariski closure contains the root groups `{T_{Ã_s}^r}` (Layer LV.0). The `Ã_s` span and have connected intersection graph, so by LV20 Lemma 2.14 (Layer LV.0) the closure is `Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))`. The closure of the image of a finite-index subgroup has the same identity component (ReductiveGroups Layer 3), and `Sp` is connected. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.9, p. 47.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.19; 0.21; 9.4; 9.7; 9.8; 10.5; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.Symplectic.geometricallyConnectedCommHopfAlgProperty_coordinateHopfAlgebra`; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2`: the closure is `Sp₆` for each of the 135 covers.
- The `(−q)`-th powers of the transvections `T_{Ã_s}` already have Zariski closure containing the full root groups.

### 10.7 Zariski density on the product (LV20 Lemma 8.7)

**Lemma.** In setting `C`, the image of `Mon : Mod(Y ∖ {y})' → ∏_i Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))` is Zariski dense.

*Proof sketch.* Identify the factors (all of dimension `(2g − 1)(q − 1)`) with one symplectic space by symplectic isomorphisms; let `G` be the Zariski closure. Each projection of `G` is `Sp` (one-factor density lemma; the projection of the closure is the closure of the projection). For `i ≠ j`, the curve `η` of LV20 Lemma 8.8 gives different cycle types of `Cov_i(η), Cov_j(η)`; for `M` a common multiple of the orders `n_η` for all `Z_k, T_η^M ∈ Mod(Y ∖ {y})'`, and `Mon_i(T_η^M), Mon_j(T_η^M)` are unipotent with fixed spaces of different codimensions (LV20 Lemma 8.3). LV20 Lemma 2.12 (Layer LV.0, symplectic Goursat) gives `G = ∏_i Sp`. (LV20, §8.5, Lemma 8.7, p. 46.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.26; 9.4; 9.6; 10.2; 10.6; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- `N = 1` is 10.6.
- Without 10.2 (LV20 Lemma 8.8), the closure could be the graph of a conjugation between two factors.

### 10.8 Closed normal subgroups of products of symplectic groups

**Lemma.** Let `k` be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, `(V_i, ω_i) (1 ≤ i ≤ N)` symplectic spaces of dimension `≥ 2` and `H ⊆ ∏_i Sp(V_i)` a Zariski-closed normal subgroup whose projection to each factor is not contained in the centre `{±1}`. Then `H = ∏_i Sp(V_i)`.

*Proof sketch.* For `h ∈ H` with `h_i ∉ {±1}` and `s` in the `i`-th factor subgroup, the commutator `[h, s] = (1, …, [h_i, s], …, 1)` lies in `H`. Let `N_i` be the closed subgroup generated by all commutators `[h_i,s], s∈Sp(V_i)`. The identity [`h_i`,st]`=[h_i,s]·s[h_i,t]s⁻¹` makes `N_i` normal. If it were central, the regular map `s↦[h_i,s]` from connected `Sp(V_i)` to `{±1}` would be constant with value 1, forcing `h_i` central, a contradiction. Closed normal subgroups of `Sp(V_i)` are `Sp(V_i)` or central (ReductiveGroups Layer 6), so `H` contains the `i`-th factor subgroup for every `i`. (LV20, §8.5, p. 46.) *Needs:* 0.15; 0.17; Tau Ceti `TauCeti.Symplectic.geometricallyConnectedCommHopfAlgProperty_coordinateHopfAlgebra`; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6.

**Checks.**

- `N = 1`: a closed normal subgroup of `Sp(V)` containing a non-central element is `Sp(V)`.
- The subgroup `{±1}^N` is normal and central in each factor, so the hypothesis is needed.

### 10.9 Zariski density of the point-pushing monodromy (LV20 Theorem 8.1)

**Theorem.** In setting `C`, the map `Mon ∘ Push : π₁(Y, y)' → ∏_{i=1}^N Sp(H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y))` has Zariski-dense image.

*Proof sketch.* `Push(π₁(Y, y)) = ker(Forget)` is normal in `Mod(Y ∖ {y})`, so `Push(π₁(Y, y)') = Push(π₁(Y, y)) ∩ Mod(Y ∖ {y})'` is normal in `Mod(Y ∖ {y})'`. The Zariski closure `H` of its image is therefore normal in the closure of `Mon(Mod(Y ∖ {y})')`, which is `∏_i Sp` by LV20 Lemma 8.7 (subgroups normalizing a group normalize its closure). By LV20 Lemma 8.6 each projection of `H` is non-central; the lemma on closed normal subgroups of symplectic products gives `H = ∏_i Sp`. (LV20, §8.2.2, Theorem 8.1, p. 41.) *Needs:* 0.15; 5.20; 9.4; 10.1; 10.7; 10.8; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2`: the closure is a product of 135 copies of `Sp₆`.
- The theorem is the topological form of full monodromy for the Kodaira–Parshin family.

### 10.10 The Kodaira–Parshin family has full monodromy (LV20 §8.2.3)

**Theorem.** Let `Y` be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over a number field `K ⊂ ℂ` and `q ≥ 3` a prime. The Kodaira–Parshin family `X_q → Y'_q → Y` has full monodromy: for `y ∈ Y(ℂ)`, the Zariski closure of the monodromy of `π₁(Y(ℂ), y)` on `H¹_B(X_{q,y}(ℂ), ℚ) = ⊕_{y' ↦ y} H¹_B(X_{q,y'}(ℂ), ℚ)` contains `∏_{y'} Sp(H¹_B(X_{q,y'}(ℂ)), ω_{y'})`.

*Proof sketch.* `Y(ℂ)` is a closed surface of genus `g` (Layer LV.8). The fibre `π⁻¹(y)` is the set of `Aff(q)`-conjugacy classes of surjections `π₁(Y(ℂ) ∖ {y}) → Aff(q)` nontrivial at `y`, which is in bijection with the singly ramified `Aff(q)`-covers `Z_1, …, Z_N` via `y' ↦ Z_{q,y'} ×^{Aff(q)} 𝔽_q` (Layer LV.9). The analytic Kodaira–Parshin curve family restricted to `Y(ℂ)` is the configuration family of Layer LV.5 attached to the Hurwitz covering (Layer LV.8); so the monodromy of `γ ∈ π₁(Y(ℂ), y)` permutes the `y'` as `Push(γ)` permutes the `Z_i`, and for `γ ∈ π₁(Y, y)'` it acts on the `y'`-summand of `H₁` by the lift of `Push(γ)`, which is unique on the degree-`q` quotient (trivial centralizer) and commutes with the `Aff(q)`-action on `Z_{q,y'}`. The identification `H₁(X_{q,y'}(ℂ), ℚ) ≅ H₁^Pr(Z_i, Y; ℚ)` (Layer LV.8) is natural for such lifts and matches the polarization with the intersection form up to a nonzero factor; so on `π₁(Y, y)'` the monodromy on `⊕_{y'} H₁(X_{q,y'})` is `Mon ∘ Push`, and the monodromy on `H¹_B` is its contragredient. By LV20 Theorem 8.1 the Zariski closure of the image of `π₁(Y, y)'` is `∏ Sp`; dualizing preserves this, and the image of `π₁(Y(ℂ), y)` contains that of `π₁(Y, y)'`. This is LV20 (5.1), and full monodromy does not depend on `y` or `ι`. (LV20, §8.2.3, p. 41; §5, p. 26.) *Needs:* 3.11; 5.26; 8.1; 8.5; 8.7; 8.12; 8.13; 9.1; 9.4; 10.9; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2.

**Checks.**

- `q = 3, g = 2`: the closure contains a product of 135 copies of `Sp₆`.
- Property `(i)` before LV20 Theorem 5.4.

### Examples

- For `g = 2` and `q = 3` the point-pushing monodromy is Zariski dense in a product of `135` copies of `Sp₆`.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.3, LV.5, LV.8, LV.9; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 1; TauCetiRoadmap AlgebraicTopology Stage 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 3; TauCetiRoadmap ReductiveGroups Layer 6; AbelianSchemesAndArithmeticModuli §§A5.6–A5.7; ComplexComparisonPartII §§C5.1–C5.2; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Layer LV.11: Faltings's theorem

The proof of Faltings's theorem chooses an auxiliary prime `q` and a friendly place `v`. The prime is taken
with `4 ∤ q − 1`, with no small odd prime and no odd prime dividing the discriminant of `K` in `q − 1`, and
large enough for the final inequality; the place is found by Chebotarev in the compositum of the Galois
closure of `K` with `ℚ(ζ_{q−1})`, so that it is friendly and its norm has large order modulo every odd prime
dividing `q − 1`. The Frobenius-fixed subgroups for exponents one to seven are then isotropic on the odd part
of `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q − 1))`, which bounds short orbits there; the linear-part map from the Kodaira–Parshin fibre has
constant fibres, so the bound passes to the fibre and gives `size_v < 1/(d_q + 1)` at every rational point.
Layer LV.7 then shows that `Y(K)` is finite.

### 11.1 Choice of the prime `q` (LV20 §5, conditions `(i)`–(iii))

**Lemma.** Let `K` be a number field with Galois closure `K'` over `ℚ, g ≥ 2` and `B > 0`. There is a prime `q > B` such that: `(i) 4 ∤ q − 1`, and no odd prime `ℓ < 8[K : ℚ]` divides `q − 1`; (ii) no odd prime dividing `q − 1` divides `disc(K)`; consequently, writing `q − 1 = 2m'` (`m'` odd), `ℚ(ζ_{q−1}) = ℚ(ζ_{m'}), K' ∩ ℚ(ζ_{q−1}) = ℚ` and `Gal(K'(ζ_{m'})/ℚ) ≅ Gal(K'/ℚ) × (ℤ/m')^× ≅ Gal(K'/ℚ) × (ℤ/(q − 1))^×`; (iii) `8·2^{g+1}/(q − 1)^g < 1/((g − 1/2)(q − 1) + 1)` (`exists_admissible_prime`).

*Proof sketch.* Let `L` be the set of odd primes `ℓ` with `ℓ < 8[K : ℚ]` or `ℓ | disc(K)`, and `N := 4∏_{ℓ∈L} ℓ`. By Dirichlet's theorem there are primes `q > B` with `q ≡ −1 mod N`; then `q − 1 ≡ 2 mod 4` and `q − 1 ≡ −2 ≢ 0 mod ℓ` for `ℓ ∈ L`, which gives `(i)` and (ii). (ii): the primes ramified in `K'` are those ramified in `K`, i.e. those dividing `disc(K)` (Mathlib). The odd primes dividing `q − 1` are unramified in `K'`, so `|disc K'|` is coprime to `m'`, and the Tau Ceti compositum isomorphism gives `Gal(K'(ζ_{m'})/ℚ) ≅ Gal(K'/ℚ) × (ℤ/m')^×`; also `(ℤ/2m')^× = (ℤ/m')^×` and `ℚ(ζ_{2m'}) = ℚ(ζ_{m'})` since `m'` is odd. (iii): the left side is `O(q^{−g})` and the right side is of order `q^{−1}`; since `g ≥ 2` the inequality holds for all large `q`. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 26.) *Needs:* Mathlib `Nat.forall_exists_prime_gt_and_eq_mod`; Mathlib `NumberField.not_dvd_discr_iff_isUnramifiedIn`; Mathlib `NumberField.exists_not_isUnramifiedIn`; Mathlib `IsCyclotomicExtension.Rat.ramificationIdx_eq_of_not_dvd`; Tau Ceti `IsCyclotomicExtension.Rat.prime_dvd_of_dvd_natAbs_discr`; Tau Ceti `IsCyclotomicExtension.galEquivProd`; Mathlib `ZMod.card_units_eq_totient`; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ, g = 2: q = 107` is admissible: `106 = 2·53`, none of 3, 5, 7 divides 106, and `64/106² ≈` 0.00570 `< 1/160 =` 0.00625.
- `K = ℚ, g = 2`: the least `q ≡ −1 mod 420` is `419 (418 = 2·11·19)`, also admissible.

### 11.2 Choice of the friendly place `v` (LV20 §5)

**Lemma.** Let `K` and `q` be as in the lemma on the choice of `q` (11.1) and `T` a finite set of places of `K`. There is a finite place `v ∉ T` of `K` such that: `(a) v` is friendly; `(b)` its residue characteristic `p` is odd, unramified in `K` and lies below no place of `T`; `(c) (q_v, q − 1) = 1`; `(d)` for every odd prime `r | q − 1`, the class of `q_v` in `(ℤ/r)^×` has order at least 8.

*Proof sketch.* Choose `σ ∈ Gal(K'/ℚ)`: if `K` has a CM subfield, let `E` be its largest CM subfield and `E⁺` the maximal totally real subfield of `E`, and take `σ ∈ Gal(K'/E⁺)` acting nontrivially on `E`; otherwise take any `σ`. By the Chinese remainder theorem choose `a ∈ (ℤ/m')^×` reducing to a primitive root modulo every prime `r | m'`. By Chebotarev applied to `K'(ζ_{m'})/ℚ` and the class of `(σ, a)` (Tau Ceti Chebotarev Layer 10 with the compositum Frobenius lemma), there are infinitely many primes `p`, unramified in `K'(ζ_{m'})`, with a prime `℘` above `p` whose Frobenius restricts to `σ` on `K'` and with `p ≡ a mod m'`; exclude `p = 2`, the primes ramified in `K` and those below `T`. Let `v` be the place of `K` below `℘` and `f` its residue degree, so `q_v = p^f` with `f ≤ [K : ℚ]`. `(c): p` is odd and prime to `m'`. `(d): p` has order `r − 1` modulo `r`, so `q_v` has order `(r − 1)/gcd(r − 1, f) ≥ (r − 1)/[K : ℚ] ≥ 8`, since `r ≥ 8[K : ℚ] + 1` by `(i)` of 11.1. `(a)`: if `E` exists, the decomposition group of `℘` in `Gal(K'/ℚ)` is generated by `σ ∈ Gal(K'/E⁺)`, so the place of `E⁺` below `℘` has residue degree 1 over `p` and its Frobenius in `Gal(E/E⁺)` is `σ|_E ≠ 1`; it is therefore inert in `E`, and `v` lies above it. In all cases `v` is unramified over `ℚ`. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 27.) *Needs:* 1.4; 1.5; 11.1; Tau Ceti `NumberField.Chebotarev.mem_frobeniusPrimeSet_galEquivProd_symm_iff`; Tau Ceti `AlgHom.IsArithFrobAt.autToPow_eq_absNorm`; Tau Ceti `IsCyclotomicExtension.galEquivProd`; Mathlib `Ideal.quotientInfRingEquivPiQuotient`; Mathlib `IsArithFrobAt`; Tau Ceti `NumberField.exists_isArithFrobAt`; TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev Layer 10.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ` (no CM subfield): every odd prime `p` that is a primitive root modulo each prime factor of `(q − 1)/2` works; for `q = 107, 2` is a primitive root modulo 53, so any odd prime `p ≡ 2 mod 53` works, for example `p = 373 = 7·53 + 2`. Any numerical auxiliary prime must also avoid the finite bad-reduction and ramification set; 373 is only usable when it avoids that set.
- `K` imaginary quadratic (a CM field): `v` must lie above a prime inert in `K`.

### 11.3 The Weil pairing on `H¹(Y, ℤ/(q − 1))` and the Frobenius at `v`

**Lemma.** Let `Y` be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g` over `K` with good reduction outside a finite set `S` of places, `N ≥ 2`, and `v ∉ S` a finite place with `(q_v, N) = 1`. Then `M := H¹_et(Y_{K̄}, ℤ/N)` is free of rank `2g` over `ℤ/N` and unramified at `v`, it carries a perfect alternating `G_K`-equivariant pairing `⟨·,·⟩ : M × M → μ_N^∨ := Hom(μ_N, ℤ/N)`, and the Frobenius `T := Frob_v` satisfies `⟨Tm₁, Tm₂⟩ = q_v⁻¹⟨m₁, m₂⟩`.

*Proof sketch.* Poincaré duality for curves with `ℤ/N` coefficients (cup product and the trace `H²(Y_{K̄}, μ_N) ≅ ℤ/N`) gives the perfect alternating equivariant pairing into `Hom(μ_N, ℤ/N)`; freeness of rank `2g` follows from `H¹ = Hom(π₁^geom, ℤ/N)` and the comparison with the topological fundamental group (Layer LV.8). Unramifiedness: smooth proper base change for the good model at `v, N` being invertible there. The arithmetic Frobenius acts on `μ_N` by `ζ ↦ ζ^{q_v}`, hence on `μ_N^∨` by multiplication by `q_v⁻¹`; equivariance of the pairing gives the relation. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 27.) *Needs:* 8.1; EtaleDualityAndPerverseSheaves §§0.5–0.7; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.2–IG.1.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.0.A1–F.0.A2; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.2.1–F.2.2, `F`.4.

**Checks.**

- `N = 2: M ≅ Jac(Y)[2]` (as `μ₂ = ℤ/2`) with the Weil pairing, and `T` preserves the pairing since `q_v` is odd.
- For an elliptic curve and `N = q − 1`, the relation is `det T = q_v⁻¹` on `H¹`.

### 11.4 Few elements lie in small Frobenius orbits

**Lemma.** Let `N = 2m'` with `m'` odd, `M` a free `ℤ/N`-module of rank `2g` with a perfect alternating pairing into a cyclic group `C ≅ ℤ/N`, and `T` an automorphism of `M` with `⟨Tm₁, Tm₂⟩ = u⟨m₁, m₂⟩` for a unit `u ∈ (ℤ/N)^×` such that `u^i ≢ 1 mod r` for every prime `r | m'` and `1 ≤ i ≤ 7`. Then `|ker(T^i − 1)| ≤ 2^g N^g` for `1 ≤ i ≤ 7`, and at most `7·2^g N^g ≤ 8·2^g N^g` elements of `M` lie in `T`-orbits with fewer than 8 elements (`card_fixed_le`).

*Proof sketch.* For `m₁, m₂ ∈ ker(T^i − 1): (u^i − 1)⟨m₁, m₂⟩ = 0`, and `u^i − 1` is a unit modulo `m'`, so the `ℤ/m'`-component of `⟨m₁, m₂⟩` vanishes and `⟨2m₁, 2m₂⟩ = 4⟨m₁, m₂⟩ = 0`. `2M ≅ (ℤ/m')^{2g}` with the pairing `(2a, 2b) ↦ 4⟨a, b⟩ ∈ 2C ≅ ℤ/m'` is perfect (2 is invertible modulo `m'`); by Layer LV.0 (isotropic subgroups) `|2·ker(T^i − 1)|² ≤ |2M| = m'^{2g}`. The kernel of `x ↦ 2x` on `ker(T^i − 1)` lies in `M[2] ≅ (ℤ/2)^{2g}`, so `|ker(T^i − 1)| ≤ 2^{2g}·m'^g = 2^g N^g`. An element in an orbit of size `s < 8` lies in `ker(T^s − 1)` with `1 ≤ s ≤ 7`. (LV20, §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 27; §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 28.) *Needs:* 0.12; Mathlib `CharacterModule`.

**Checks.**

- `N = 106 = 2·53, g = 2: |ker(T^i − 1)| ≤ 4·106² = 44944`, while `|M| = 106⁴ ≈` 1.26`·10⁸`.
- Lawrence and Venkatesh state the union over `1 ≤ i ≤ 8`; for `i = 8` the unit condition may fail when `q_v` has order exactly 8 modulo `r`, but orbits of size `< 8` only need `i ≤ 7`.

### 11.5 The size bound (LV20 (5.4))

**Lemma.** Let `Y` be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve of genus `g ≥ 2` over `K, q` an admissible prime, `X_q → Y'_q → Y` the Kodaira–Parshin family with a good model over `𝒪_S` (`𝒴` proper), and `v ∉ S` a place satisfying `(b)`–`(d)` of 11.2 for `T ⊇ S`. Then `size_v(π⁻¹(y)) < 1/(d_q + 1)` for every `y ∈ Y(K)`.

*Proof sketch.* `π⁻¹(y)` is finite étale over `𝒪_S`, hence unramified at `v`. The fibre map `ψ : π⁻¹(y)(K̄) → M = H¹(Y_{K̄}, ℤ/(q − 1))` is `G_K`-equivariant with image `Υ` (the generating `2g`-tuples) and fibres of constant size (Layer LV.8), so `size_v(π⁻¹(y)) ≤ size_v(Υ)` (LV20 (5.3)). The Frobenius `T` at `v` preserves `Υ` and satisfies `⟨Tm₁, Tm₂⟩ = q_v⁻¹⟨m₁, m₂⟩`; by `(c), (d), u = q_v⁻¹` satisfies the unit condition of the orbit count, and `q − 1 = 2m'` with `m'` odd by admissibility. So at most `8·2^g(q − 1)^g` elements of `Υ` lie in small orbits. `|Υ| = J_{2g}(q − 1) ≥ (q − 1)^{2g}/2` (Layer LV.0), so `size_v(Υ) ≤ 8·2^{g+1}/(q − 1)^g < 1/((g − 1/2)(q − 1) + 1) = 1/(d_q + 1)` by (iii). (LV20, §5, (5.4), p. 26; §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 27.) *Needs:* 0.11; 7.1; 8.13; 8.14; 11.1; 11.2; 11.3; 11.4.

**Checks.**

- `K = ℚ, g = 2, q = 107: size_v ≤ 64/106² ≈` 0.0057 `< 1/160`.
- The bound does not depend on `y`.

### 11.6 Faltings's theorem (LV20 Theorem 5.4)

**Theorem.** Let `K` be a number field and `Y` a smooth projective geometrically connected curve over `K` of genus `g ≥ 2`. Then `Y(K)` is finite (`mordell_finite`). The native signature uses a smooth proper geometrically connected one-dimensional scheme, `Scheme.genus K`, finite-dimensional `H¹(C, O_C)`, and its morphisms over `Spec K`; for curves proper and projective are equivalent.

*Proof sketch.* Fix an embedding `K ⊂ ℂ`. Choose an admissible prime `q` and form the Kodaira–Parshin family `X_q → Y'_q → Y` (Layer LV.8); choose `S` such that it has a good model over `𝒪_S` with `𝒴` proper. The family has full monodromy (Layer LV.10), and `Y(ℂ)` is connected. Choose `v` by 11.2 with `T` the set of places in `S`, above 2, and above the primes lying below `S`; then `v` is friendly, `v ∉ S`, its residue characteristic is odd and lies below no place of `S`, and `(c), (d)` hold. By 11.5, `Y(K)* = Y(K)` for `d = d_q`; 7.9 (LV20 Proposition 5.3) shows that `Y(K)*` is finite. (LV20, §5, Theorem 5.4, p. 26; §5, proof of Theorem 5.4, p. 26.) *Needs:* 2.3; 3.10; 7.9; 8.13; 10.10; 11.1; 11.2; 11.5; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5.

**Checks.**

- The statement is the declaration `mordell_finite` of HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions, §§5.12, 6.4.
- For `K = ℚ` and `g = 2` the proof runs with `q = 107 (d_q = 159)` and a prime `p ≡ 2 mod 53`. Any numerical auxiliary prime must also avoid the finite bad-reduction and ramification set; 373 is only usable when it avoids that set.

### Examples

- For `K = ℚ` and `g = 2`, `q = 107` is admissible: `106 = 2·53`, no prime `3, 5, 7` divides `106`, and
  `8·2³/106² = 64/11236 < 1/160 = 1/(d_q + 1)` with `d_q = 159`. A friendly place needs `q_v` of order at least
  `8` modulo `53`; `p ≡ 2 mod 53` works since `2` has order `52` modulo `53`, and `p = 373` is such a prime
  (it must also avoid the finite exceptional set). For `N = 106` the fixed-space bound is
  `|ker(T^i − 1)| ≤ 4·106²` for `1 ≤ i ≤ 7`.

### Dependencies

Layers LV.0, LV.1, LV.2, LV.3, LV.7, LV.8, LV.10; TauCetiRoadmap LocalFieldsRamification Layer 2; TauCetiRoadmap Chebotarev Layer 10; EtaleDualityAndPerverseSheaves §§0.5–0.7; InverseGaloisAndArithmeticFundamentalGroups §§IG.1.2–IG.1.3; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.0.A1–F.0.A2; EtaleCohomologyFoundations §§F.2.1–F.2.2, `F`.4; ComplexComparisonPartII §C4.5; Mathlib and Tau Ceti as listed under each target.

## Worked examples

- **The smallest affine example.** For `q = 3`, `Aff(3) = Sym(𝔽₃)`. For a curve of genus two there are `810`
  surjections from the free group of rank four onto `Aff(3)` that are nontrivial on the product of commutators, and
  `810/6 = 135` classes modulo conjugation; the corresponding degree-three covers have genus `5`, primitive rational
  homology of dimension `6` and reduced Prym of dimension `3`. The monodromy theorem then concerns `135` factors `Sp₆`. This
  checks at once the conjugacy quotient, the Riemann–Hurwitz count `g(C′) = qg − (q − 1)/2` and the distinction between the
  degree-`q` quotient and the degree-`q(q − 1)` Galois cover.
- **Transfer.** For the trivial disconnected double cover of a surface, primitive homology is `{(x, −x)}` with twice the
  base intersection form; a connected cyclic cover of degree three has monodromy `C₃` and is not an `Aff(3)`-cover.
- **Period varieties.** For `E = k × k` and `V = E²` with the standard form, the period variety is `ℙ¹ × ℙ¹`, and the
  `E`-stable subspace `k² × 0` is excluded from the componentwise-rank-one Grassmannian although its total dimension is
  right. A symplectic plane gives `ℙ¹` and the zero space `Spec k`.
- **Short orbits.** `A` `7`-cycle contributes fully to `size_v`, an `8`-cycle not at all, and a fixed point together with an
  `8`-cycle gives `size_v = 1/9`.
- **The auxiliary choices for `K = ℚ`, `g = 2`.** `q = 107` is admissible (`106 = 2·53`, `d_q = 159`,
  `64/106² < 1/160`); a prime `p ≡ 2 mod 53` has order `52` modulo `53`, and `p = 373` is one, to be used only if it
  also avoids the finite exceptional set. For `N = 106` the fixed-space bound is `|ker(T^i − 1)| ≤ 4·106²`.
- **Hodge weights.** `ℚ_p(1)` has `t_H = −1`; jumps `−1` and `+1` together give `t_H = 0`. At `K = ℚ(i)` the prime `3` is
  friendly, the primes above `5` are not, and `2` is ramified. The reciprocity identities of Layer LV.1 hold on an open
  subgroup of units, with `χ_cyc(Art_F(u)) = N(u)^{−1}`.

## Downstream consumers

- **HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions**, §§5.12, 6.4, owns the declaration of Faltings's theorem; 11.6 is one of its
  proofs, independent of the Parshin–Shafarevich and Vojta–Bombieri routes of that roadmap.
- **DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence**, §2.4, owns the unit equation; 6.10 proves its two-term case over
  number fields without the subspace theorem.
- **MetaplecticAutomorphicForms**, §0.9a (Lagrangian complements and adapted bases), uses the same adapted-basis linear algebra as §3.2.
- The method of Layers LV.2–LV.4 (Gauss–Manin transport on residue disks, period maps and the centralizer bound) is the
  entry point for the higher-dimensional applications of Lawrence–Venkatesh and its successors, which are not part of
  this roadmap.
